Certification and Classification of Linear Quantum Error Mitigation Methods
Zach Blunden-Codd, Mohamed Tamaazousti

Abstract
Numerous mitigation methods exist for quantum noise suppression, making it challenging to identify the optimum approach for a specific application – especially as ongoing advances in hardware tuning and error correction are expected to reduce logical error rates. In order to facilitate the ‘future proof’ application-dependent comparison of mitigation methods, we develop a set of quantitative metrics that account for continual improvements in logical gate quality. We use these metrics to define qualitative criteria (e.g. scalability, efficiency, and robustness to characterised imperfections in the mitigation implementation), which we combine into application-specific certifications. We then provide a taxonomy of linear mitigation methods, characterising them by their features and requirements. Finally, we use our framework to produce and evaluate a mitigation strategy. A mitigation strategy is a collections of mitigation methods and compilation procedures designed to mitigate all relevant errors for a given piece of characterised hardware. Our example mitigation strategy is targeted at mitigating the outputs of hardware suffering from stochastic noise and/or rotational errors. We find the most significant determinant of efficient mitigation is accurate and precise characterisation.



Contents

1
Introduction

In this section we introduce and motivate our study (Sec.
1.1
), highlight our contributions (Sec.
1.2
), discuss the assumptions inherent to our study (Sec.
1.3
), and provide an overview of the remainder of this document (Sec.
1.4
). This document also has a companion piece: Ref.
[
1
]
, which follows the same basic layout but has more extensive cross-referenced appendices that contain all the derivations required for this work. In App.
B
we provide a complete worked example for the mitigation of uniform dephasing errors, to aid comprehension.

1.1
Context and Motivation

Quantum devices inherently suffer from noise
[
2
]
. While unavoidable at present, one effective way to reduce its impact on computations is to employ error mitigation techniques (see
[
3
,
4
]
). Error mitigation permits one to increase the accuracy of quantum calculations at the cost of a longer runtime (and/or increased numbers of qubits).

There are a great many mitigation methods available, all with different parameters, assumptions, requirements, trade-offs, etc. Our aim is to provide a more comprehensive framework to facilitate the selection of mitigation methods for particular applications implemented on well-characterised hardware. We build on the existing literature to simplify the assessment of mitigation methods.

In Ref.
[
5
]
the authors discuss a trade-off between the sampling cost (due to the increased variance of mitigated circuits) and the increase in the runtime of an individual circuit (due to additional gates being used to effect the mitigation) for methods using identity insertion. We combine both these factors and the increases in the number of qubits required for the mitigation into a single metric: the runtime scaling, which measures the effective increase in total runtime due to the mitigation.

In Ref.
[
6
]
a lower bound is provided for the sampling cost of a variety of classes of mitigation methods as a function of the maximum bias for any observable measurement. We provide a strategy to calculate an upper bound for the post-mitigation bias without requiring quantum measurements of the observable, or simulation of the circuit.

In Ref.
[
7
]
they provide order of magnitude estimates for the sampling costs and fidelities of a variety of classes of mitigation methods. We provide finer grained measures of bias and sampling cost to permit the comparison of mitigation methods of the same class, such as different variants of zero-noise extrapolation or probabilistic error cancellation.

Error mitigation is only one of three main types of noise suppression:

1.

Hardware Tuning

[
8
]
: Improving the quality of physical gates and qubits through improved manufacturing techniques, pulse shaping, lowering temperatures, feedback control, etc.

2.

Error Correction

[
9
]
: Improving the quality of logical gates and qubits (and therefore outputs) for each run of the circuit through redundancy, encoding, real time intervention, etc.

3.

Error Mitigation

[
3
,
4
]
: Improving the quality of observable expectation values on average for an ensemble of runs through the use of symmetry verification, effective quasi-stochastic superpositions of gates, etc.

Although we focus on error mitigation, our framework is designed to be generalisable. It accounts for future reductions in the gate-wise noise amplitude of quantum circuits, due to hardware tuning and/or error correction, by giving results in terms of the overall noise level. The cost of error mitigation has been shown to grow exponentially with the number of gates (for stochastic noise with fixed gate-wise error probability, see Ref.
[
6
]
). This means that the only way to mitigate larger circuits efficiently is to reduce the gate-wise error probability, which means we can have the same noise level with a greater number of gates. The error probability could be reduced using error correction but in this case we should mitigate the logical qubits rather than physical qubits, in order to profit from the noise reduction.

It should be straightforward to extend this framework to incorporate some aspects of error correction, such as: increased numbers of qubits and increased execution time. In order to generalise the framework further, to incorporate the costs associated with hardware tuning, and the classical computing resources required for error correction, we might have to use energy or resource consumption as our metric of choice to characterise the cost of noise suppression (akin to Ref.
[
10
]
).

1.2
Contributions

1.

Taxonomy (Sec.
4
):
We introduce a taxonomy dedicated to linear mitigation methods for quantum noise suppression, categorising them according to key features such as the use of hidden inverses, custom noise amplification channels, and identity insertion techniques. This taxonomy enables a systematic assessment of the utility and limitations of individual mitigation features, and provides a foundation for generalising and optimising existing methods.

2.

Certification (Sec.
2.3
):
There are myriad mitigation methods in the literature and it can be difficult to determine the most appropriate method for a given application. We provide a selection of novel (proxy bias, noise boundary, runtime scaling, and area scale factor) and existing (sampling cost and length scale factor) quantitative metrics for the circuit-independent evaluation of linear mitigation methods (i.e. these metrics do not require numerical simulation of circuits or their implementation on a quantum computer). We then use these metrics to determine the compliance of a method with a set of qualitative criteria (robust, scalable, efficient, precise, etc. ) that can be used to certify the method for a particular task.

3.

Case Studies:
We carry out the certification procedure for a selection of mitigation methods (Sec.
5
) for stochastic noise (Sec.
3.3
) and rotational errors (Sec.
3.4
). We also introduce the idea of a mitigation strategy (combination of mitigation method, gate set, and compilation scheme; see Sec.
2.5.2
) and certify it to be USEful (Sec.
2.5.2
) if it is a scalable strategy that is efficacious for the production of the mitigated circuit for any quantum algorithm. Finally, we study the USEM:ORE strategy (Sec.
6.1
) for the universal and scalable mitigation of hardware suffering from (stochastic) over-rotation errors (Sec.
3.5
) and demonstrate that it complies with the USEful certification.

1.3
Assumptions

•

Linear Methods:
Our discussion is limited to linear mitigation methods (Sec.
2.1
) since they are particularly amenable to circuit-independent analysis
1
1

1

In this work an analysis is circuit independent if it depends only on coarse properties of the circuit such as the size or number of gates and not on the precise nature of the algorithm/gates being implemented.
. There are many non-linear methods (such as virtual distillation
[
11
,
12
,
13
]
and symmetry verification
[
7
]
) that would also be worthy of study but are beyond the scope of this paper.

•

Noise Models:
Though we provide some relatively general analyses (see Sec.
2.3
), our main focus is on stochastic noise (Sec.
3.3
) and rotational errors (Sec.
3.4
). So our conclusions should not be applied to other noise channels without further verification. We assume perfect characterisation of our noise models but in practice there will always be limitations to the precision of characterisation, which will affect the final bias.

•

Large Circuit Limit:
One of our core focuses is scalability. So a number of our criteria are only appropriate in the asymptotic regime of large circuits (Sec.
2.4.3
). Care should be taken when applying our analysis to shorter circuits.

•

Small Noise Levels:
Our analysis of bias assumes the small noise level regime (Sec.
2.4.2
). Additional analysis would be required to generalise to larger noise levels.

•

Pauli Observables:
We assume that our measured observables are single qubit Pauli operators, this assumption can be imposed without loss of generality, but would require extra gates and post-processing for the measurement of arbitrary observables.

•

Gate Errors:
We focus on noise related to the application of gates but one could also apply some of these methods to SPAM (state preparation and measurement) errors, if one assigned the effective noise channels to virtual identity gates.

•

Uniform Noise Models:
For simplicity we assume that our noisy circuits have uniform noise. That is, every gate suffers from the same noise (see Sec.
2.2.2
). However, our analysis can be generalised to non-uniform noise models (e.g. see Sec.
4.4.2
).

1.4
Overview

The structure of this paper is the following:

1.

Certification Scheme (Sec.
2
):
We provide quantitative metrics for the evaluation of mitigation methods: the proxy bias, sampling cost, area scale factor, runtime scaling, and noise boundary. Then we use these metrics to define qualitative criteria for certifying mitigation methods (Sec.
2.5.1
) and strategies (Sec.
2.5.2
).

2.

Characterisation of noise models (Sec.
3
):
We ‘characterise’ the three types of noise we analyse in this work: stochastic noise (Sec.
3.3
), rotational errors (Sec.
3.4
), and (stochastic) over-rotation errors (Sec.
3.5
).

3.

Taxonomical Classification Scheme (Sec.
4
):
We classify the resources we have available to modify error channels: customised channels (Sec.
4.2.3
), identity insertions (Sec.
4.2.4
), hidden inverses (Sec.
4.2.2
); the scope of noise amplification (Sec.
4.1
): synchronous, asynchronous, and local; and the type of pre-tailoring we apply: none or local cancellation (Sec.
4.3
). Then we produce a taxonomy of mitigation methods derived from various combinations of these aspects (Fig.
1
).

4.

Case Study for Methods (Sec.
5
):
We consider the mitigation methods in our taxonomy (Table
1
) and evaluate them with respect to the metrics and certification criteria developed in Sec.
2
for stochastic noise (Sec.
3.3
) and rotational errors (Sec.
3.4
).

5.

Case Study for Strategy (Sec.
6
):
We use the results of Sec.
5
to design USEM:ORE, a mitigation strategy for hardware suffering from (stochastic) over-rotation errors and certify that this strategy is USEful (see Sec.
2.5.2
).

6.

Conclusions:
We conclude by summarising our contributions and discussing possible applications of our work (see Sec.
7
).

2
Certification Scheme

Here we will work through the quantitative metrics (Sec.
2.3
) and qualitative criteria (Sec.
2.5
) that we develop for the certification of mitigation methods. But first, we shall briefly discuss linear mitigation methods and their advantages.

2.1
Linear Mitigation

The purpose of quantum error mitigation is to suppress the bias (
\varepsilon_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{O}}
) of an observable (
\hat{O}
) estimate generated using a quantum computer. We define the bias by:

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{O}}=

\displaystyle\left|\left\langle\tilde{O}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{M}}-\left\langle\hat{O}\right\rangle\right|,

(1)

where
\left\langle\hat{O}\right\rangle
is the target observable expectation in the absence of noise and
\left\langle\tilde{O}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{M}}
is the mitigated estimate.

Linear
2
2

2

An example of a non-linear mitigation method is virtual distillation (see Refs
[
11
,
12
,
13
]
), where the effective state after mitigation is the original noisy state raised to some integer power and is not a linear combination of physical states, in general.
mitigation is a way of mitigating an observable using a linear combination of (noisy) observable estimates from noisy circuits:

\displaystyle\left\langle\tilde{O}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{M}}=\sum_{i=0}^{N_{\mathrm{C}}}c_{\mathrm{Gl};i}\left\langle\tilde{O}\right\rangle_{i}\approx\left\langle\hat{O}\right\rangle,

(2)

where
c_{\mathrm{Gl};i}
is the (global) mitigation coefficient for the
i
th observable;
\left\langle\tilde{O}\right\rangle_{i}
is the
i
th noisy observable estimate, e.g. the result of the measurement of observable
\hat{O}
using the
i
th noisy circuit variant (
\tilde{\mathcal{C}}_{{\mathrm{}};{\mathrm{}i}}
);
N_{\mathrm{C}}
is the number of circuits used for the mitigation; and
\left\langle\hat{O}\right\rangle
is the noise-free observable expectation. We can also define our mitigation in terms of noisy states (
\tilde{\rho}_{i}
) or noisy circuit variants (
\tilde{\mathcal{C}}_{{\mathrm{}};{\mathrm{}i}}
):

\displaystyle\tilde{\rho}_{\mathrm{M}}=

\displaystyle\sum_{i=0}^{N_{\mathrm{C}}}c_{\mathrm{Gl};i}\tilde{\rho}_{i}\approx\rho,

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathcal{C}}_{{\mathrm{}};{\mathrm{M}}}=

\displaystyle\sum_{i=0}^{N_{\mathrm{C}}}c_{\mathrm{Gl};i}\tilde{\mathcal{C}}_{{\mathrm{}};{\mathrm{}i}}\approx\mathcal{C}_{{\mathrm{}}{\mathrm{}}},

(3)

where we use the same coefficients (
c_{\mathrm{Gl};i}
) for observables (Eq. (
2
)), states, and circuits. Here
\tilde{\rho}_{\mathrm{M}}
is the mitigated state,
\tilde{\rho}_{i}
is the noisy states output by the
i
th noisy circuit, and
\rho
is the (target) noise-free state.
\tilde{\mathcal{C}}_{{\mathrm{}};{\mathrm{M}}}
is the mitigated circuit,
\tilde{\mathcal{C}}_{{\mathrm{}};{\mathrm{}i}}
is the (noisy) channel of the
i
th circuit, and
\mathcal{C}_{{\mathrm{}}{\mathrm{}}}
is the (noise-free) channel corresponding to the circuit we wish to implement. To move between the representations we use:

\displaystyle\left\langle\tilde{O}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{M}}=

\displaystyle\mathrm{Tr}\left[\hat{O}\tilde{\rho}_{\mathrm{M}}\right]=\mathrm{Tr}\left[\hat{O}\tilde{\mathcal{C}}_{{\mathrm{}};{\mathrm{M}}}\left(\rho_{0}\right)\right],

\displaystyle\left\langle\tilde{O}\right\rangle_{i}=

\displaystyle\mathrm{Tr}\left[\hat{O}\tilde{\rho}_{i}\right]=\mathrm{Tr}\left[\hat{O}\tilde{\mathcal{C}}_{{\mathrm{}};{\mathrm{}i}}\left(\rho_{0}\right)\right],

(4)

where (
\rho_{0}
) is the initial state input into the circuit. Some common examples of linear mitigation are probabilistic error cancellation
[
14
]
, subspace expansion
[
15
]
, and Richardson extrapolation
[
16
]
.

Linear mitigation methods are interesting because it is possible to develop analytic bounds for their bias and costs (see Sec.
2.3
). An additional advantage of linear mitigation methods is that they can be composed (see Secs
4.4.2
and
4.5.2
). So we can combine multiple mitigation methods in order to mitigate different sections of our circuit or different types of noise. In the next section we will discuss how we can model the effect of noise in a circuit-independent way (Sec.
2.2
) and then use these models to analyse mitigation methods (Sec.
2.3
).

2.2
Circuit-Independent Noise Modelling

Here we model the effect of noise in a circuit-independent way. The idea is to find a model for error channels that denotes them as linear combinations of (c-number) explicit functions of some noise amplitude (
x
) and (q-number) quantum channels, which are independent of the noise amplitude. This allows one to model the effect of multiple noise channels within a circuit without requiring precise knowledge of the circuit itself or the precise nature of the quantum channels within our error channels.

2.2.1
Channels

In this work we focus on noise associated to gates so we model each noisy gate (e.g.
\tilde{U}_{{\mathrm{}}{\mathrm{}}}
) as a noise-free gate (e.g.
U_{{\mathrm{}}{\mathrm{}}}
) followed
3
3

3

The analysis is analogous when the noise channel occurs before the noise-free gate so we will not explicitly consider that case in this work.
by an error channel (
\mathcal{E}_{{\mathrm{}}{\mathrm{}}}
). Each error channel has a noise amplitude
4
4

4

In this work we mainly focus on noise models with a single noise parameter but the analysis can be readily generalised to multi-noise parameter noise models.
(
x
) where the larger the noise parameter the large the effect of the noise (in general
5
5

5

When we have coherent errors sometimes larger noise amplitudes can result in smaller errors due to periodicity in the noise parameter but we will focus on the small noise amplitude regime,
x\ll 1
, in this work so that shouldn’t be an issue.
). We separate the full error channel (
\mathcal{E}_{{\mathrm{}}{\mathrm{}}}
) into
N_{\mathrm{N}}
sub-components, each with a scalar valued coefficient (
f_{k}\left(x\right)
) that depends on the noise amplitude and a ‘channel-valued’ term (
\mathcal{N}_{{\mathrm{}k}{\mathrm{}}}
) that is independent of the amplitude (
x
):

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{{\mathrm{}}{\mathrm{}}}=\sum_{k=0}^{N_{\mathrm{N}}}f_{{\mathrm{}k}{\mathrm{}}}\!\left(x\right)\mathcal{N}_{{\mathrm{}k}{\mathrm{}}},

(5)

where we assume:

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{{\mathrm{}}{\mathrm{}}}\underset{x\rightarrow 0}{\rightarrow}\mathcal{I}_{{\mathrm{}}{\mathrm{}}},

(6)

where
\mathcal{I}_{{\mathrm{}}{\mathrm{}}}
is the identity channel, which has no effect on the quantum state. For the rest of this work we also assume that:

\displaystyle f_{{\mathrm{}k}{\mathrm{}}}\!\left(0\right)=

\displaystyle\delta_{0k},

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}_{{\mathrm{}0}{\mathrm{}}}=

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}_{{\mathrm{}}{\mathrm{}}},

(7)

where
\delta_{0k}
is the Kronecker delta (so zero unless
k=0
, when it is unity) and that
\mathcal{N}_{{\mathrm{}k}{\mathrm{}}}
is a completely-positive and trace-preserving (CPTP) channel.

To illustrate this notation we consider the example of a local dephasing (DPh) noise channel:

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{{\mathrm{DPh}}{\mathrm{}}}\!\left(\rho\right)=\left(1-p\right)\rho+p\mathrm{Z}\rho\mathrm{Z},

(8)

where
\rho
is the density operator representing a quantum state,
p
is the noise probability, and
\mathrm{Z}
is the Pauli Z operator. For this channel we can define:

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{{\mathrm{DPh}}{\mathrm{}}}=

\displaystyle f_{{\mathrm{}0}{\mathrm{}}}\!\left(x\right)\mathcal{N}_{{\mathrm{}0}{\mathrm{}}}+f_{{\mathrm{}1}{\mathrm{}}}\!\left(x\right)\mathcal{N}_{{\mathrm{}1}{\mathrm{}}},

\displaystyle x=

\displaystyle p,

\displaystyle f_{{\mathrm{}0}{\mathrm{}}}\!\left(x\right)=

\displaystyle 1-x,

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}_{{\mathrm{}0}{\mathrm{}}}=

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}_{{\mathrm{}}{\mathrm{}}},

(9)

\displaystyle N_{\mathrm{N}}=

\displaystyle 2,

\displaystyle f_{{\mathrm{}1}{\mathrm{}}}\!\left(x\right)=

\displaystyle x,

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}_{{\mathrm{}1}{\mathrm{}}}\!\left(\rho\right)=

\displaystyle\mathrm{Z}\rho\mathrm{Z},

(10)

which satisfies our requirements since
f_{{\mathrm{}0}{\mathrm{}}}\!\left(0\right)=1
,
f_{{\mathrm{}1}{\mathrm{}}}\!\left(0\right)=0
, and
\mathcal{N}_{{\mathrm{}0}{\mathrm{}}}
and
\mathcal{N}_{{\mathrm{}1}{\mathrm{}}}
are CPTP channels.

2.2.2
Circuits

We assume a uniform noise model, meaning each error channel in the circuit shares the same type and noise amplitude. This means all error channels in the circuit have the same functional form for the coefficients (
f_{k}
); and the same noise amplitude (
x
); but they could have different associated channels (
\mathcal{N}_{{\mathrm{}k}{\mathrm{}}}
). The uniformity assumption is limiting, but we can account for non-uniform noise models by sequentially applying this procedure for every error channel that appears in the circuit. We assume there are
N_{\mathrm{G}}
(uniform) noisy gates in the circuit and therefore
N_{\mathrm{G}}
error channels. Between the noisy gates we permit any number of noise-free gates and we place no restriction on the form of the noise-free components of the noisy gates. The result is that our noisy circuit (
\tilde{\mathcal{C}}_{{\mathrm{}}{\mathrm{}}}
) can be expressed by the channel:

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathcal{C}}_{{\mathrm{}}{\mathrm{}}}=

\displaystyle\sum_{\left\{l_{k}\right\}:\sum_{j=0}^{N_{\mathrm{N}}}l_{j}=N_{\mathrm{G}}}g_{\scriptscriptstyle{\left\{l_{k}\right\}}}\!\left(x\right)\breve{\chi}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\left\{l_{k}\right\}}},

\displaystyle\text{ where }g_{\scriptscriptstyle{\left\{l_{k}\right\}}}\!\left(x\right)=

\displaystyle\left(\prod_{k=0}^{N_{\mathrm{N}}}\left(f_{k}\left(x\right)\right)^{l_{k}}\right),

(11)

but this expression needs a bit of unpacking. We use the short-hand notation:

\displaystyle\left\{l_{k}\right\}=

\displaystyle\left\{l_{k}\right\}_{k=0}^{N_{\mathrm{N}}}=l_{0},l_{1},\dots,l_{N_{\mathrm{N}}}.

(12)

The sum includes every list of
l_{k}
’s (
\left\{l_{k}\right\}
where
0\leq l_{k}\leq N_{\mathrm{G}}
) for which the sum of the list elements (
l_{k}
’s) is equal to the number of noise channels in the circuit (
N_{\mathrm{G}}
):

\displaystyle\sum_{j=0}^{N_{\mathrm{N}}}l_{j}=N_{\mathrm{G}}.

(13)

\breve{\chi}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\left\{l_{k}\right\}}}
is a quasi-channel
6
6

6

Quasi-channels are linear sums of completely-positive and trace-preserving (CPTP) channels but may not be CPTP themselves.
\breve{\chi}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\left\{l_{k}\right\}}}
is a special type of quasi-channel where all the linear coefficients are equal to unity.
and is the linear combination of all the channels that can be obtained by replacing
l_{k}
of the error channels in the circuit with
\mathcal{N}_{{\mathrm{}k}{\mathrm{}}}
for each
k
(
0\leq k\leq N_{\mathrm{N}}
).
\breve{\chi}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\left\{l_{k}\right\}}}
is independent of the noise amplitude (
x
) since all noise amplitude dependence is encoded in
g_{\scriptscriptstyle{\left\{l_{k}\right\}}}\!\left(x\right)
.

As an example we will consider the case of a circuit with
N_{\mathrm{G}}
gates, each of which are followed by a local dephasing noise channel (
\mathcal{E}_{{\mathrm{DPh}}{\mathrm{}}}
), so the noisy circuit is given by:

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathcal{C}}_{{\mathrm{}}{\mathrm{}}}=

\displaystyle\bigcirc_{i=1}^{N_{\mathrm{G}}}\left(\mathcal{E}_{{\mathrm{DPh}}{\mathrm{}}}\circ\mathcal{U}_{{\mathrm{}i}{\mathrm{}}}\right)=\sum_{l_{0},l_{1}:l_{0}+l_{1}=N_{\mathrm{G}}}g_{\scriptscriptstyle{l_{0},l_{1}}}\!\left(x\right)\breve{\chi}_{\scriptscriptstyle{l_{0},l_{1}}},

(14)

where:

\displaystyle\mathcal{U}_{{\mathrm{}i}{\mathrm{}}}\!\left(\rho\right)=

\displaystyle U_{{\mathrm{}i}{\mathrm{}}}\rho{U}^{\dagger}_{{\mathrm{}i}{\mathrm{}}},

(15)

is the quantum channel associated with the quantum gate
U_{{\mathrm{}i}{\mathrm{}}}
, and (see Eqs (
9
) and (
11
)):

\displaystyle g_{\scriptscriptstyle{l_{0},N_{\mathrm{G}}-l_{0}}}=

\displaystyle\left(1-x\right)^{l_{0}}x^{N_{\mathrm{G}}-l_{0}},

\displaystyle\breve{\chi}_{\scriptscriptstyle{l_{0},N_{\mathrm{G}}-l_{0}}}=

\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{\frac{N_{\mathrm{G}}!}{l_{0}!\left(N_{\mathrm{G}}-l_{0}\right)!}}\bigcirc_{i=1}^{N_{\mathrm{G}}}\left(\mathcal{N}_{{\mathrm{}S_{{\mathrm{}i};{\mathrm{}l_{0},j}}}{\mathrm{}}}\circ\mathcal{U}_{{\mathrm{}i}{\mathrm{}}}\right),

(16)

where
S_{{\mathrm{}i};{\mathrm{}l_{0},j}}
is either zero or one, i.e.
\mathcal{N}_{{\mathrm{}S_{{\mathrm{}i};{\mathrm{}l_{0},j}}}{\mathrm{}}}
is either
\mathcal{N}_{{\mathrm{}0}{\mathrm{}}}
or
\mathcal{N}_{{\mathrm{}1}{\mathrm{}}}
. More concretely
S_{{\mathrm{}i};{\mathrm{}l_{0},j}}
is the
i
th element of the
j
th permutation of a set of
l_{0}
zeros and
l_{1}=N_{\mathrm{G}}-l_{0}
ones, we sum over all possible distinct permutations. Since
l_{1}=N_{\mathrm{G}}-l_{0}
these are the only terms that need to be considered.

Fortunately, we do not need to understand the structure of
\breve{\chi}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\left\{l_{k}\right\}}}
since it is independent of the noise amplitude (
x
). For the following analysis all we need to know is that a decomposition of the form in Eq. (
11
) exists and the definition of
g_{\scriptscriptstyle{\left\{l_{k}\right\}}}\!\left(x\right)
. The nature of
\breve{\chi}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\left\{l_{k}\right\}}}
is irrelevant for our calculations as long as it is independent of the noise amplitude and we know its multiplicity (how many CPTP channels it comprises, see Eq. (
17
)).

2.3
Quantitative Mitigation Method Metrics

Mitigation suppresses the bias (Eq. (
1
)) at the cost of an increase in the variance, thus we require longer runtimes to obtain mitigated results to the same precision as unmitigated results. In the following sections, we derive a novel, circuit-independent, upper bound for the bias – termed the proxy bias (Secs
2.3.1
and
2.3.3
) – as well as a new characterisation of runtime scaling, which quantifies the relative increase in runtime resulting from mitigation (Sec.
2.3.4
). We combine the two to determine the noise boundary (Sec.
2.3.5
), a new measure of the maximum noise level (see Eq. (
24
)) that can be mitigated for a given target bias and tolerated runtime scaling. We then use these metrics to help us define unambiguous qualitative criteria for certification (Sec.
2.5
).

2.3.1
Proxy Bias

In order to determine the true bias (Eq. (
1
)) we would have to implement or simulate the full quantum circuit both with and without noise. This is not feasible for (novel) large-scale circuits, where no ground truth is available. As an alternative we provide the proxy bias (Eq. (
2.3.1
)), which is an upper bound for the true bias that can be determined without any explicit simulation, once we have a model such as that derived in Sec.
2.2.2
. This facilitates the comparison of mitigation methods.

It is useful to define the multiplicity
7
7

7

In general the multiplicity of a quasi-channel is the sum of the magnitudes of the linear coefficients pre-multiplying the CPTP channels that are summed together to give the quasi-channel. Since all the linear coefficients of
\breve{\chi}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\left\{l_{k}\right\}}}
are unity the multiplicity
\breve{\chi}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\left\{l_{k}\right\}}}
is just the number of constituent channels.
of (i.e. the number of CPTP channels that make up) the quasi-channel
\breve{\chi}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\left\{l_{k}\right\}}}
:

\displaystyle\#\left[\breve{\chi}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\left\{l_{k}\right\}}}\right]=\frac{N_{\mathrm{G}}!}{\prod_{j=0}^{N_{\mathrm{N}}}\left(l_{j}!\right)}.

(17)

This means any physical (i.e. properly normalised) state (
\rho_{0}
) acted on by the quasi-channel
\breve{\chi}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\left\{l_{k}\right\}}}
will have an expectation value that satisfies:

\displaystyle 0\leq\left|\mathrm{Tr}\left[\hat{O}\breve{\chi}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\left\{l_{k}\right\}}}\left(\rho_{0}\right)\right]\right|\leq\#\left[\breve{\chi}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\left\{l_{k}\right\}}}\right],

(18)

if
\hat{O}\in\left(\mathrm{I},\mathrm{X},\mathrm{Y},\mathrm{Z}\right)^{{N_{\mathrm{Q}}}}
is a Pauli word operator and
{N_{\mathrm{Q}}}
is the number of qubits. We have this upper bound because we know that the maximum expectation magnitude for any Pauli observable with respect to a physical state is unity and CPTP channels (e.g.
\mathcal{N}_{{\mathrm{}k}{\mathrm{}}}
) acting on a physical state return physical states. Since there are
\#\left[\breve{\chi}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\left\{l_{k}\right\}}}\right]
physical channels that make up
\breve{\chi}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\left\{l_{k}\right\}}}
that is the maximum possible observable expectation after passing through the quasi-channel:
\breve{\chi}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\left\{l_{k}\right\}}}
.

So the noisy state will have a bias given by (Eqs (
1
), (
4
), and (
11
)):

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{O}}=

\displaystyle\left|\mathrm{Tr}\left[\hat{O}\rho\right]-\mathrm{Tr}\left[\hat{O}\tilde{\mathcal{C}}_{{\mathrm{}}{\mathrm{}}}\left(\rho_{0}\right)\right]\right|

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left|\left(1-\left(f_{0}\left(x\right)\right)^{N_{\mathrm{G}}}\right)\mathrm{Tr}\left[\hat{O}\rho\right]-\sum_{\left\{l_{k}\right\}:\sum_{j=0}^{N_{\mathrm{N}}}l_{j}=N_{\mathrm{G}};l_{0}\neq N_{\mathrm{G}}}g_{\scriptscriptstyle{\left\{l_{k}\right\}}}\!\left(x\right)\mathrm{Tr}\left[\hat{O}\breve{\chi}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\left\{l_{k}\right\}}}\left(\rho_{0}\right)\right]\right|\leq\varepsilon_{\scriptscriptstyle\heartsuit},

(19)

where we have separated out the noise-free component (
\rho
), i.e. that corresponding to the case where we act with the identity component (
\mathcal{N}_{{\mathrm{}0}{\mathrm{}}}
) of every noise channel (see Eq. (
7
)). In this case
l_{0}=N_{\mathrm{G}}
so (see Eq. (
12
)):

\displaystyle\left\{l_{k}\right\}=

\displaystyle N_{\mathrm{G}},0,\dots,0,

(20)

and
\breve{\chi}_{\scriptscriptstyle{N_{\mathrm{G}},0,\dots,0}}\left(\rho_{0}\right)=\rho
is the noise-free state.
\hat{O}
is any Pauli operator, and we define:

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{\scriptscriptstyle\heartsuit}=

\displaystyle\left|1-\left(f_{0}\left(x\right)\right)^{N_{\mathrm{G}}}\right|+\sum_{\left\{l_{k}\right\}_{k}:\sum_{k=0}^{N_{\mathrm{N}}}l_{k}=N_{\mathrm{G}};l_{0}\neq N_{\mathrm{G}}}\left|g_{\scriptscriptstyle{\left\{l_{k}\right\}}}\!\left(x\right)\right|\#\left[\breve{\chi}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\left\{l_{k}\right\}}}\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left|1-\left(f_{0}\left(x\right)\right)^{N_{\mathrm{G}}}\right|+\left(\sum_{k=0}^{N_{\mathrm{N}}}\left|f_{k}\left(x\right)\right|\right)^{N_{\mathrm{G}}}-\left|f_{0}\left(x\right)\right|^{N_{\mathrm{G}}},

(21)

as the (unmitigated) proxy bias.

If we consider our local dephasing noise example (see Eqs (
9
) and (
14
)) we find the proxy bias is given by (see Eqs (
9
) and (
2.3.1
)):

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{\scriptscriptstyle\heartsuit}=

\displaystyle 2\left(1-\left(1-x\right)^{N_{\mathrm{G}}}\right),

(22)

since
0\leq x\leq 1
. This is upper bounded by 2 (which occurs in the limit
N_{\mathrm{G}}\rightarrow\infty
or
x=1
). This bound is tight since it is indeed the maximum possible error for a Pauli expectation, which must be in the range
-1
to
1
(inclusive). However, in general we do not expect this method to yield a tight upper bound on the bias. However, by choosing the decomposition components carefully one can make the bound tighter.

2.3.2
Noise Level

The noise level (
e_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{G}}
) is the leading order term of the (unmitigated) proxy bias (Eq. (
2.3.1
)) in the small noise regime (
N_{\mathrm{G}}\left|x\right|\ll 1
). So if we define:

\displaystyle f_{k}\left(x\right)=

\displaystyle f_{k}\left(0\right)+f^{\prime}_{k}\left(x\right)+\mathcal{O}_{\mathrm{S}}\left(x^{2}\right),

\displaystyle f_{0}\left(0\right)=

\displaystyle 1,

\displaystyle f_{k>0}\left(0\right)=

\displaystyle 0,

\displaystyle f^{\prime}_{k}\left(x\right)=

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{\mathrm{S}}\left(x\right),

(23)

where
\mathcal{O}_{\mathrm{S}}\left(x\right)
is of linear order in
x
(see Sec.
2.4.2
), then the noise level is given by:

\displaystyle e_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{G}}=N_{\mathrm{G}}\sum_{k=0}^{N_{\mathrm{N}}}\left|f^{\prime}_{k}\left(x\right)\right|.

(24)

We find this to be a useful parameter to quantify the strength of the noise and it is proportional to the noise level defined in Ref.
[
7
]
for stochastic noise (see Sec.
3.3
). It is independent of the precise structure of the circuit, only depending on the number of noise channels the circuit contains. If we have multiple different types of error channels then the noise level of the full circuit is just the sum of the noise levels due to the constituent channels. For lower noise levels we expect smaller biases, for the unmitigated circuits, but owing to the complex interaction between gates and noise channels this will not always hold in practice. The noise level is always an upper bound for the bias but is not guaranteed to be tight, one should choose the channel decomposition (see Eq. (
5
)) that results in the smallest noise level to achieve the tightest bound.

If we consider our local dephasing noise example (see Eqs (
9
) and (
14
)) we find the noise level is given by (see Eq. (
22
)):

\displaystyle e_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{G}}=

\displaystyle 2N_{\mathrm{G}}x,

(25)

since (Eq. (
9
)):

\displaystyle f_{0}\left(0\right)=

\displaystyle 1,

\displaystyle f^{\prime}_{0}\left(x\right)=

\displaystyle-x,

\displaystyle f_{1}\left(0\right)=

\displaystyle 0,

\displaystyle f^{\prime}_{1}\left(x\right)=

\displaystyle x.

(26)

2.3.3
Proxy Bias after Mitigation

The proxy bias after mitigation is simply the proxy bias (see Sec.
2.3.1
) of the mitigated channel, which serves as an upper bound for the bias of the mitigated state (for a Pauli observable). So the lower the proxy bias the better the mitigation, in general (the true bias after mitigation will depend on the specific structure of the circuit and could be significantly less than the proxy bias). In this work we consider linear mitigation and assume that all the noisy circuits used for our mitigation have the same noise model but different noise amplitudes. In this case the mitigated circuit has the channel (Eqs (
3
) and (
11
)):

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathcal{C}}_{{\mathrm{}};{\mathrm{M}}}=

\displaystyle\sum_{i=0}^{N_{\mathrm{C}}}c_{\mathrm{Gl};i}\tilde{\mathcal{C}}_{{\mathrm{}};{\mathrm{}i}}=\!\!\!\!\sum_{\left\{l_{k}\right\}_{k}:\sum_{k=0}^{N_{\mathrm{N}}}l_{k}=N_{\mathrm{G}}}\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!g_{\mathrm{M},\scriptscriptstyle{\left\{l_{k}\right\}}}\!\left(x\right)\breve{\chi}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\left\{l_{k}\right\}}},

where

\displaystyle g_{\mathrm{M},\scriptscriptstyle{\left\{l_{k}\right\}}}\!\left(x\right)=

\displaystyle\sum_{i=0}^{N_{\mathrm{C}}}c_{\mathrm{Gl};i}g_{\scriptscriptstyle{\left\{l_{k}\right\}}}\!\left(x_{i}\right),

(27)

and
x_{i}
is the noise amplitude of the
i
th noisy circuit used for the mitigation (generally we assume
x_{0}=x
is the original noise level). Notice the
\breve{\chi}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\left\{l_{k}\right\}}}
terms are not altered by the mitigation only their coefficients (
g_{\mathrm{M},\scriptscriptstyle{\left\{l_{k}\right\}}}\!\left(x\right)
). The proxy bias is therefore given by (see Eq. (
2.3.1
)):

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{\scriptscriptstyle\heartsuit}=\left|1-g_{\mathrm{M},\scriptscriptstyle{\left\{N_{\mathrm{G}},0,\dots,0\right\}}}\!\left(x\right)\right|+\sum_{\left\{l_{k}\right\}_{k}:\sum_{k=0}^{N_{\mathrm{N}}}l_{k}=N_{\mathrm{G}};l_{0}\neq N_{\mathrm{G}}}\left|g_{\mathrm{M},\scriptscriptstyle{\left\{l_{k}\right\}}}\!\left(x\right)\right|\#\left[\breve{\chi}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\left\{l_{k}\right\}}}\right].

(28)

In this work we will only make use of the most significant order approximation to the proxy bias (i.e. the noise level after mitigation). We provide example derivations of the proxy bias in Apps
B.4.4
and
B.5.3
.

2.3.4
Runtime Scaling

Although mitigation reduces the bias, it will increase the variance (
\sigma^{2}
) and may increase the number of gates required within a circuit, and therefore each circuit’s runtime. In order to account for both the increase in variance and the increase in the runtime we introduce the runtime scaling (
S_{\mathrm{Gl}}
), which is the ratio of the total runtime required in the mitigated case to the total runtime in the unmitigated case to obtain the same precision. Naturally, the smaller the runtime scaling the better (for a fixed bias). For the equations below, we assume that the observables measured are Pauli word operators
8
8

8

The application of the derivation in Sec.
2.3.4
to non-Pauli observables is straightforward since any observable can be decomposed as a function of Pauli observables.

\hat{O}\in\left\{\mathrm{I},\mathrm{X},\mathrm{Y},\mathrm{Z}\right\}^{\otimes{N_{\mathrm{Q}}}}
, where
{N_{\mathrm{Q}}}
is the number of qubits.

We define the unmitigated total runtime by:

\displaystyle t_{0}=N_{\mathrm{R}}\left(\tau_{\mathrm{E}}+\tau_{0}\right),

(29)

where
N_{\mathrm{R}}
is the number of repeated runs (or shots) of a circuit we must make (on average) to obtain our target variance (
\sigma^{2}
). For the worst case observable expectation we have:

\displaystyle N_{\mathrm{R}}=\frac{1}{\sigma^{2}}.

(30)

\tau_{\mathrm{E}}
is the average execution time of our unmitigated circuit and
\tau_{0}
is the time taken for initialisation and readout (treated as independent of the size of the circuit). For mitigated circuits the total runtime is given by:

\displaystyle t_{\mathrm{M}}=C_{\mathrm{Gl}}^{2}N_{\mathrm{R}}\left(F_{{\mathrm{Gl,L}}{\mathrm{}}}\tau_{\mathrm{E}}+\tau_{0}\right),

(31)

where
F_{{\mathrm{Gl,L}}{\mathrm{}}}
is the length scale factor, i.e. the average increase in runtime owing to the mitigation; and
C_{\mathrm{Gl}}
is the global sampling cost for the mitigation, i.e. the scale factor for the increase in the number of samples required for the mitigation to obtain the same variance as the unmitigated circuit
[
14
]
:

\displaystyle C_{\mathrm{Gl}}=

\displaystyle\sum_{i=0}^{N_{\mathrm{C}}}\left|c_{\mathrm{Gl};i}\right|,

\displaystyle F_{{\mathrm{Gl,L}}{\mathrm{}}}=

\displaystyle\frac{\sum_{i=0}^{N_{\mathrm{C}}}\left|c_{\mathrm{Gl};i}\right|\tau_{\mathrm{E},i}}{C_{\mathrm{Gl}}\tau_{\mathrm{E}}},

(32)

where
\tau_{\mathrm{E},i}
and
c_{\mathrm{Gl};i}
are the average execution time and the mitigation coefficient, respectively, of the
i
th circuit variant (see Eq. (
3
) and Ref.
[
14
]
).

The runtime scaling (
S_{\mathrm{Gl}}
) is given by the ratio of the mitigated runtime to the unmitigated runtime:

\displaystyle S_{\mathrm{Gl}}=\frac{t_{\mathrm{M}}}{t_{0}}\approx\begin{cases}C_{\mathrm{Gl}}^{2}&\text{ if }\tau_{\mathrm{E}}\ll\tau_{0},\\
C_{\mathrm{Gl}}^{2}F_{{\mathrm{Gl,L}}{\mathrm{}}}&\text{ if }\tau_{\mathrm{E}}\gg\tau_{0}.\end{cases}

(33)

Since we will mainly focus on the large circuit regime (see Sec.
2.4.3
) we will use the latter limit.

We can also define the width scale factor:

\displaystyle F_{{\mathrm{Gl,W}}{\mathrm{}}}=\frac{N_{\mathrm{Q},\mathrm{M}}}{{N_{\mathrm{Q}}}},

(34)

which is the ratio of qubits required for the mitigation (
N_{\mathrm{Q},\mathrm{M}}
) to those required for the original noisy circuit (
{N_{\mathrm{Q}}}
). This provides a natural definition for the area scale factor:

\displaystyle F_{{\mathrm{Gl,A}}{\mathrm{}}}=F_{{\mathrm{Gl,L}}{\mathrm{}}}\left\lfloor F_{{\mathrm{Gl,W}}{\mathrm{}}}\right\rfloor,

(35)

which expresses the number of times we could run the original circuit using the time and resources required to run the mitigated circuit, by running the original circuit multiple times in parallel on the circuit used for the mitigated results. We take the largest integer smaller than
F_{{\mathrm{Gl,W}}{\mathrm{}}}
because we cannot split up the qubits required to implement the original circuit between runs, in general. In this more general case the runtime scaling is given by:

\displaystyle S_{\mathrm{Gl}}=\frac{t_{\mathrm{M}}\left\lfloor F_{{\mathrm{Gl,W}}{\mathrm{}}}\right\rfloor}{t_{0}}\approx\begin{cases}C_{\mathrm{Gl}}^{2}\left\lfloor F_{{\mathrm{Gl,W}}{\mathrm{}}}\right\rfloor&\text{ if }\tau_{\mathrm{E}}\ll\tau_{0},\\
C_{\mathrm{Gl}}^{2}F_{{\mathrm{Gl,A}}{\mathrm{}}}&\text{ if }\tau_{\mathrm{E}}\gg\tau_{0}.\end{cases}

(36)

However, for the methods we consider in this work
F_{{\mathrm{Gl,W}}{\mathrm{}}}=1
.

2.3.5
Noise Boundaries

It is often possible to reduce the bias at the cost of increasing the runtime scaling, for a given mitigation method (such as zero-noise extrapolation). We call this ‘increasing the order of the mitigation’ and define an order parameter
N_{\mathrm{M}}
, where larger
N_{\mathrm{M}}
means, in general, lower bias but longer runtime. For most applications one must solve a Pareto optimisation problem where the weighting given to the (proxy) bias and the runtime scaling will depend on the application.

In order to permit the comparison of mitigation methods that manage the bias-runtime-scaling (or equivalently bias-variance) trade-off relation differently, we introduce the noise boundary (
e_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{B}}
). The noise boundary is the maximum noise level (Sec.
2.3.2
) that can be mitigated, given that we require a bias smaller than or equal to
\varepsilon_{\scriptscriptstyle\pluscirc}
and a runtime scaling less than or equal to
S_{\pluscirc}
(where the exact values of
\varepsilon_{\scriptscriptstyle\pluscirc}
and
S_{\pluscirc}
will be set by the problem we wish to solve and the equipment we have available). Naturally, the larger the noise boundary, the better the mitigation. A general expression for the noise boundary can be difficult to formulate analytically so we will focus on the large circuit regime (see Sec.
2.4.3
), considering only the most significant contributions to the proxy bias and runtime scaling
9
9

9

Where approximations to the runtime scaling and/or bias must be made one should err on the side of caution (i.e. round up the bias and runtime scaling) so that the noise boundary is guaranteed to be an upper bound (to the specified order).
.

In order to calculate the noise boundary we first characterise the proxy bias and the runtime scaling as functions of the number of noisy gates (
N_{\mathrm{G}}
), noise level (
e_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{G}}
), and the order of mitigation (
N_{\mathrm{M}}
) for a given mitigation method and noise model:

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{\scriptscriptstyle\heartsuit;\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{M}}=

\displaystyle f_{\scriptscriptstyle\heartsuit}\left(N_{\mathrm{G}},e_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{G}},N_{\mathrm{M}}\right),

\displaystyle S_{\mathrm{Gl}}=

\displaystyle f_{\mathrm{RS}}\left(N_{\mathrm{G}},e_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{G}},N_{\mathrm{M}}\right).

(37)

Then we solve these equations simultaneously with
\varepsilon_{\scriptscriptstyle\heartsuit;\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{M}}=\varepsilon_{\scriptscriptstyle\pluscirc}
and
S_{\mathrm{Gl}}=S_{\pluscirc}
to obtain
e_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{G}}
as a function of
\varepsilon_{\scriptscriptstyle\pluscirc}
and
S_{\pluscirc}
(and possibly
N_{\mathrm{G}}
). In this case
e_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{G}}=e_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{B}}
(i.e. the noise boundary):

\displaystyle e_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{B}}=f_{\mathrm{NB}}\left(N_{\mathrm{G}},\varepsilon_{\scriptscriptstyle\pluscirc},S_{\pluscirc}\right).

(38)

The larger the boundary the higher the noise level we can mitigate, so larger boundaries imply better mitigation methods. However, it is the rate of change with
S_{\pluscirc}
that is more interesting when we are comparing methods in the large circuit regime (see Sec.
2.4.3
).

For an examples of a noise boundary calculation see App.
B.5.5
.

2.4
Regimes

In order to permit the comparison of different mitigation methods in a tractable way we will focus on two main regimes: large circuits and small noise levels. To facilitate our analysis we define our order parameter:

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}\left(f\left(y_{0},y_{1},\dots\right)\right),

(39)

as a function that tends to zero in the limit
f\rightarrow 0
.
f
is an arbitrary function that will indicate the most significant term of the Laurent series of
\mathcal{O}
, up to constant scale factors. We add a subscript (or two) to
\mathcal{O}
in order to specify the regime that we are in.

2.4.1
Small Amplitude Regime

In all regimes we assume that the noise amplitude of each individual gate is small, e.g.
x,x^{2}N_{\mathrm{G}}\ll 1
, where
N_{\mathrm{G}}
is the number of noisy gates in the circuit. So we can neglect all but the smallest powers of
x
in our calculations, remaining terms can be replaced by:

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{0}\left(f\left(x\right)\right),

(40)

where
\mathcal{O}_{0}\rightarrow 0
as
x\rightarrow 0
.

2.4.2
Small Noise Level Regime

In the small noise level regime, the noise level (a function of the noise amplitude:
x
and the number of gates:
N_{\mathrm{G}}
, see Sec.
2.3.2
) in the circuit is small (e.g.
e_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{G}}\ll 1
) so that we consider only the highest orders in
e_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{G}}
. In this regime we denote the neglected component:

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{\mathrm{S}}\left(f\left(e_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{G}}\right)\right),

(41)

this tends to zero in the limit
e_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{G}}\rightarrow 0
, which is the small noise limit. It is in this regime that we calculate the proxy bias but it may be possible to generalise to other regimes.

2.4.3
Large Circuit Regime

In the large circuit regime we assume that the number of gates in the circuit is large (
N_{\mathrm{G}}\gg 1
) so that we consider only the highest orders in
N_{\mathrm{G}}
. In this regime we denote the neglected component:

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{\mathrm{L}}\left(f\left(N_{\mathrm{G}}\right)\right),

(42)

this tends to zero in the limit
N_{\mathrm{G}}\rightarrow\infty
(so
N_{\mathrm{G}}
will generally appear in the denominator). The large circuit limit is when the number of gates tends to infinity but the (full circuit) noise level (
e_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{G}}
) stays fixed, so this requires that the (gate-wise) noise level tends to zero, i.e.
x\rightarrow 0
as
N_{\mathrm{G}}\rightarrow\infty
such that
e_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{G}}
is independent of
N_{\mathrm{G}}
(at least to leading order). If we are in the regime of large circuits and low noise levels then we use
\mathcal{O}_{\mathrm{LS}}
or, if the behaviour is the same for either large circuits or small noise levels, we use
\mathcal{O}_{\mathrm{L/S}}
.

2.5
Qualitative Certification Criteria

We can use our quantitative metrics (Sec.
2.3
) to derive some qualitative criteria for certifying our mitigation methods (Sec.
2.5.1
) and strategies (Sec.
2.5.2
). One can pick and choose the relevant certification criteria depending on the target application.

2.5.1
Mitigation Methods

We certify mitigation methods with respect to a particular noise model (such as stochastic noise (Sec.
3.3
) or rotational errors (Sec.
3.4
)) as their behaviour can depend strongly on the type of noise they are mitigating. We propose the following certification criteria:

1.

Scalable (S):
A scalable mitigation method is one for which the runtime scaling (
S_{\mathrm{Gl}}
) is finite and independent of circuit size (
N_{\mathrm{G}}
) to leading order, in the large circuit regime (
N_{\mathrm{G}}\gg 1
, see Sec.
2.4.3
) when the noise level (
e_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{G}}
, Sec.
2.3.2
) is independent of
N_{\mathrm{G}}
. i.e.
S_{\mathrm{Gl}}<\infty
for
N_{\mathrm{G}}\rightarrow\infty
if
e_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{G}}<\infty
. This means we can mitigate arbitrarily large circuits without a change in the runtime scaling provided the noise amplitude (
x
) is reduced by a proportionate amount, so that the noise level (
e_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{G}}
) remains fixed. This will generally mean the length of circuit we can mitigate using our method will be inversely proportional to the noise amplitude.

2.

Unbounded (U):
An unbounded (in principle) mitigation method can mitigate arbitrarily large noise levels, assuming we permit arbitrarily large runtime scaling, in the large circuit limit, i.e.
e_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{B}}\rightarrow\infty
(Sec.
2.3.5
) as
S_{\pluscirc}\rightarrow\infty
for fixed
\varepsilon_{\scriptscriptstyle\pluscirc}
and
N_{\mathrm{G}}=\infty
.

3.

Precise (P):
A precise mitigation method is either unbiased or has a bias after mitigation that can be made arbitrarily small for suitably large runtime scaling, in the small noise regime, i.e.
\varepsilon_{\scriptscriptstyle\heartsuit;\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{M}}\rightarrow 0
for
S_{\pluscirc}\rightarrow\infty
, where
e_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{G}}\ll 1
.

4.

Efficient (E):
An efficient mitigation method has a runtime scaling (
S_{\mathrm{Gl}}
, Sec.
2.3.4
) that tends to unity (i.e. cost free) as the noise level tends to zero, for fixed target bias (
\varepsilon_{\scriptscriptstyle\pluscirc}
). i.e.
S_{\mathrm{Gl}}\rightarrow 1
as
e_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{G}}\rightarrow 0
for
\varepsilon_{\scriptscriptstyle\pluscirc}
fixed.

5.

Robust (R):
A robust mitigation method has metrics (i.e. runtime scaling, Sec.
2.3.4
, and proxy bias, Sec.
2.3.3
) that are independent of deviations from the target noise due to small, characterised, imperfections in variant generation (see Sec.
4.2
), to leading order. In general, we expect the process of generating noisy gate variants, i.e. noise amplification or transformation (e.g. see Secs
4.2.2
and
4.2.3
) to be imperfect and to result in noise differing slightly from the target noise. A method is robust if these imperfections, provided they are characterised and of the same order as the original noise amplitude (
x
), do not change the value of the metrics to leading order in the noise level for the small noise regime (Sec.
2.4.2
) and to leading order in the number of gates for the large circuit regime (Sec.
2.4.3
). If the coefficients, but not the order, of the leading order terms change then it is quasi-robust (qR). A (quasi-)hardware-robust method is (q)R for the imperfections that occur on a particular hardware, not necessarily for all possible imperfections. Imperfections must be well characterised and taken into account in the coefficient and metric determination.

Some of the implications of these certifications are give below:

•

Scalable
mitigation methods can take advantage of reductions in noise levels (e.g. through hardware tuning
[
8
]
or error correction
[
9
]
) to mitigate larger circuits without an increase in relative cost.

•

Unbounded
mitigation methods can mitigate, arbitrarily large, arbitrarily noisy circuits with a runtime that depends only on their noise level and (possibly) their target bias (in the large circuit limit).

•

Precise
mitigation methods make high precision applications, e.g. precise characterisation, possible. Since they can make the bias after mitigation arbitrarily small.

•

Efficient
mitigation methods make high precision applications practical (in the small noise regime). Since the cost of mitigation disappears as the (pre-mitigation) noise level decreases.

•

Robust
methods maintain their performance in the face of inevitable hardware imperfections.

A mitigation method that is scalable, unbounded, precise, efficient, and robust is certified as a SUPER mitigation method. If the method is only quasi-robust, we certify it SUPEqR. This latter designation is sufficient for inclusion in an efficacious mitigation strategy (see Sec.
2.5.2
).

2.5.2
Mitigation Strategies

A mitigation strategy (e.g. see Sec.
6.1
) is a complete prescription for producing mitigated observable estimates from a quantum algorithm using a particular piece of noisy quantum hardware. They include:

•

Physical Operations:
The (characterised) physical operations
10
10

10

Physical operations are a generalisation of gates and consists of all gates implementable on a piece of quantum hardware as well as all other transformations it can induce. Examples of physical operations are the process of changing a single (or small subset) of parameters of the effective Hamiltonian that generates a particular gate, measurements, the turning on or off of an external field, a period of the free evolution of a qubit, and a period of evolution under a static or dynamic external field.
the quantum hardware can implement.

•

Mitigation Methods:
A (set of) mitigation method(s) designed to obtain mitigated observable estimates using the available physical operations.

•

Compilation Scheme:
A scheme for converting target algorithms into mitigated quantum circuits.

•

Additional Implementation-Dependent Requirements:
Depending on the hardware available and certification required (Sec.
2.5
) there may be additional considerations. For example, for a scalable strategy we require that the noise level be independent of the circuit length in order to ensure the runtime scaling is independent of the circuit length (see Sec.
2.5.1
).

The idea is that a particular hardware will have a basis set of physical operations that it can implement, each of which will have an associated noise model. Mitigation methods will generally
11
11

11

Identity insertion based methods (Sec.
4.2.4
) can often perform mitigation without requiring any gates beyond those required for a particular algorithm.
require additional gates or physical operations to implement the mitigation, beyond those required to implement the unmitigated target circuit. The mitigation methods used within the mitigation strategy should be tailored to the hardware they are designed to mitigate and the gate set(s) they require must be implementable (and, for noise-aware (Sec.
4.1
) strategies, characterisable) on that hardware. A mitigation method is
compatible
with a piece of hardware if the hardware can generate all the gates or physical operations required to implement the mitigation method. We introduce three strategy certification criteria:

1.

Universal:
A universal mitigation strategy can mitigate any quantum algorithm
12
12

12

If we have a ‘quantum accelerator’, i.e. a piece of hardware that is not universal but targeted at a particular task, we can relax the universality requirement and require only that the mitigation strategy can mitigate any algorithm the ‘quantum accelerator’ is designed to implement.
. It is designed to be implemented using a particular universally-mitigatable piece of hardware, which is a piece of hardware that can generate all the gates or physical operations required to produce a mitigated circuit for any quantum algorithm. The strategy is hardware specific so the mitigation methods it contains may not be able to mitigate any possible noise model (unlike, for example, the PEC strategy of
[
14
]
). Universality requires only that the mitigation methods can mitigate the noise (or some of the noise) that occurs on the specified hardware. The advantage of this restriction is that we can target specific noise models and focus on scalability and efficacy rather than requiring our methods to be completely general.

2.

Scalable:
A scalable mitigation strategy consists of only scalable mitigation methods (see Sec.
2.5.1
). This means we can mitigate arbitrarily large circuits without a diverging runtime scaling (Sec.
2.3.4
). There is quite convincing evidence that, for stochastic noise
13
13

13

Pure rotational errors are generally cheaper to mitigate than stochastic noise and can be scalably mitigated even if the noise level (Sec.
2.3.2
) grows with the square root of the number of gates (see Table
4
).
, the sampling cost of any mitigation method scales exponentially with the number of gates (
N_{\mathrm{G}}
) for constant (gate-wise) noise amplitude (
x
), e.g. see Ref.
[
6
]
. Thus, for fixed
x
no mitigation method is scalable (for stochastic noise). Therefore, we are forced to consider reductions in the noise amplitude (e.g. through hardware tuning
[
8
]
or error correction
[
9
]
) as part of our mitigation strategy when we scale to larger circuits. It is why we consider a method scalable if the runtime scaling is finite in the limit
N_{\mathrm{G}}\rightarrow\infty
when the limit is taken such that the noise level (
e_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{G}}
, Sec.
2.3.2
) is kept constant. One must ensure that the methods are still scalable when implemented using the quantum hardware specified in the strategy.

3.

Efficacious:
A mitigation strategy is efficacious if all the mitigation methods it contains are precise, efficient, unbounded, and robust (or at least quasi-hardware-robust), see Sec.
2.5.1
. This means, given a sufficiently high tolerated runtime scaling (
S_{\pluscirc}
) it can reduce the bias (of the circuits it can mitigate) to any desired level no matter what the initial noise level, i.e. we can ensure:

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{\scriptscriptstyle\heartsuit;\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{M}}\leq

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{\scriptscriptstyle\pluscirc},

(43)

for
S_{\pluscirc}
sufficiently large, regardless of the initial value of
e_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{G}}
. It also means that any improvements to our hardware that lead to reductions in the circuit noise level will reduce the runtime scaling required for mitigation. Lastly, quasi-hardware-robustness means that (for the hardware in our strategy) the runtime scaling and proxy bias are the same, to leading order, when actually implemented on our hardware as in the ideal case (where the noise tailoring required for mitigation does not introduce any new errors). Without quasi-hardware-robustness there is no guarantee that the other certifications will be meaningful for our strategy in practice.

Universal strategies are required for universal quantum computation, scalable strategies are worth investing in long term, and efficacious strategies are more likely to be practical. If a strategy is universal, scalable, and efficacious then we certify it as a USEful mitigation strategy. In the next section we investigate and characterise our target noise models.

3
Characterisation of Noise

Here we introduce some of our notation for noise channels (Sec.
3.2
) as well as Pauli transfer matrices (Sec.
3.2.1
), a useful representation for analysing noise models. Then we present characterisation data for three types of noise: stochastic noise (SN, Sec.
3.3
), rotational errors (RE, Sec.
3.4
), and over-rotation errors (ORE, Sec.
3.5
).

3.1
Characterisation

We consider a noise channel to be characterised when we can express it as a Kraus map
[
2
]
or transfer matrix (Sec.
3.2.1
). With access to quantum hardware one can characterise (i.e. determine the structure of) noise channels using gate-set tomography
[
17
]
, classical simulation, etc. But, for illustrative purposes, we will consider three explicit classes of noise: stochastic noise (SN, Sec.
3.3
), rotational errors (RE, Sec.
3.4
), and (stochastic) over-rotation errors (ORE, Sec.
3.5
). It can be useful to model noise channels in different ways for different applications.

3.2
Circuit-Independent Noise Models

We consider noise to be associated with a gate. A noisy gate (
\tilde{U}_{{\mathrm{}}{\mathrm{}}}
) is equivalent to a unitary (noise-free) gate (
U_{{\mathrm{}}{\mathrm{}}}
) followed by an error channel
14
14

14

Our analysis will be based on modelling error channels as following their gate but all the analysis is analogous if the channel precedes the gate.
(
\mathcal{E}_{{\mathrm{}}{\mathrm{}}}
), which may or may not be unitary. We augment the standard circuit notation to account for noisy gates by representing noisy gates (which may not be unitaries) as gates (e.g.
U_{{\mathrm{}}{\mathrm{}}}
) with a tilde atop:

(47)

where
p_{\mathrm{P},k}\geq 0
is the probability of the operator
\mathrm{P}_{k}
occurring on a given run and
p
is the probability of any error occurring.
\mathrm{P}_{k}\in\left\{\mathrm{I},\mathrm{X},\mathrm{Y},\mathrm{Z}\right\}^{\otimes{N_{\mathrm{Q}}}}
is the
k
th Pauli word (tensor products of Pauli operators) for
{N_{\mathrm{Q}}}
qubits (see App.
B.1.1
);
\mathrm{P}_{0}
is the global identity. An alternative way to represent an error channel is with a (Pauli) transfer matrix.

3.2.1
Transfer Matrices

The (Pauli) transfer matrix (
\hat{T}_{\mathcal{E}_{{\mathrm{}}{\mathrm{}}}}
) associated with a channel (
\mathcal{E}_{{\mathrm{}}{\mathrm{}}}
) is defined by
[
2
,
17
]
:

\displaystyle\left(\hat{T}_{\mathcal{E}_{{\mathrm{}}{\mathrm{}}}}\right)_{i,j}=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2^{{N_{\mathrm{Q}}}}}\mathrm{Tr}\left[\mathrm{P}_{i}\mathcal{E}_{{\mathrm{}}{\mathrm{}}}\left(\mathrm{P}_{j}\right)\right],

(48)

where
{N_{\mathrm{Q}}}
is the number of qubits in the channel and
\mathrm{P}_{k}\in\left\{\mathrm{I},\mathrm{X},\mathrm{Y},\mathrm{Z}\right\}^{\otimes{N_{\mathrm{Q}}}}
is the
k
th Pauli word operator. Transfer matrices have the nice property that the composition of two channels is just the product of their transfer matrices:

\displaystyle\hat{T}_{\mathcal{E}_{{\mathrm{}2}{\mathrm{}}}\circ\mathcal{E}_{{\mathrm{}1}{\mathrm{}}}}=\hat{T}_{\mathcal{E}_{{\mathrm{}2}{\mathrm{}}}}\hat{T}_{\mathcal{E}_{{\mathrm{}1}{\mathrm{}}}}.

(49)

If a transfer matrix is diagonalisable then we can write it as:

\displaystyle\hat{T}_{\mathcal{E}_{{\mathrm{}}{\mathrm{}}}}=\hat{W}\hat{D}\hat{W}^{-1}.

(50)

If, in addition,
\hat{W}
is independent of the noise amplitude (
x
) of the channel
\mathcal{E}_{{\mathrm{}}{\mathrm{}}}
; then we have a model similar to that of Sec.
2.2.1
and all the
x
dependence is included in the diagonal matrix (
\hat{D}
). This property is particularly useful for local mitigation strategies (see Sec.
4.5
) as it massively simplifies the problem. In this case the noise channel can be fully characterised by its eigenvalues (
\lambda_{m}\left(x\right)
) and the matrix
\hat{W}
. There are some useful properties of error channels that we shall discuss in the following sections.

3.2.2
Compatibility

Noise channels are compatible if they commute (i.e.
\mathcal{N}_{{\mathrm{}k}{\mathrm{}}}
and
\mathcal{N}_{{\mathrm{}l}{\mathrm{}}}
are compatible if they satisfy the relation
\left[\mathcal{N}_{{\mathrm{}k}{\mathrm{}}},\mathcal{N}_{{\mathrm{}l}{\mathrm{}}}\right]_{-}=0
) and an error channel decomposition is self-compatible if all its noise channels are compatible. A self-compatible error channel is one that has a diagonalisation matrix (
\hat{W}
, see Sec.
3.2.1
) that is independent of the noise amplitude, in which case it boasts a self-compatible decomposition.

3.2.3
Closure

An error channel decomposition (see Eq. (
45
) and Eq. (
5
) of Sec.
2.2.1
) is closed if the span of the constituent noise channels (i.e.
\left\{\mathcal{N}_{{\mathrm{}k}{\mathrm{}}}\right\}_{k=0}^{N_{\mathrm{N}}}
) is closed under composition. Composing two closed channels (with the same constituent noise channels) next to each other will result in a composite channel with the same constituent channels, but potentially different parameters (e.g. noise amplitude).

3.3
Stochastic Noise

(Single parameter) stochastic noise (SN) can be expressed by:

8.5359pt\scalebox{5}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}$\displaystyle{\mathrm{}}$}}\@add@raggedright\end{minipage}}}\put(0.0,0.0){\includegraphics[width,page]{gateM.pdf}}\end{picture}}\end{matrix}=\begin{cases}\begin{matrix}\scalebox{0.1}{\begin{picture}\put(0.0,0.0){\includegraphics[width,page]{gateMWire.pdf}}\put(0.40119,0.42637){\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0)[lt]{\begin{minipage}[335.53992pt]\end{minipage}}}\put(0.27218,0.60885){\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0)[lt]{\begin{minipage}[391.46324pt]\raggedright\vskip \begin{matrix}\scalebox{0.1}{\begin{picture}\put(0.0,0.0){\includegraphics[width,page]{gateM.pdf}}\put(0.40119,0.42637){\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0)[lt]{\begin{minipage}[335.53992pt]\raggedright\@add@raggedright\end{minipage}}}\put(0.27218,0.60885){\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0)[lt]{\begin{minipage}[391.46324pt]\raggedright\vskip 65.0pt-\heightof{\scalebox{8}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}$\displaystyle{\mathcal{N}}_{{\mathrm{}}}^{\phantom{\dagger}}$}}}/2\hskip 72.0pt\scalebox{8}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}$\displaystyle{\mathcal{N}}_{{\mathrm{}}}^{\phantom{\dagger}}$}}\@add@raggedright\end{minipage}}}\put(0.27218,0.63959){\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0)[lt]{\smash{\begin{tabular}[t]{l}\end{tabular}}}}\put(0.77218,0.10885){\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0)[lt]{\begin{minipage}[391.46324pt]\raggedright\vskip-17.07182pt\par\noindent\hskip 8.5359pt\scalebox{5}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}$\displaystyle{\mathrm{}}$}}\@add@raggedright\end{minipage}}}\put(0.0,0.0){\includegraphics[width,page]{gateM.pdf}}\end{picture}}\end{matrix}&\text{ with probability }p.\end{cases}

(51)

where
\mathcal{N}_{{\mathrm{}}{\mathrm{}}}
is some noise channel (that may or may not cause an actual error to occur depending on the current state the qubits are in), which must be a physical (i.e. CPTP) channel for the error channel (
\mathcal{E}_{{\mathrm{}}{\mathrm{}}}
) to be physical.
p
is the probability of such a noise channel occurring and this is the noise amplitude (i.e.
x=p
). So this channel can be characterised by (see Eq. (
5
) of Sec.
2.2.1
):

\displaystyle f_{0}=

\displaystyle 1-p,

\displaystyle f_{1}=

\displaystyle p,

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}_{{\mathrm{}0}{\mathrm{}}}=

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}_{{\mathrm{}}{\mathrm{}}},

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}_{{\mathrm{}1}{\mathrm{}}}=

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}_{{\mathrm{}}{\mathrm{}}},

(52)

where
\mathcal{I}_{{\mathrm{}}{\mathrm{}}}
is the identity channel and does nothing. We can derive the corresponding proxy bias (upper bound on deviation) as follows (see Eq. (
2.3.1
) of Sec.
2.3.1
):

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{\scriptscriptstyle\heartsuit}=2\left(1-\left(1-p\right)^{N_{\mathrm{G}}}\right).

(53)

So, in the limit of infinitely many applications of the channel, we have a proxy bias of two, which is indeed the maximum possible bias for a Pauli operator, so the proxy bias may well be a tight bound. The noise level (
e_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{G}}
) is the leading order component of the proxy bias (see Sec.
2.3.2
), for stochastic noise:

\displaystyle e_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{G}}=e_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{SN}}=

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{\scriptscriptstyle\heartsuit}+\mathcal{O}_{\mathrm{S}}\left(e_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{SN}}^{2}\right),

(54)

where:

\displaystyle e_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{SN}}=2pN_{\mathrm{G}}.

(55)

This is the same order as the noise level of Ref.
[
3
]
but has an additional factor of two in order that the noise level be an upper bound (to first order).

If we have uniform stochastic noise affecting
N_{\mathrm{G}}
gates in our circuit then the channel associated to the circuit can be represented by (see Eq. (
11
) of Sec.
2.2.2
):

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathcal{C}}_{{\mathrm{}N_{\mathrm{G}},p}{\mathrm{}}}=

\displaystyle\sum_{k=0}^{N_{\mathrm{G}}}\left(1-p\right)^{N_{\mathrm{G}}-k}p^{k}\breve{\chi}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{SN},k}},

(56)

where
\breve{\chi}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{SN},k}}
is independent of
p
(see Sec.
2.2.2
):

\displaystyle\breve{\chi}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{SN},k}}=\breve{\chi}_{\scriptscriptstyle{N_{\mathrm{G}}-k,k}},

(57)

i.e.
\breve{\chi}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{SN},k}}
is a linear combination of all the channels that can be obtained from the original noisy circuit by replacing
N_{\mathrm{G}}-k
of the error channels with the identity channel (
\mathcal{I}_{{\mathrm{}}{\mathrm{}}}
) and
k
with
\mathcal{N}_{{\mathrm{}}{\mathrm{}}}
. The multiplicity (see Eq. (
17
)) is given by:

\displaystyle\#\left[\breve{\chi}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{SN},k}}\right]=\frac{N_{\mathrm{G}}!}{k!\left(N_{\mathrm{G}}-k\right)!}.

(58)

All stochastic noise channels are self-compatible since the identity channel commutes with any other channel but they are only closed if:

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}_{{\mathrm{}}{\mathrm{}}}^{2}=a\mathcal{I}_{{\mathrm{}}{\mathrm{}}}+\left(1-a\right)\mathcal{N}_{{\mathrm{}}{\mathrm{}}},

(59)

for some
0\leq a\leq 1
(i.e. the product of two constituent channels is a linear combination of the existing constituent channels). The channel has eigenvalues given by:

\displaystyle\lambda_{m}\left(p\right)=\left(1-p\right)+p\lambda_{\mathcal{N}_{{\mathrm{}}{\mathrm{}}},m},

(60)

where
\lambda_{\mathcal{N}_{{\mathrm{}}{\mathrm{}}},m}
is the
m
th eigenvalue of
\mathcal{N}_{{\mathrm{}}{\mathrm{}}}
. If the error channel is closed then
\lambda_{\mathcal{N}_{{\mathrm{}}{\mathrm{}}},0}=1
and
\lambda_{\mathcal{N}_{{\mathrm{}}{\mathrm{}}},1}=-a
:

\displaystyle\lambda_{0}\left(p\right)=

\displaystyle 1,

\displaystyle\lambda_{1}\left(p\right)=

\displaystyle 1-\left(1+a\right)p.

(61)

3.4
Rotational Errors

Rotational errors (RE) are unitary (coherent) errors generated by a Hermitian unitary operator (
P
):

\displaystyle\begin{matrix}\scalebox{0.1}{\begin{picture}\put(0.0,0.0){\includegraphics[width,page]{gateM.pdf}}\put(0.40119,0.42637){\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0)[lt]{\begin{minipage}[335.53992pt]\raggedright\@add@raggedright\end{minipage}}}\put(0.27218,0.60885){\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0)[lt]{\begin{minipage}[391.46324pt]\raggedright\vskip 65.0pt-\heightof{\scalebox{8}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}$\displaystyle{\mathcal{E}}_{{\mathrm{RE}}}^{\phantom{\dagger}}$}}}/2\hskip 72.0pt\scalebox{8}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}$\displaystyle{\mathcal{E}}_{{\mathrm{RE}}}^{\phantom{\dagger}}$}}\@add@raggedright\end{minipage}}}\put(0.27218,0.63959){\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0)[lt]{\smash{\begin{tabular}[t]{l}\end{tabular}}}}\put(0.77218,0.10885){\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0)[lt]{\begin{minipage}[391.46324pt]\raggedright\vskip-17.07182pt\par\noindent\hskip 8.5359pt\scalebox{5}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}$\displaystyle{\mathrm{}}$}}\@add@raggedright\end{minipage}}}\put(0.0,0.0){\includegraphics[width,page]{gateM.pdf}}\end{picture}}\end{matrix}\penalty\ =\penalty\ \begin{matrix}\scalebox{0.1}{\begin{picture}\put(0.0,0.0){\includegraphics[width,page]{gateMVVL.pdf}}\put(0.40119,0.42637){\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0)[lt]{\begin{minipage}[335.53992pt]\raggedright\@add@raggedright\end{minipage}}}\put(0.27218,0.60885){\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0)[lt]{\begin{minipage}[391.46324pt]\raggedright\vskip 65.0pt-\heightof{\scalebox{8}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}$\displaystyle{\penalty\ \mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\frac{\phi}{2}P}}_{{\mathrm{}}}^{\phantom{\dagger}}$}}}/2\hskip 72.0pt\scalebox{8}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}$\displaystyle{\penalty\ \mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\frac{\phi}{2}P}}_{{\mathrm{}}}^{\phantom{\dagger}}$}}\@add@raggedright\end{minipage}}}\put(0.27218,0.63959){\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0)[lt]{\smash{\begin{tabular}[t]{l}\end{tabular}}}}\put(0.954,0.10885){\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0)[lt]{\begin{minipage}[391.46324pt]\raggedright\vskip-17.07182pt\par\noindent\hskip 8.5359pt\scalebox{5}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}$\displaystyle{\mathrm{}}$}}\@add@raggedright\end{minipage}}}\put(0.0,0.0){\includegraphics[width,page]{gateMVVL.pdf}}\end{picture}}\end{matrix},

(62)

where the noise amplitude is the rotation angle (i.e.
x=\phi
) and
P
is some self-inverse operator (
P^{2}=\mathbb{I}
). This channel can be characterised by (see Eq. (
5
) of Sec.
2.2.1
):

\displaystyle f_{0}=

\displaystyle\cos^{2}\frac{\phi}{2},

\displaystyle f_{1}=

\displaystyle\sin\phi,

\displaystyle f_{2}=

\displaystyle\sin^{2}\frac{\phi}{2},

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}_{{\mathrm{}0}{\mathrm{}}}=

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}_{{\mathrm{}}{\mathrm{}}},

\displaystyle\check{\mathcal{N}}_{{\mathrm{}1}{\mathrm{}}}\left(\rho\right)=

\displaystyle\mathrm{i}\frac{P\rho-\rho P}{2},

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}_{{\mathrm{}2}{\mathrm{}}}\left(\rho\right)=

\displaystyle P\rho P.

(63)

In this case
\mathcal{N}_{{\mathrm{}0}{\mathrm{}}}
and
\mathcal{N}_{{\mathrm{}2}{\mathrm{}}}
are CPTP channels, as Sec.
2.2.1
requires, but
\check{\mathcal{N}}_{{\mathrm{}1}{\mathrm{}}}
is not. However
\check{\mathcal{N}}_{{\mathrm{}1}{\mathrm{}}}
is normalised and can be decomposed in terms of CPTP channels:

\displaystyle\check{\mathcal{N}}_{{\mathrm{}1}{\mathrm{}}}=\frac{1}{2}\mathcal{N}_{{\mathrm{}1}{\mathrm{}}}-\frac{1}{2}\mathcal{N}_{{\mathrm{}3}{\mathrm{}}},

(64)

where
\mathcal{N}_{{\mathrm{}1}{\mathrm{}}}
and
\mathcal{N}_{{\mathrm{}3}{\mathrm{}}}
are the CPTP channels:

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}_{{\mathrm{}1}{\mathrm{}}}\left(\rho\right)=

\displaystyle\mathrm{S}_{P}\rho\mathrm{S}^{\dagger}_{P},

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}_{{\mathrm{}3}{\mathrm{}}}\left(\rho\right)=

\displaystyle\mathrm{S}^{\dagger}_{P}\rho\mathrm{S}_{P},

\displaystyle\mathrm{S}_{P}=

\displaystyle\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\frac{\uppi}{4}P}=\frac{\mathbb{I}+\mathrm{i}P}{\sqrt{2}},

(65)

so we can perform all the analyses that we need to. The proxy bias is given by (see Eq. (
2.3.1
) of Sec.
2.3.1
):

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{\scriptscriptstyle\heartsuit}=1-2\cos^{2N_{\mathrm{G}}}\frac{\phi}{2}+\left(1+\sin\left|\phi\right|\right)^{N_{\mathrm{G}}}.

(66)

Clearly the bound is not tight in this case since the noise level tends to infinity as
N_{\mathrm{G}}\rightarrow\infty
. The noise level (
e_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{G}}
) is the leading order component of the proxy bias (see Sec.
2.3.2
), for rotational errors:

\displaystyle e_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{G}}=e_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{RE}}=

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{\scriptscriptstyle\heartsuit}+\mathcal{O}_{\mathrm{S}}\left(e_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{RE}}^{2}\right),

(67)

where:

\displaystyle e_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{RE}}=N_{\mathrm{G}}\left|\phi\right|.

(68)

If we have uniform rotational errors affecting
N_{\mathrm{G}}
gates in our circuit then the channel associated to the circuit can be represented by (see Eq. (
11
) of Sec.
2.2.2
):

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathcal{C}}_{{\mathrm{}N_{\mathrm{G}},\phi}{\mathrm{}}}=\sum_{k=0}^{2N_{\mathrm{G}}}\cos^{2N_{\mathrm{G}}-k}\frac{\phi}{2}\sin^{k}\frac{\phi}{2}\breve{\chi}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{RE},k}},

(69)

where
\breve{\chi}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{RE},k}}
is independent of
\phi
, and the multiplicity (see Eq. (
17
)) is given by:

\displaystyle\#\left[\breve{\chi}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{RE},k}}\right]=\sum_{l=0}^{\left\lfloor\frac{k}{2}\right\rfloor}\frac{2^{k-2l}\left(N_{\mathrm{G}}!\right)}{l!\left(k-2l\right)!\left(N_{\mathrm{G}}+l-k\right)!}.

(70)

The factors of two and the summation are due to the fact there are different combinations of
\mathcal{N}_{{\mathrm{}0}{\mathrm{}}}
,
\check{\mathcal{N}}_{{\mathrm{}1}{\mathrm{}}}
, and
\mathcal{N}_{{\mathrm{}2}{\mathrm{}}}
that lead to the same prefactor in Eq. (
69
).

All rotational noise channels are self-compatible since
\mathcal{N}_{{\mathrm{}0}{\mathrm{}}}
,
\check{\mathcal{N}}_{{\mathrm{}1}{\mathrm{}}}
, and
\mathcal{N}_{{\mathrm{}2}{\mathrm{}}}
commute with each other and they are also closed since:

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}_{{\mathrm{}0}{\mathrm{}}}\circ\mathcal{N}_{{\mathrm{}k}{\mathrm{}}}=

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}_{{\mathrm{}k}{\mathrm{}}},

\displaystyle\check{\mathcal{N}}_{{\mathrm{}1}{\mathrm{}}}^{2}=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\mathcal{N}_{{\mathrm{}2}{\mathrm{}}}-\frac{1}{2}\mathcal{N}_{{\mathrm{}0}{\mathrm{}}},

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}_{{\mathrm{}1}{\mathrm{}}}\circ\mathcal{N}_{{\mathrm{}2}{\mathrm{}}}=

\displaystyle-\mathcal{N}_{{\mathrm{}1}{\mathrm{}}},

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}_{{\mathrm{}2}{\mathrm{}}}^{2}=

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}_{{\mathrm{}0}{\mathrm{}}}.

(71)

The channel has eigenvalues given by:

\displaystyle\lambda_{\mathcal{E}_{{\mathrm{}}{\mathrm{}}},0}\left(\phi\right)=

\displaystyle 1,

\displaystyle\lambda_{\mathcal{E}_{{\mathrm{}}{\mathrm{}}},1}\left(\phi\right)=

\displaystyle\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\phi},

\displaystyle\lambda_{\mathcal{E}_{{\mathrm{}}{\mathrm{}}},2}\left(\phi\right)=

\displaystyle\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\phi}.

(72)

3.5
(Stochastic) Over-Rotation Errors

Now (stochastic) over-rotation errors (ORE) are rotational errors that occur when a gate is produced by an operation equivalent to a rotation generated by some Hermitian unitary operator (
P
) and there is an overshoot
15
15

15

We also have under-rotation errors where there is an undershoot and we rotate less than we aim for. For ease of notation we shall consider under-rotation as a negative over-rotation.
. So we rotate by more than the target angle. Thus over-rotation errors are generated by the same operator that generates the gate. In order to have a more realistic, and general, scenario we consider stochastic over-rotation errors. Here we have a random distribution of rotational errors, which is equivalent to a rotational error channel followed by a stochastic error channel, with the same generator:
