(73)

This channel has a channel decomposition given by:

\displaystyle f_{0}=

\displaystyle\cos^{2}\frac{\phi}{2}-p\cos\phi,

\displaystyle f_{1}=

\displaystyle\left(1-2p\right)\sin\phi,

\displaystyle f_{2}=

\displaystyle\sin^{2}\frac{\phi}{2}+p\cos\phi,

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}_{{\mathrm{}0}{\mathrm{}}}=

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}_{{\mathrm{}}{\mathrm{}}},

\displaystyle\check{\mathcal{N}}_{{\mathrm{}1}{\mathrm{}}}\left(\rho\right)=

\displaystyle\mathrm{i}\frac{P\rho-\rho P}{2},

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}_{{\mathrm{}2}{\mathrm{}}}\left(\rho\right)=

\displaystyle P\rho P.

(74)

So we have the same constituent noise channels as for rotational errors (see Sec.
3.4
). The proxy bias is given by (see Eq. (
2.3.1
) of Sec.
2.3.1
):

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{\scriptscriptstyle\heartsuit}=1-2\left(\cos^{2}\frac{\phi}{2}-p\cos\phi\right)^{N_{\mathrm{G}}}+\left(1+\left|1-2p\right|\sin\left|\phi\right|\right)^{N_{\mathrm{G}}},

(75)

and we define the noise level (
e_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{ORE}}
) as the leading order component of the proxy bias for over-rotation errors:

\displaystyle e_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{ORE}}=

\displaystyle 2N_{\mathrm{G}}p+N_{\mathrm{G}}\left|\phi\right|=e_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{SN}}+e_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{RE}}=\varepsilon_{\scriptscriptstyle\heartsuit}+\mathcal{O}_{\mathrm{S}}\left(e_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{RE}}^{2},e_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{SN}}^{2}\right).

(76)

Over-rotation error channels are self-compatible and closed since the noise channels are the same as for rotational error channels (c.f. Eqs (
3.4
) and (
3.5
)). The channel has eigenvalues given by:

\displaystyle\lambda_{\mathcal{E}_{{\mathrm{}}{\mathrm{}}},0}\left(p,\phi\right)=

\displaystyle 1,

\displaystyle\lambda_{\mathcal{E}_{{\mathrm{}}{\mathrm{}}},1}\left(p,\phi\right)=

\displaystyle\left(1-2p\right)\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\phi},

\displaystyle\lambda_{\mathcal{E}_{{\mathrm{}}{\mathrm{}}},2}\left(p,\phi\right)=

\displaystyle\left(1-2p\right)\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\phi}.

(77)

In the next section we will look in more detail at some mitigation methods and their components.

4
Taxonomical Classification of Mitigation Methods

Here we classify our mitigation methods based on their component parts and the variant generation procedures they make use of. That is, the procedures used to produce the collection of noisy (variant) circuits that are used in the mitigation (i.e.
\left\{\tilde{\mathcal{C}}_{{\mathrm{}};{\mathrm{}i}}\right\}_{i=0}^{N_{\mathrm{C}}}
, see Eq. (
3
)). First, we discuss the naming convention we adopt for our mitigation methods (Sec.
4.1
); then we present the components and variant generation procedures (Secs
4.2
,
4.3
,
4.4
, and
4.5
) that we consider; and, finally, we present the methods that we study in this work, in the context of a taxonomical scheme (Sec.
4.6
).

4.1
Nomenclature

We give our mitigation methods a four component name (see Table
1
for examples):

\displaystyle\text{[Pre-tailoring]-[Variant generation][Scope]M:[Noise Awareness]},

(78)

where each component uses an acronym.

The pre-tailoring strategies are used to convert the noise models of the noisy gates to different forms before the mitigation proper. In this work we introduce and use only one: local cancellation (LC), which we use to convert non-Pauli to Pauli noise (see Sec.
4.3
).

In order to effect the mitigation we need to combine a number of different variants of our original circuit. Each circuit is produced by replacing (some of) our noisy gates with other gates, which we denote noisy gate variants. These have the same noise-free components as the gates they replace but different noise channels. We consider four variant generation strategies, which can also be used in combination: custom channels (C), these are tailored (i.e. custom made) error channels inserted after the noisy gate in order to produce new gate variants with amplified errors (see Sec.
4.2.3
); hidden inverses (HI), this is a strategy for reversing the sign (direction) of coherent error channels (see Sec.
4.2.2
); identity insertion (II), this is a noise amplification strategy that involves alternating a noisy gate and its noisy inverse (see Sec.
4.2.4
); and tuned identity insertion (TII), this is a variant of identity insertion where we tune the number of identity insertions in order to reduce the runtime scaling (see Sec.
4.2.4
).

There are three possible scopes for noise amplification: synchronous (S), where we use the same variant generation procedure for each gate being mitigated within a particular circuit; local (L), where we have a mitigation strategy that is equivalent to mitigating each gate
16
16

16

The definition of a gate is used quite flexibly for local mitigation, each ‘gate’ could be a single recognised gate, a collection of adjacent gates, or a physical operation that is part of what makes up a gate.
independently; and asynchronous (A), the most general scheme, where we can have different variant generation procedures for different gates within the same circuit (this implicitly includes local methods).

Noise awareness denotes whether the method uses quantitative knowledge about the noise models to choose the mitigation coefficients (see Sec.
2.1
), which we denote noise aware (NA); or not, which we denote knowledge free (KF). In order to specify the type of noise our method is optimised for, we can replace NA with an acronym for the noise model, e.g. SN for stochastic noise, RE for rotational errors, or ORE for (stochastic) over-rotation errors.

In the following subsections we will explore some of the components of our taxonomy in more detail.

4.2
Variant Generation Procedures

In order to effect our mitigation we make use of a number of noisy gate variants (
\tilde{U}_{{\mathrm{}};{\mathrm{}i}}
):

\displaystyle\begin{matrix}\scalebox{0.1}{\begin{picture}\put(0.0,0.0){\includegraphics[width,page]{gateM.pdf}}\put(0.40119,0.42637){\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0)[lt]{\begin{minipage}[335.53992pt]\raggedright\@add@raggedright\end{minipage}}}\put(0.27218,0.60885){\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0)[lt]{\begin{minipage}[391.46324pt]\raggedright\vskip 65.0pt-\heightof{\scalebox{8}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}$\displaystyle{U}_{{\mathrm{}}}^{\phantom{\dagger}}$}}}/2\hskip 72.0pt\scalebox{8}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}$\displaystyle{U}_{{\mathrm{}}}^{\phantom{\dagger}}$}}\@add@raggedright\end{minipage}}}\put(0.27218,0.63959){\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0)[lt]{\smash{\begin{tabular}[t]{l}\noindent\hskip 42.67912pt\scalebox{8}{$\displaystyle\sim$}\end{tabular}}}}\put(0.77218,0.10885){\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0)[lt]{\begin{minipage}[391.46324pt]\raggedright\vskip-17.07182pt\par\noindent\hskip 8.5359pt\scalebox{5}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}$\displaystyle{\mathrm{}i}$}}\@add@raggedright\end{minipage}}}\put(0.0,0.0){\includegraphics[width,page]{gateM.pdf}}\end{picture}}\end{matrix}=\begin{matrix}\scalebox{0.1}{\begin{picture}\put(0.0,0.0){\includegraphics[width,page]{gateM.pdf}}\put(0.40119,0.42637){\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0)[lt]{\begin{minipage}[335.53992pt]\raggedright\@add@raggedright\end{minipage}}}\put(0.27218,0.60885){\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0)[lt]{\begin{minipage}[391.46324pt]\raggedright\vskip 65.0pt-\heightof{\scalebox{8}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}$\displaystyle{U}_{{\mathrm{}}}^{\phantom{\dagger}}$}}}/2\hskip (79)

which have the same noise-free component (
U_{{\mathrm{}}{\mathrm{}}}
) but different noise channels (
\mathcal{E}_{{\mathrm{}};{\mathrm{}i}}
).

4.2.1
Type Preserving Procedures

For global mitigation (Sec.
4.4
) we focus on type preserving variant generation procedures (TPP, akin to Ref.
[
18
]
but extended to more general noise models). These procedures result in noisy gate variants with channels of the same type (see Sec.
3
) as the original channel. This means we can use the same form of model (see Sec.
2.2.1
) for the amplified channel as for the original channel, which simplifies the mitigation procedure (see Sec.
4.4.1
). This means we require:

p_{i}&:\begin{matrix}\scalebox{0.1}{\begin{picture}\put(0.0,0.0){\includegraphics[width,page]{gateM.pdf}}\put(0.40119,0.42637){\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0)[lt]{\begin{minipage}[335.53992pt]\raggedright\@add@raggedright\end{minipage}}}\put(0.27218,0.60885){\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0)[lt]{\begin{minipage}[391.46324pt]\raggedright\vskip 65.0pt-\heightof{\scalebox{8}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}$\displaystyle{\mathcal{N}}_{{\mathrm{}}}^{\phantom{\dagger}}$}}}/2\hskip 72.0pt\scalebox{8}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}$\displaystyle{\mathcal{N}}_{{\mathrm{}}}^{\phantom{\dagger}}$}}\@add@raggedright\end{minipage}}}\put(0.27218,0.63959){\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0)[lt]{\smash{\begin{tabular}[t]{l}\end{tabular}}}}\put(0.77218,0.10885){\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0)[lt]{\begin{minipage}[391.46324pt]\raggedright\vskip-17.07182pt\par\noindent\hskip 8.5359pt\scalebox{5}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}$\displaystyle{\mathrm{}}$}}\@add@raggedright\end{minipage}}}\put(0.0,0.0){\includegraphics[width,page]{gateM.pdf}}\end{picture}}\end{matrix}\end{cases}&\text{ for stochastic noise,}\\

(80)

with
p_{i}\neq p
or
\phi_{i}\neq\phi
for stochastic noise and rotational errors, respectively. The restrictions on the channels imposed by the requirement for type preservation will vary depending on the variant generation procedures one has access to and the noise channels that need mitigating. The hidden inverse procedure should be a TPP if it is implementable (i.e. if it is possible to reverse the sign of coherent error channels, see Sec.
4.2.2
). The use of custom channels (i.e. placing a single tailoring noise channel after the noisy gate of the same type
17
17

17

If we permit custom (tailoring) channels of more general types, i.e. not necessarily of the same type as the noise channel they are tailoring, or we permit the use of multiple sequential custom channels, then the use of custom channels is a TPP for more general noise models.
as the original noise channel) is a TPP for rotational errors (Sec.
3.4
); and also for stochastic noise (Sec.
3.3
), provided that the channel is closed (Sec.
3.2.3
). For identity insertion to be a TPP, we have the same requirements as for custom channels but we also require that the noise channels of the noisy gates commute with the noise-free components of the noisy gates. There are other variant production techniques that we do not explicitly consider here, e.g. the tuning of Hamiltonian parameters (or pulse stretching, see Refs
[
16
,
19
,
20
]
), and these will have different requirements to be TPPs.

For local mitigation (Sec.
4.5
) we do not require TPPs and it is sufficient for the error channels being mitigated to be self-compatible (see Sec.
3.2.2
) and implementable. This means for identity insertion the noise component must commute with the noise-free component of the noisy gate. For the hidden inverse procedure it must be possible to reverse the sign of coherent error channels.

If we use the completely general form of probabilistic error cancellation (see Ref.
[
16
]
) then we do not explicitly require TPPs or self-compatible error channels. We only need access to a set of (noisy) gates or physical operations with our target gate within their span (e.g. see Ref.
[
14
]
).

4.2.2
Hidden Inverses

Hidden inverses (HIs) are used to invert coherent error channels. The idea comes from Refs
[
21
,
22
]
. Their method is designed for Hermitian (self-inverse) gates with coherent error channels.

Gates are implemented by a sequence of physical operations, if the order and direction of these physical operations can be reversed then we should be able to produce the inverse of our original gate, e.g. if our noisy gate (
\tilde{U}_{{\mathrm{}}{\mathrm{}}}
) is produced using the physical operations
\tilde{A}
,
\tilde{B}
, and
\tilde{D}
(
[
21
]
):

\displaystyle\tilde{U}_{{\mathrm{}}{\mathrm{}}}=\tilde{A}\circ\tilde{B}\circ\tilde{D},

(81)

then the inverse gate is given by:

\displaystyle\widetilde{{U}^{\dagger}}_{{\mathrm{}}{\mathrm{}}}=\widetilde{D^{-1}}\circ\widetilde{B^{-1}}\circ\widetilde{A^{-1}}.

(82)

If our gate (without noise) is Hermitian then the noise-free components of both implementations (
\tilde{U}_{{\mathrm{}}{\mathrm{}}}
and
\widetilde{{U}^{\dagger}}_{{\mathrm{}}{\mathrm{}}}
) are the same (i.e.
U_{{\mathrm{}}{\mathrm{}}}=U_{{\mathrm{}}{\mathrm{}}}
). This means the only difference between
\tilde{U}_{{\mathrm{}}{\mathrm{}}}
and
\widetilde{{U}^{\dagger}}_{{\mathrm{}}{\mathrm{}}}
should be the noise channel. If the noise is completely coherent then
\widetilde{{U}^{\dagger}}_{{\mathrm{}}{\mathrm{}}}
should have an error channel that is the inverse of the error channel of
\tilde{U}_{{\mathrm{}}{\mathrm{}}}
. So, if we have a fully coherent error channel then the effective error channel of a hidden inverse is given by:

8.5359pt\scalebox{5}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}$\displaystyle{\mathrm{HI}}$}}\@add@raggedright\end{minipage}}}\put(0.0,0.0){\includegraphics[width,page]{gateM.pdf}}\end{picture}}\end{matrix}=\Big(\!\begin{matrix}\scalebox{0.1}{\begin{picture}\put(0.0,0.0){\includegraphics[width,page]{gateM.pdf}}\put(0.40119,0.42637){\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0)[lt]{\begin{minipage}[335.53992pt]\raggedright\@add@raggedright\end{minipage}}}\put(0.27218,0.60885){\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0)[lt]{\begin{minipage}[391.46324pt]\raggedright\vskip .

(83)

(85)

are the noise-free gate and noise channel respectively. We assume that it is a true over-rotation so our target (
\omega
) and erroneous (
\phi
) rotations always have the same sign, i.e.
\omega\phi\geq 0
regardless of the value of
\omega
(this approach also works if we have a true under-rotation error, i.e. we always have
\omega\phi\leq 0
). In this case we define the hidden inverse (target) angle:

\displaystyle\omega_{\mathrm{HI}}=\omega-2\frac{\omega}{\left|\omega\right|}\uppi,

(86)

so
\omega_{\mathrm{HI}}\omega<0
and the hidden inverse is a gate with a target angle of
\omega_{\mathrm{HI}}
instead of
\omega
:

8.5359pt\scalebox{5}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}$\displaystyle{\mathrm{HI}}$}}\@add@raggedright\end{minipage}}}\put(0.0,0.0){\includegraphics[width,page]{gateMVL.pdf}}\end{picture}}\end{matrix}\penalty\ \penalty\ .

(88)

Since a global sign for a gate is irrelevant (for a quantum channel) this is equivalent to our original noisy gate but with a rotational error
\phi_{\mathrm{HI}}
instead of
\phi
. Since we assume the sign of the rotational error is always the same as that of the target rotation (and
\omega_{\mathrm{HI}}
has the opposite sign to
\omega
) this means that
\phi_{\mathrm{HI}}
will have the opposite sign to
\phi
. Thus
\tilde{U}_{{\mathrm{}};{\mathrm{HI}}}
is a gate variant with the opposite rotational error to our original gate, but the same noise-free component. If the magnitude of the over-rotational error is fixed, then we have
\phi=-\phi_{\mathrm{HI}}
and therefore
\mathcal{E}_{{\mathrm{RE}};{\mathrm{HI}}}=\mathcal{E}_{{\mathrm{RE}}{\mathrm{}}}^{\dagger}
, as with the standard hidden inverse procedure. However, as we will see in Sec.
6.2.2
, we can tolerate some difference in the magnitudes of
\phi_{\mathrm{HI}}
and
\phi
as long as their signs are opposed and they are the same order of magnitude (i.e.
\phi_{\mathrm{HI}}^{2}\ll\phi
,
\phi^{2}\ll\phi_{\mathrm{HI}}
).

So we should be able to apply the hidden inverse procedure to Hermitian operators/gates and operators/gates suffering from correlated over-rotation errors (i.e. when the relative sign between the rotational error and the target rotation is fixed and independent of the rotation angle).

4.2.3
Custom channels

We can also produce gate variants by adding a custom (C) tailoring channel (
\mathcal{E}_{{\mathrm{}};{\mathrm{C},i}}
) after the original noisy gate:

(91)

The custom stochastic noise channel involves doing nothing with probability
1-r_{i}
and applying
\mathcal{N}_{{\mathrm{}}{\mathrm{}}}
with probability
r_{i}
, while the rotational channel involves deterministically applying a rotational error of
\theta_{i}
generated by the same
P
that generates the original error channel.

If we apply the tailoring channel (Eq. (
91
)) we end up with the effective channel in Eq. (
80
) with:

\displaystyle p_{i}=

\displaystyle p+r_{i}\left(p_{\mathrm{Max}}-p\right),

\displaystyle\phi_{i}=

\displaystyle\phi+\theta_{i},

(92)

where
p
and
\phi
are the original unamplified errors and we have assumed that the stochastic noise channel is closed (see Eq. (
59
) of Sec.
3.3
, and Secs
3.2.3
and
4.2.1
). Since
0\leq r_{i}\leq 1
we have (see Eq. (
59
)):

\displaystyle p_{\mathrm{Max}}=1-ap,

(93)

as the largest stochastic error we can realise using a custom channel, assuming
p\ll 1
.

We have found the optimal distributions for minimising the sampling cost of the mitigation are given by:

\displaystyle r_{i}=

\displaystyle\frac{1-\cos\frac{i\uppi}{N_{\mathrm{M}}}}{2},

and

\displaystyle\theta_{0}=

\displaystyle 0,

\displaystyle\theta_{i}=

\displaystyle\frac{\left(2i-1\right)\uppi}{N_{\mathrm{M}}},

(94)

for stochastic and rotational errors, respectively, where
N_{\mathrm{M}}+1
is the number of variants in the set of noisy gates (
0\leq i\leq N_{\mathrm{M}}
, see Ref.
[
1
]
for more details). We choose
r_{i}
and
\theta_{i}
independently of the original errors (
p
and
\phi
), since otherwise we are in the realm of feedback control rather than mitigation.

The custom channels we use are optimised to reduce the sampling cost using quantitative knowledge of the noise model. In Ref.
[
26
]
they investigate optimising (effectively) customised channels to reduce the bias, for a fixed sampling cost. They do without explicit knowledge of the noise model since they use Hamiltonian rescaling. Their work complements our own and, for stochastic noise, our custom channels could be considered as special cases of their extremal Chebyshev nodes (not their tilted Chebyshev nodes).

Customised channels and Hidden Inverses

If we have access to both hidden inverses (see Sec.
4.2.2
) and custom channels (i.e. CHI) then we can use:

\displaystyle\phi_{i}=

\displaystyle\phi+\theta_{i},

\displaystyle\theta_{i}=

\displaystyle\frac{2i\uppi}{N_{\mathrm{C}}},

\displaystyle\phi_{N_{\mathrm{C}}}=

\displaystyle\phi_{\mathrm{HI}}=-\phi,

(95)

for mitigating rotational errors, where for
i=N_{\mathrm{C}}
we use a hidden inverse and for the other (non-zero)
i
s (i.e.
0<i<N_{\mathrm{C}}
) we use custom channels.

4.2.4
Identity Insertion

Identity insertion (II, see Ref.
[
27
]
) is a knowledge-free noise-amplification technique. We amplify the noise by inserting resolutions of the identity, in terms of our noisy gate and its noisy inverse (which may be the same gate if the noise-free gate is Hermitian), before the original noisy gate, in the hope that this will amplify the noise channel without changing its structure. II requires the noisy gate (
\tilde{U}_{{\mathrm{}}{\mathrm{}}}
) and its (noisy) inverse
18
18

18

The noisy inverse gate (
\widetilde{{U}^{\dagger}}_{{\mathrm{}}{\mathrm{}}}
) is given by:

65.0pt-\heightof{\scalebox{8}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}$\displaystyle{U}_{{\mathrm{}}}^{\dagger}$}}}/2\hskip 72.0pt\scalebox{8}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}$\displaystyle{U}_{{\mathrm{}}}^{\dagger}$}}\@add@raggedright\end{minipage}}}\put(0.27218,0.63959){\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0)[lt]{\smash{\begin{tabular}[t]{l}\noindent\hskip 72.0pt\scalebox{8}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}$\displaystyle{\mathcal{E}}_{{\mathrm{}}}^{\phantom{\dagger}}$}}\@add@raggedright\end{minipage}}}\put(0.27218,0.63959){\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0)[lt]{\smash{\begin{tabular}[t]{l}\end{tabular}}}}\put(0.77218,0.10885){\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0)[lt]{\begin{minipage}[391.46324pt]\raggedright\vskip-17.07182pt\par\noindent\hskip 8.5359pt\scalebox{5}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}$\displaystyle{\mathrm{}}$}}\@add@raggedright\end{minipage}}}\put(0.0,0.0){\includegraphics[width,page]{gateM.pdf}}\end{picture}}\end{matrix}\Big)^{\!2i+1}\hskip-15.0pt\begin{matrix}\scalebox{0.1}{\begin{picture}\put(0.0,0.0){\includegraphics[width,page]{gateMWireVS.pdf}}\put(1.60476,1.70547){\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0)[lt]{\begin{minipage}[335.53949pt]\end{minipage}}}\end{picture}}\end{matrix}\penalty\ .

(98)

To first order, in the original noise amplitude (e.g.
p
or
\phi
), the noise amplitude of the amplified gate (
\tilde{U}_{{\mathrm{}};{\mathrm{}i}}
) is
2i+1
times the noise amplitude of the unamplified gate (
\tilde{U}_{{\mathrm{}}{\mathrm{}}}
) and we have:

\displaystyle p_{i}=

\displaystyle\frac{1-\left(1-p\left(1+a\right)\right)^{2i+1}}{1+a},

\displaystyle\phi_{i}=

\displaystyle\left(2i+1\right)\phi.

(99)

for stochastic noise and rotational errors, respectively, where we have assumed the stochastic noise channel is closed (
0\leq a\leq 1
, see Secs
3.2.3
and
3.3
).

Tuned Identity Insertion

Tuned identity insertion (TII) is a variant of the identity insertion method where, rather than using
i
identity insertions for the
i
th noisy gate variant (
\tilde{U}_{{\mathrm{}};{\mathrm{}i}}
), we make the number of identity insertions, used for each variant, a free parameter (
m_{i}
):

(100)

We choose
m_{i}
to minimise the runtime scaling (see Sec.
2.3.4
). We have not yet found an analytic expression for
m_{i}
so we optimise it numerically (see Table
7
and Figs (
2
)-(
7
)).
To first order the noise amplitude of the amplified gate is
2m_{i}+1
times the noise amplitude of the unamplified gate (
\tilde{U}_{{\mathrm{}}{\mathrm{}}}
) as we have:

\displaystyle p_{i}=

\displaystyle\frac{1-\left(1-p\left(1+a\right)\right)^{2m_{i}+1}}{1+a},

\displaystyle\phi_{i}=

\displaystyle\left(2m_{i}+1\right)\phi.

(101)

for stochastic noise and rotational errors, respectively, where we have assumed the stochastic noise channel is closed (c.f. Eqs (
99
) and (
101
)).

Custom channel Identity Insertion

Custom channel identity insertion (CII) is a variant of identity insertion where we produce a single noisy variant (and its noisy inverse) of our noisy gate (we do not need to produce the original noisy gate at all) with amplified noise using custom error channels. For rotational errors we have:

.

(103)

Here we have assumed the noisy inverse gate is amplified by the same amount as the original noisy gate and that they have the same error channel after amplification. For rotational errors we have found that the optimal amplification is given by:

\displaystyle\theta=

\displaystyle\frac{2\uppi}{3},

\displaystyle\implies

\displaystyle\phi_{i}=

\displaystyle\left(2i+1\right)\left(\frac{2\uppi}{3}+\phi\right),

(104)

for unbiased local mitigation. Here
\phi_{i}
is just a variant of Eq. (
99
) with the original noise level replaced by the amplified noise (i.e.
\phi
replaced by
\phi+\theta
). If we permit different custom channels for the noisy gate and its inverse we can achieve lower runtime scalings (Sec.
2.3.4
) at the cost of additional complexity. There is no equivalent approach for stochastic noise; as stochastic noise is not periodic.

4.3
Pre-tailoring: Local Cancellation

Prior to performing the mitigation proper we can apply some tailoring to our gates to make them more amenable to mitigation.

A common strategy is Pauli twirling
[
28
,
29
,
30
,
31
]
(or randomised compiling
[
32
]
), which is a knowledge-free tailoring strategy to convert non-Pauli error channels into Pauli error channels (Eq. (
47
) of Sec.
3.2
). It involves stochastically sandwiching noisy gates between other (twirling) operators. In order for it to function correctly the twirling procedure should not introduce any non-Pauli errors, which, in general, means the twirling operators should be noise free.

A similar strategy is Clifford twirling where one requires an enlarged twirling set, but the resulting channel is depolarising. In Ref.
[
33
]
they propose Clifford twirling the entire circuit, to obtain global depolarising noise, which is particularly easy to mitigate.

We introduce local cancellation (LC) as an alternative to Pauli twirling, which achieves similar ends but has different requirements. The idea is that one finds, for each noisy gate, a stochastic combination of noisy gate variants that is equivalent to the noise-free gate followed by a stochastic noise channel. The full procedure is given in App.
C.11
and requires knowledge of the noise model. However, here we shall consider the special case of applying local cancellation to a gate suffering from an over-rotation error for which we can apply hidden inverses perfectly (see Sec.
4.2.2
), to invert the over-rotation error channel.

If we assume we have a noisy gate with an over-rotation error
\phi
then our locally cancelled gate is given by (see Eqs (
51
), (
62
), and (
83
)):

\displaystyle\begin{matrix}\scalebox{0.1}{\begin{picture}\put(0.0,0.0){\includegraphics[width,page]{gateMVVL.pdf}}\put(0.40119,0.42637){\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0)[lt]{\begin{minipage}[335.53992pt]\raggedright\@add@raggedright\end{minipage}}}\put(0.27218,0.60885){\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0)[lt]{\begin{minipage}[391.46324pt]\raggedright\vskip 65.0pt-\heightof{\scalebox{8}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}$\displaystyle{\penalty\ \mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\frac{\phi}{2}P}}_{{\mathrm{}}}^{\phantom{\dagger}}$}}}/2\hskip 72.0pt\scalebox{8}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}$\displaystyle{\penalty\ \mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\frac{\phi}{2}P}}_{{\mathrm{}}}^{\phantom{\dagger}}$}}\@add@raggedright\end{minipage}}}\put(0.27218,0.63959){\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0)[lt]{\smash{\begin{tabular}[t]{l}\end{tabular}}}}\put(0.954,0.10885){\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0)[lt]{\begin{minipage}[391.46324pt]\raggedright\vskip-17.07182pt\par\noindent\hskip 8.5359pt\scalebox{5}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}$\displaystyle{\mathrm{}}$}}\@add@raggedright\end{minipage}}}\put(0.0,0.0){\includegraphics[width,page]{gateMVVL.pdf}}\end{picture}}\end{matrix},

\displaystyle\begin{matrix}\scalebox{0.1}{\begin{picture}\put(0.0,0.0){\includegraphics[width,page]{gateM.pdf}}\put(0.40119,0.42637){\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0)[lt]{\begin{minipage}[335.53992pt]\raggedright\@add@raggedright\end{minipage}}}\put(0.27218,0.60885){\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0)[lt]{\begin{minipage}[391.46324pt]\raggedright\vskip 65.0pt-\heightof{\scalebox{8}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}$\displaystyle{\mathcal{E}}_{{\mathrm{SN}}}^{\phantom{\dagger}}$}}}/2\hskip 72.0pt\scalebox{8}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}$\displaystyle{\mathcal{E}}_{{\mathrm{SN}}}^{\phantom{\dagger}}$}}\@add@raggedright\end{minipage}}}\put(0.27218,0.63959){\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0)[lt]{\smash{\begin{tabular}[t]{l}\end{tabular}}}}\put(0.77218,0.10885){\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0)[lt]{\begin{minipage}[391.46324pt]\raggedright\vskip-17.07182pt\par\noindent\hskip 8.5359pt\scalebox{5}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}$\displaystyle{\mathrm{}}$}}\@add@raggedright\end{minipage}}}\put(0.0,0.0){\includegraphics[width,page]{gateM.pdf}}\end{picture}}\end{matrix}=\begin{cases}\begin{matrix}\scalebox{0.1}{\begin{picture}\put(0.0,0.0){\includegraphics[width,page]{gateMWire.pdf}}\put(0.40119,0.42637){\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0)[lt]{\begin{minipage}[335.53992pt]\end{minipage}}}\put(0.27218,0.60885){\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0)[lt]{\begin{minipage}[391.46324pt]\raggedright\vskip 65.0pt-\heightof{\scalebox{8}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}$\displaystyle{}_{{\mathrm{}}}^{\phantom{\dagger}}$}}}/2\hskip 72.0pt\scalebox{8}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}$\displaystyle{}_{{\mathrm{}}}^{\phantom{\dagger}}$}}\@add@raggedright\end{minipage}}}\put(0.27218,0.63959){\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0)[lt]{\smash{\begin{tabular}[t]{l}\end{tabular}}}}\put(0.77219,0.10885){\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0)[lt]{\begin{minipage}[391.46324pt]\raggedright\vskip-17.07182pt\par\noindent\hskip 8.5359pt\scalebox{5}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}$\displaystyle{\mathrm{}}$}}\@add@raggedright\end{minipage}}}\end{picture}}\end{matrix}&\text{ with prob. }1-p_{\mathrm{LC}},\\
\begin{matrix}\scalebox{0.1}{\begin{picture}\put(0.0,0.0){\includegraphics[width,page]{gateM.pdf}}\put(0.40119,0.42637){\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0)[lt]{\begin{minipage}[335.53992pt]\raggedright\@add@raggedright\end{minipage}}}\put(0.27218,0.60885){\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0)[lt]{\begin{minipage}[391.46324pt]\raggedright\vskip 65.0pt-\heightof{\scalebox{8}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}$\displaystyle{P}_{{\mathrm{}}}^{\phantom{\dagger}}$}}}/2\hskip 72.0pt\scalebox{8}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}$\displaystyle{P}_{{\mathrm{}}}^{\phantom{\dagger}}$}}\@add@raggedright\end{minipage}}}\put(0.27218,0.63959){\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0)[lt]{\smash{\begin{tabular}[t]{l}\end{tabular}}}}\put(0.77218,0.10885){\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0)[lt]{\begin{minipage}[391.46324pt]\raggedright\vskip-17.07182pt\par\noindent\hskip 8.5359pt\scalebox{5}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}$\displaystyle{\mathrm{}}$}}\@add@raggedright\end{minipage}}}\put(0.0,0.0){\includegraphics[width,page]{gateM.pdf}}\end{picture}}\end{matrix}&\text{ with prob. }p_{\mathrm{LC}}.\end{cases}

(106)

So, instead of implementing our original noisy gate for every run of the circuit, we implement the original gate half the time and its hidden inverse the other half of the time. Since this is stochastic (the coefficients are true probabilities) this tailoring is free (i.e. it has a runtime scaling, Eq. (
33
), of unity), and since it is a linear ‘mitigation’ (Sec.
2.1
) it can be composed with other linear mitigation procedures as a pre-tailoring step (see Sec.
4.4.2
). The effective error probability (
p_{\mathrm{LC}}
) of the effective stochastic noise channel is given by:

\displaystyle p_{\mathrm{LC}}=\frac{1-\cos\phi}{2}=\frac{\phi^{2}}{4}+\mathcal{O}_{0}\left(\phi^{4}\right),

(107)

(see App.
C.11
for details). This means, if the original noise level is given by
e_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{RE}}
, after local cancellation the noise level is given by (see Sec.
2.3.2
):

\displaystyle e_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{SN}}=

\displaystyle 2N_{\mathrm{G}}p_{\mathrm{LC}}+\mathcal{O}_{\mathrm{L}}\left(\frac{e_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{RE}}^{4}}{N_{\mathrm{G}}^{3}}\right)=\frac{e_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{RE}}^{2}}{2N_{\mathrm{G}}},

\displaystyle e_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{RE}}=

\displaystyle N_{\mathrm{G}}\phi.

(108)

So, for fixed noise level (
e_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{RE}}
), the effective noise level after local cancellation will tend to zero in the large circuit limit (Sec.
2.4.3
). Therefore local cancellation will not only tailor but will also mitigate the noise in the small noise level or large circuit regime.

4.4
Global Mitigation

When we apply global mitigation methods we mitigate uniform noise (i.e. a collection of gates with the same type of error channel, e.g. all stochastic or rotational but not necessarily the same noise channel:
\mathcal{N}_{{\mathrm{}}{\mathrm{}}}
or Hermitian operator:
P
) with the same noise amplitude (e.g. the same
\phi
or
p
). If we wish to mitigate multiple different types of error channels with global mitigation methods then we must compose multiple separate methods (see Sec.
4.4.2
). We will start by providing an example of the derivation of linear mitigation coefficients for the mitigation of uniform stochastic noise or rotational errors.

4.4.1
Mitigation Coefficients

We consider noisy circuit variants (e.g.
\tilde{\mathcal{C}}_{{\mathrm{}};{\mathrm{}i}}
) with gates suffering from stochastic noise (Sec.
3.3
) or rotational errors (Sec.
3.4
) with noise amplitude
p_{i}
or
\phi_{i}
, respectively, for circuit variant
i
. In these cases we have a simple model of the output of the full noisy circuit (Eqs (
56
) and (
69
)):

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathcal{C}}_{{\mathrm{}};{\mathrm{}i}}=\begin{cases}\sum_{k=0}^{N_{\mathrm{G}}}\left(1-p\right)^{N_{\mathrm{G}}-k}p^{k}\breve{\chi}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{SN},k}}&\text{ for stochastic noise,}\\
\sum_{k=0}^{2N_{\mathrm{G}}}\cos^{2N_{\mathrm{G}}-k}\frac{\phi}{2}\sin^{k}\frac{\phi}{2}\breve{\chi}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{RE},k}}&\text{ for rotational errors,}\end{cases}

where
\breve{\chi}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{RE},0}}=\mathcal{C}_{{\mathrm{}}{\mathrm{}}}=\breve{\chi}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{SN},0}}
are the noise-free circuits. Now the mitigated circuit is a linear superposition of these noisy circuits (Eq. (
3
) of Sec.
2.1
):

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathcal{C}}_{{\mathrm{}};{\mathrm{M}}}=

\displaystyle\sum_{i=0}^{N_{\mathrm{C}}}c_{\mathrm{Gl};i}\tilde{\mathcal{C}}_{{\mathrm{}};{\mathrm{}i}},

so the noise-free circuit can be obtained if we require:

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\sum_{i=0}^{N_{\mathrm{C}}}c_{\mathrm{Gl};i}\left(1-p_{i}\right)^{N_{\mathrm{G}}}=1,\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \sum_{i=0}^{N_{\mathrm{C}}}c_{\mathrm{Gl};i}\left(1-p_{i}\right)^{N_{\mathrm{G}}-k}p_{i}^{k}=0&\text{ for stochastic noise,}\\
\sum_{i=0}^{N_{\mathrm{C}}}c_{\mathrm{Gl};i}\cos^{2N_{\mathrm{G}}}\frac{\phi_{i}}{2}=1,\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \sum_{i=0}^{N_{\mathrm{C}}}c_{\mathrm{Gl};i}\cos^{2N_{\mathrm{G}}-k}\frac{\phi_{i}}{2}\sin^{k}\frac{\phi_{i}}{2}=0,&\text{ for rotational errors,}\end{cases}

(109)

for
0<k\leq N_{\mathrm{M}}
where:

\displaystyle N_{\mathrm{M}}=\begin{cases}N_{\mathrm{G}},&\text{ for stochastic noise,}\\
2N_{\mathrm{G}},&\text{ for rotational errors,}\end{cases}

(110)

which will result in unbiased mitigation.

If we can accept a biased mitigation we could use:

\displaystyle\sum_{i=0}^{N_{\mathrm{M}}}c_{\mathrm{Gl};i}=

\displaystyle 1,

\displaystyle\sum_{i=0}^{N_{\mathrm{M}}}c_{\mathrm{Gl};i}p_{i}^{k}=

\displaystyle 0,

(111)

for
0<k\leq N_{\mathrm{M}}\leq N_{\mathrm{G}}
for stochastic noise and:

\displaystyle\sum_{i=0}^{N_{\mathrm{M}}}c_{\mathrm{Gl};i}=

\displaystyle 1,

\displaystyle\sum_{i=0}^{N_{\mathrm{M}}}c_{\mathrm{Gl};i}\sin\phi_{i}\sin^{2\left(m-1\right)}\frac{\phi_{i}}{2}=

\displaystyle 0,

\displaystyle\sum_{i=0}^{N_{\mathrm{M}}}c_{\mathrm{Gl};i}\sin^{2m}\frac{\phi_{i}}{2}=

\displaystyle 0,

(112)

for rotational errors. In the latter case we use the first
N_{\mathrm{M}}\leq 2N_{\mathrm{G}}
equations (iterating from left to right of Eq. (
112
) for sequential values of
m
until we reach
N_{\mathrm{M}}+1
equations). This should neglect the mitigation of higher order terms
19
19

19

In order for us to achieve exponential suppression of errors we require
p_{i}^{2}\ll p
and/or
\phi_{i}^{2}\ll\phi
for all
i
.
(e.g. terms of the same order as
\mathcal{O}_{0}\left(p^{N_{\mathrm{M}}+1}\right)
or
\mathcal{O}_{0}\left(\phi^{N_{\mathrm{M}}+1}\right)
), which will have a smaller contribution to the overall error.

The mitigation coefficients that satisfy Eqs (
111
) and (
112
) are given by:

\displaystyle c_{\mathrm{Gl};i}=\begin{cases}\prod_{m=0:m\neq i}^{N_{\mathrm{M}}}\frac{p_{m}}{p_{m}-p_{i}},&\text{ for stochastic noise,}\\
\prod_{j=0,j\neq i}^{N_{\mathrm{M}}}\frac{\sin\frac{\phi_{j}}{2}}{\sin\frac{\phi_{j}-\phi_{i}}{2}}&\text{ for rotational errors,}\end{cases}

(113)

(where the rotational expression only holds for even
N_{\mathrm{M}}
). The strategy outlined above is for synchronous mitigation strategies, where all the noisy gates are amplified by the same amount. So all the noisy circuits (
\tilde{\mathcal{C}}_{{\mathrm{}};{\mathrm{}i}}
) suffer from uniform noise. For asynchronous strategies we amplify each noisy gate by a different amount so even if the original circuit suffered from uniform noise the variant circuits (
\tilde{\mathcal{C}}_{{\mathrm{}};{\mathrm{}i}}
) may not. All concatenated local methods (see Sec.
4.5.2
) are examples of asynchronous methods but the only global non-synchronous method we consider is identity-insertion-assisted asynchronous mitigation (IIAM) otherwise known as RIIM
[
34
,
5
]
. In App.
C.8
we provide mitigation coefficients for IIAM that can be used for mitigating both stochastic noise and rotational errors (and perhaps other error types), generalising Ref.
[
34
]
(which targets stochastic noise).

4.4.2
Mitigation Composition

Mitigation composition involves (effectively) applying mitigation strategies sequentially to the same circuit to strip away one uniform noise layer at a time. The full implementation is discussed in Ref.
[
1
]
but we will present a brief example. We assume that we have a circuit (
\tilde{\mathcal{C}}_{{\mathrm{SN,RE}}{\mathrm{}}}
) suffering from independent stochastic noise and rotational errors. First, we derive two independent mitigation strategies. The first one mitigates stochastic noise (see Eq. (
3
)):

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathcal{C}}_{{\mathrm{SN}};{\mathrm{M}}}=\sum_{i=0}^{N_{\mathrm{M},\mathrm{SN}}}c_{\mathrm{SN};i}\tilde{\mathcal{C}}_{{\mathrm{SN}};{\mathrm{}i}}=\mathcal{C}_{{\mathrm{}}{\mathrm{}}}+\mathcal{O}_{\mathrm{S}}\left(e_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{SN}}^{N_{\mathrm{M},\mathrm{SN}}+1}\right),

(114)

where
\tilde{\mathcal{C}}_{{\mathrm{SN}};{\mathrm{M}}}
is the mitigated version of a circuit suffering from stochastic noise,
N_{\mathrm{M},\mathrm{SN}}
is the order of the stochastic mitigation,
\tilde{\mathcal{C}}_{{\mathrm{SN}};{\mathrm{}i}}
is the
i
th noisy circuit variant (suffering from stochastic noise) that we use in our mitigation, and
\mathcal{C}_{{\mathrm{}}{\mathrm{}}}
is the noise-free (target) circuit. The second strategy is to mitigate rotational errors (see Eq. (
3
)):

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathcal{C}}_{{\mathrm{RE}};{\mathrm{M}}}=\sum_{j=0}^{N_{\mathrm{M},\mathrm{RE}}}c_{\mathrm{RE};j}\tilde{\mathcal{C}}_{{\mathrm{RE}};{\mathrm{}j}}=\mathcal{C}_{{\mathrm{}}{\mathrm{}}}+\mathcal{O}_{\mathrm{S}}\left(e_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{RE}}^{N_{\mathrm{M},\mathrm{RE}}+1}\right),

(115)

where
\tilde{\mathcal{C}}_{{\mathrm{RE}};{\mathrm{M}}}
is the mitigated version of a circuit suffering from rotational errors,
N_{\mathrm{M},\mathrm{RE}}
is the order of the rotational mitigation, and
\tilde{\mathcal{C}}_{{\mathrm{RE}};{\mathrm{}j}}
is the
j
th noisy circuit variant (suffering from rotational errors) that we use in our mitigation. Now, we define variant generation procedures
\mathfrak{G}_{{\mathrm{SN}};{\mathrm{}i}}
and
\mathfrak{G}_{{\mathrm{RE}};{\mathrm{}j}}
where:

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathcal{C}}_{{\mathrm{SN}};{\mathrm{}i}}=

\displaystyle\mathfrak{G}_{{\mathrm{SN}};{\mathrm{}i}}\circ\tilde{\mathcal{C}}_{{\mathrm{SN}}{\mathrm{}}},

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathcal{C}}_{{\mathrm{RE}};{\mathrm{}j}}=

\displaystyle\mathfrak{G}_{{\mathrm{RE}};{\mathrm{}j}}\circ\tilde{\mathcal{C}}_{{\mathrm{RE}}{\mathrm{}}},

(116)

where
\tilde{\mathcal{C}}_{{\mathrm{SN}}{\mathrm{}}}
and
\tilde{\mathcal{C}}_{{\mathrm{RE}}{\mathrm{}}}
are the original noisy circuits suffering from stochastic noise or rotational errors, respectively, and
\tilde{\mathcal{C}}_{{\mathrm{SN}};{\mathrm{}j}}
and
\tilde{\mathcal{C}}_{{\mathrm{RE}};{\mathrm{}j}}
are the
j
th amplified (or otherwise modified) circuits used for the mitigations (see Secs
2.1
and
4.2
). So, for example,
\mathfrak{G}_{{\mathrm{RE}};{\mathrm{}j}}
could be a procedure that amplifies all the rotational error amplitude from
\phi
to
\phi_{j}
(see Sec.
4.2.3
), replaces all the gates with rotational errors with their hidden inverses (see Sec.
4.2.2
), or applies identity insertion
j
times before each gate with a rotational error (see Sec.
4.2.4
).

Now, if we have a circuit that suffers from both rotational errors and stochastic noise (
\tilde{\mathcal{C}}_{{\mathrm{SN,RE}}{\mathrm{}}}
) then we simply apply the mitigation method for rotational errors followed by
20
20

20

We assume in Eqs (
117
) and (
118
) that the two constituent mitigation methods commute, i.e.
\mathfrak{G}_{{\mathrm{SN}};{\mathrm{}i_{2}}}
and
\mathfrak{G}_{{\mathrm{RE}};{\mathrm{}i_{1}}}
commute for all
i_{1}
,
i_{2}
. This may not be the case if we use identity insertion to amplify the errors. If the mitigation of one type of error changes the amplitude or type of another we must take this into account when applying our subsequent mitigation strategies.
the one for stochastic noise. The composed mitigation strategy is given by:

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathcal{C}}_{{\mathrm{SN,RE}};{\mathrm{M}}}=\sum_{i=0}^{N_{\mathrm{C}}}c_{\mathrm{Gl};i}\tilde{\mathcal{C}}_{{\mathrm{SN,RE}};{\mathrm{}i}}=\mathcal{C}_{{\mathrm{}}{\mathrm{}}}+\mathcal{O}_{\mathrm{S}}\left(e_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{SN}}^{N_{\mathrm{M},\mathrm{SN}}+1},e_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{RE}}^{N_{\mathrm{M},\mathrm{RE}}+1}\right),

(117)

where:

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathcal{C}}_{{\mathrm{SN,RE}};{\mathrm{}i}}=

\displaystyle\mathfrak{G}_{{\mathrm{SN}};{\mathrm{}i_{2}}}\circ\mathfrak{G}_{{\mathrm{RE}};{\mathrm{}i_{1}}}\circ\tilde{\mathcal{C}}_{{\mathrm{SN,RE}}{\mathrm{}}},

\displaystyle c_{\mathrm{Gl};i}=

\displaystyle c_{\mathrm{SN};i_{2}}c_{\mathrm{RE};i_{1}},

(118)

\displaystyle N_{\mathrm{C}}=

\displaystyle\left(N_{\mathrm{M},\mathrm{SN}}+1\right)\left(N_{\mathrm{M},\mathrm{RE}}+1\right)-1

\displaystyle i_{1}=

\displaystyle i\bmod N_{\mathrm{M},\mathrm{RE}},

\displaystyle i_{2}=

\displaystyle\left\lfloor\frac{i}{N_{\mathrm{M},\mathrm{RE}}}\right\rfloor,

(119)

where
\left\lfloor\frac{i}{N_{\mathrm{M},\mathrm{RE}}}\right\rfloor
is the integer part of the division of
i
by
N_{\mathrm{M},\mathrm{RE}}
and
i\bmod N_{\mathrm{M},\mathrm{RE}}
is the remainder.

In general, our sampling cost is the product of the sampling costs of the constituent mitigation methods. So, for our example, we have (Eq. (
32
)):

\displaystyle C_{\mathrm{Gl}}=

\displaystyle\sum_{i=0}^{N_{\mathrm{C}}}\left|c_{\mathrm{Gl};i}\right|=C_{\mathrm{SN}}C_{\mathrm{RE}},

\displaystyle C_{\mathrm{SN}}=

\displaystyle\sum_{j=0}^{N_{\mathrm{M},\mathrm{SN}}}\left|c_{\mathrm{SN};j}\right|,

\displaystyle C_{\mathrm{RE}}=

\displaystyle\sum_{j=0}^{N_{\mathrm{M},\mathrm{RE}}}\left|c_{\mathrm{RE};j}\right|,

(120)

and the length scale factor is given by the sum of the length scale factors of the constituent mitigation methods minus one less than the number of constituent mitigation methods, so for our example we have (Eq. (
32
)):

\displaystyle F_{{\mathrm{Gl,L}}{\mathrm{}}}=

\displaystyle\frac{\sum_{i=0}^{N_{\mathrm{C}}}\left|c_{\mathrm{Gl};i}\right|}{C_{\mathrm{Gl}}}\frac{\tau_{\mathrm{E},i}}{\tau_{\mathrm{E}}}=F_{{\mathrm{SN,L}}{\mathrm{}}}+F_{{\mathrm{RE,L}}{\mathrm{}}}-1,

(121)

\displaystyle F_{{\mathrm{SN,L}}{\mathrm{}}}=

\displaystyle\frac{\sum_{j=0}^{N_{\mathrm{M},\mathrm{SN}}}\left|c_{\mathrm{SN};j}\right|}{C_{\mathrm{SN}}}\frac{\tau_{\mathrm{E},\mathrm{SN},j}}{\tau_{\mathrm{E}}},

\displaystyle F_{{\mathrm{RE,L}}{\mathrm{}}}=

\displaystyle\frac{\sum_{j=0}^{N_{\mathrm{M},\mathrm{RE}}}\left|c_{\mathrm{RE};j}\right|}{C_{\mathrm{RE}}}\frac{\tau_{\mathrm{E},\mathrm{RE},j}}{\tau_{\mathrm{E}}},

(122)

where
\tau_{\mathrm{E}}
is the runtime of the unmodified circuit,
\tau_{\mathrm{E},i}
is the runtime of the
i
th amplified circuit (
\tilde{\mathcal{C}}_{{\mathrm{SN,RE}};{\mathrm{}i}}
),
\tau_{\mathrm{E},\mathrm{SN},j}
is the runtime of the
j
th stochastically amplified circuit (
\tilde{\mathcal{C}}_{{\mathrm{SN}};{\mathrm{}j}}
),
\tau_{\mathrm{E},\mathrm{RE},j}
is the runtime of the
j
th rotationally amplified circuit (
\tilde{\mathcal{C}}_{{\mathrm{RE}};{\mathrm{}j}}
).

To the most significant order the mitigated noise level is the sum of the noise levels of the constituent mitigation strategies, so, for our example:

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{\scriptscriptstyle\heartsuit;\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{M}}=\mathcal{O}_{\mathrm{S}}\left(e_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{SN}}^{N_{\mathrm{M},\mathrm{SN}}+1},e_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{RE}}^{N_{\mathrm{M},\mathrm{RE}}+1}\right),

(123)

assuming
e_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{SN}}
and
e_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{RE}}
are of the same order.

4.5
Local Mitigation

When we apply local mitigation methods we (effectively) mitigate each gate (which could be a composite gate) independently, defining:

\displaystyle\begin{matrix}\scalebox{0.1}{\begin{picture}\put(0.0,0.0){\includegraphics[width,page]{gateM.pdf}}\put(0.40119,0.42637){\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0)[lt]{\begin{minipage}[335.53992pt]\raggedright\@add@raggedright\end{minipage}}}\put(0.27218,0.60885){\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0)[lt]{\begin{minipage}[391.46324pt]\raggedright\vskip 65.0pt-\heightof{\scalebox{8}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}$\displaystyle{U}_{{\mathrm{}}}^{\phantom{\dagger}}$}}}/2\hskip 72.0pt\scalebox{8}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}$\displaystyle{U}_{{\mathrm{}}}^{\phantom{\dagger}}$}}\@add@raggedright\end{minipage}}}\put(0.27218,0.63959){\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0)[lt]{\smash{\begin{tabular}[t]{l}\noindent\hskip 42.67912pt\scalebox{8}{$\displaystyle\sim$}\end{tabular}}}}\put(0.77218,0.10885){\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0)[lt]{\begin{minipage}[391.46324pt]\raggedright\vskip-17.07182pt\par\noindent\hskip \approx\penalty\ \begin{matrix}\scalebox{0.1}{\begin{picture}\put(0.0,0.0){\includegraphics[width,page]{gateM.pdf}}\put(0.40119,0.42637){\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0)[lt]{\begin{minipage}[335.53992pt]\raggedright\@add@raggedright\end{minipage}}}\put(0.27218,0.60885){\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0)[lt]{\begin{minipage}[391.46324pt]\raggedright\vskip 65.0pt-\heightof{\scalebox{8}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}$\displaystyle{U}_{{\mathrm{}}}^{\phantom{\dagger}}$}}}/2\hskip 72.0pt\scalebox{8}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}$\displaystyle{U}_{{\mathrm{}}}^{\phantom{\dagger}}$}}\@add@raggedright\end{minipage}}}\put(0.27218,0.63959){\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0)[lt]{\smash{\begin{tabular}[t]{l}\end{tabular}}}}\put(0.77218,0.10885){\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0)[lt]{\begin{minipage}[391.46324pt]\raggedright\vskip-17.07182pt\par\noindent\hskip 8.5359pt\scalebox{5}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}$\displaystyle{\mathrm{}}$}}\@add@raggedright\end{minipage}}}\put(0.0,0.0){\includegraphics[width,page]{gateM.pdf}}\end{picture}}\end{matrix},

(124)

where the definition of the noisy gate variant
\tilde{U}_{{\mathrm{}};{\mathrm{}i}}
and the values of
c_{\mathrm{L};i}
will depend on the mitigation method we use (see Table
1
).
U_{{\mathrm{}}{\mathrm{}}}
is the noise-free component of the noisy gate and
\mathcal{E}_{{\mathrm{}};{\mathrm{}i}}
is the error channel associated to the
i
th noisy gate variant. We use
N_{\mathrm{L},\mathrm{C}}
extra noisy gate variants (in addition to the original noisy gate). The curly brackets denote a quasi-stochastic combination, so the channel
\tilde{\mathcal{U}}_{{\mathrm{}};{\mathrm{M}}}
(equivalent to the effective gate we denote
\tilde{U}_{{\mathrm{}};{\mathrm{M}}}
) is given by (Eq. (
79
)):

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathcal{U}}_{{\mathrm{}};{\mathrm{M}}}=

\displaystyle\sum_{i=0}^{N_{\mathrm{L},\mathrm{C}}}c_{\mathrm{L};i}\tilde{\mathcal{U}}_{{\mathrm{}};{\mathrm{}i}},

where

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathcal{U}}_{{\mathrm{}};{\mathrm{}i}}\left(\rho\right)=

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{{\mathrm{}};{\mathrm{}i}}\left(U_{{\mathrm{}}{\mathrm{}}}\rho{U}^{\dagger}_{{\mathrm{}}{\mathrm{}}}\right).

(125)

All unbiased local mitigation methods are examples of probabilistic error cancellation (PEC, see Ref.
[
35
,
16
,
14
]
) but rather than using the universal mitigation gate set of Ref.
[
14
]
or allowing complete generality (see Ref.
[
6
]
), we focus on finding optimal gate sets for particular noise models, building on the work of Ref.
[
18
]
.

4.5.1
Eigenvalue Mitigation

If the transfer matrices (e.g.
\hat{T}_{\mathcal{E}_{{\mathrm{}};{\mathrm{}i}}}
, see Sec.
3.2.1
) of all our noisy gate variant error channels (e.g.
\mathcal{E}_{{\mathrm{}};{\mathrm{}i}}
) can be diagonalised in the same basis, i.e. we have (Eq. (
50
)):

\displaystyle\hat{T}_{\mathcal{E}_{{\mathrm{}};{\mathrm{}i}}}=\hat{W}\hat{D}_{i}\hat{W}^{-1},

where
\hat{W}
is independent of
i
, then we can perform eigenvalue mitigation. Eq. (
124
) (with equality instead of approximate equality) is equivalent to the requirement that the quasi-stochastic sum of our error channels is equivalent to the identity channel, which means:

\displaystyle\hat{T}_{\mathcal{I}_{{\mathrm{}}{\mathrm{}}}}=

\displaystyle\sum_{i=0}^{N_{\mathrm{L},\mathrm{C}}}c_{\mathrm{L};i}\hat{T}_{\mathcal{E}_{{\mathrm{}};{\mathrm{}i}}}=\hat{W}\left(\sum_{i=0}^{N_{\mathrm{L},\mathrm{C}}}c_{\mathrm{L};i}\hat{D}_{i}\right)\hat{W}^{-1}

\displaystyle\implies\sum_{i=0}^{N_{\mathrm{L},\mathrm{C}}}c_{\mathrm{L};i}\hat{D}_{i}=

\displaystyle\hat{T}_{\mathcal{I}_{{\mathrm{}}{\mathrm{}}}}

\displaystyle\implies\sum_{i=0}^{N_{\mathrm{L},\mathrm{C}}}c_{\mathrm{L};i}\lambda_{i,k}=1,

(126)

where
\hat{T}_{\mathcal{I}_{{\mathrm{}}{\mathrm{}}}}
is the identity channel transfer matrix,
c_{\mathrm{L};i}
is the
i
th mitigation coefficient, and
\lambda_{i,k}
is the
k
th eigenvalue of the
i
th noisy gate variant used in our mitigation. So we require (at most) one (distinct) noisy gate variant for each eigenvalue of our noisy transfer matrix, for unbiased mitigation.

4.5.2
Mitigation Concatenation

To mitigate an entire circuit we must concatenate the mitigation strategies for the individual gates. This can be considered as a special case of mitigation composition (see Sec.
4.4.2
) where each of the constituent strategies applies only to a single gate. Concatenated local mitigation (see
[
36
]
) involves determining mitigation coefficients (
c_{\mathrm{L};j}
) and producing noisy gate variants (
\tilde{U}_{{\mathrm{}};{\mathrm{}j}}
) for each gate independently and then concatenating these (locally) mitigated gates in order to produce a complete mitigated circuit. As an example we consider the case of a noisy circuit (
\tilde{\mathcal{C}}_{{\mathrm{}}{\mathrm{}}}
) consisting of two noisy gates:

65.0pt-\heightof{\scalebox{8}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}$\displaystyle{V}_{{\mathrm{}2}}^{\phantom{\dagger}}$}}}/2\hskip 72.0pt\scalebox{8}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}$\displaystyle{V}_{{\mathrm{}2}}^{\phantom{\dagger}}$}}\@add@raggedright\end{minipage}}}\put(0.27218,0.63959){\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0)[lt]{\smash{\begin{tabular}[t]{l}\noindent\hskip 42.67912pt\scalebox{8}{$\displaystyle\sim$}\end{tabular}}}}\put(0.77218,0.10885){\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0)[lt]{\begin{minipage}[391.46324pt]\raggedright\vskip-17.07182pt\par\noindent\hskip 8.5359pt\scalebox{5}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}$\displaystyle{\mathrm{}i_{2}}$}}\@add@raggedright\end{minipage}}}\put(0.0,0.0){\includegraphics[width,page]{gateM.pdf}}\end{picture}}\end{matrix},

\displaystyle i_{1}=

\displaystyle i\bmod N_{\mathrm{L},\mathrm{C},1},

\displaystyle i_{2}=

\displaystyle\left\lfloor\frac{i}{N_{\mathrm{L},\mathrm{C},1}}\right\rfloor,

(130)

where
\left\lfloor\frac{i}{N_{\mathrm{L},\mathrm{C},1}}\right\rfloor
is the integer part of the division of
i
by
N_{\mathrm{L},\mathrm{C},1}
and
i\bmod N_{\mathrm{L},\mathrm{C},1}
is the remainder.
N_{\mathrm{C}}
is the total number of extra circuits required for the global mitigation.

The sampling cost (see Sec.
2.3.4
) for a concatenated local mitigation with
N_{\mathrm{G}}
gates is given by:

\displaystyle C_{\mathrm{Gl}}=

\displaystyle\prod_{m=1}^{N_{\mathrm{G}}}C_{\mathrm{L},m},

\displaystyle C_{\mathrm{L},m}=

\displaystyle\sum_{j=0}^{N_{\mathrm{L},\mathrm{C},m}}\left|c_{\mathrm{L};m,j}\right|,

(131)

where
C_{\mathrm{L},m}
is the sampling cost for the
m
th gate. The length scale factor (see Sec.
2.3.4
) is given by:

\displaystyle F_{{\mathrm{Gl,L}}{\mathrm{}}}=

\displaystyle\sum_{m=1}^{N_{\mathrm{G}}}F_{{\mathrm{L,L},m}{\mathrm{}}}\frac{\tau_{\mathrm{E},\mathrm{L},m}}{\tau_{\mathrm{E}}},

\displaystyle F_{{\mathrm{L,L},m}{\mathrm{}}}=

\displaystyle\frac{\sum_{j=0}^{N_{\mathrm{L},\mathrm{C},m}}\left|c_{\mathrm{L};m,j}\right|\tau_{\mathrm{E},\mathrm{L},m,j}}{C_{\mathrm{L},m}\tau_{\mathrm{E},\mathrm{L},m}},

\displaystyle\tau_{\mathrm{E}}=\sum_{m=1}^{N_{\mathrm{G}}}\tau_{\mathrm{E},\mathrm{L},m},

(132)

where
F_{{\mathrm{L,L},m}{\mathrm{}}}
is the length scale factor of the
m
th gate,
\tau_{\mathrm{E},\mathrm{L},m}
is the unmitigated execution time of the
m
th gate,
\tau_{\mathrm{E},\mathrm{L},m,j}
is the execution time of the
j
th noisy variant of the
m
th gate, and
\tau_{\mathrm{E}}
is the unmitigated execution time of the entire circuit. If we have a uniform noise model and local execution time (
\tau_{\mathrm{E}}=\tau_{\mathrm{E},\mathrm{L}}N_{\mathrm{G}}
and
\tau_{\mathrm{E},\mathrm{L},m}=\tau_{\mathrm{E},\mathrm{L},}
for all
m
); such that the sampling costs and length scale factors are identical for every gate (
F_{{\mathrm{L,L},m}{\mathrm{}}}=F_{{\mathrm{L,L}}{\mathrm{}}}
and
C_{\mathrm{L},m}=C_{\mathrm{L}}
(we could use the geometric mean for rigour)), we find:

\displaystyle F_{{\mathrm{Gl,L}}{\mathrm{}}}=

\displaystyle F_{{\mathrm{L,L}}{\mathrm{}}},

\displaystyle C_{\mathrm{Gl}}=

\displaystyle C_{\mathrm{L}}^{N_{\mathrm{G}}}.

(133)

So if the local cost is linear in the noise amplitude (
x
:
p
or
\phi
for stochastic noise or rotational errors, respectively), we find:

\displaystyle C_{\mathrm{L}}=1+bx+\mathcal{O}_{0}\left(x^{2}\right)=\mathrm{e}^{bx}\left(1+\mathcal{O}_{0}\left(x^{2}\right)\right)

\displaystyle\implies C_{\mathrm{Gl}}=

\displaystyle\mathrm{e}^{bN_{\mathrm{G}}x}\left(1+\mathcal{O}_{0}\left(N_{\mathrm{G}}x^{2}\right)\right),

(134)

that the global cost grows exponentially with the noise level. So a linear local cost is sufficient to ensure a finite cost in the large gate limit (
N_{\mathrm{G}}\rightarrow\infty
) provided we have a constant noise level. At least for stochastic noise and rotational errors where the noise level is proportional to
N_{\mathrm{G}}x
(see Secs
2.3.2
,
3.3
, and
3.4
).

In order to determine the most significant contributions to the proxy bias for concatenated mitigation methods we separate our circuit into collections of gates with uniform noise models, determine the most significant term in the proxy bias of each and then add them up to obtain the total noise level (see Sec.
4.4.2
). This requires modelling the global effect of our mitigated channels on the circuit (see Secs
2.2.1
,
2.2.2
, and
2.3.2
), which will, in general, be qualitatively different to the original unmitigated channels. If, however, the local mitigation strategy preserves the structure of the channel and only modifies the noise amplitude (
x
is reduced to
x_{\mathrm{M}}
) then the leading contribution to the proxy bias is simply the noise level (Eq. (
2.3.1
)) with
x
replaced by
x_{\mathrm{M}}
. So we have (see Secs
3.3
and
3.4
):

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{\scriptscriptstyle\heartsuit;\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{M}}=\begin{cases}2p_{\mathrm{M}}N_{\mathrm{G}}+\mathcal{O}_{\mathrm{LS}}\left(\left(p_{\mathrm{M}}N_{\mathrm{G}}\right)^{2}\right),&\text{ for stochastic noise,}\\
N_{\mathrm{G}}\left|\phi_{\mathrm{M}}\right|+\mathcal{O}_{\mathrm{LS}}\left(\left(N_{\mathrm{G}}\phi_{\mathrm{M}}\right)^{2}\right),&\text{ for rotational errors.}\end{cases}

(135)

where
p_{\mathrm{M}}
is the stochastic noise amplitude after mitigation and
\phi_{\mathrm{M}}
is the rotational noise amplitude after mitigation.

4.6
Mitigation Method Catalogue

We now summarise the collection of mitigation methods derived from combinations of the variant generation procedures discussed above, which form the foundation of our taxonomy. Table
1
summarises the linear mitigation methods examined in this work, detailing their characteristics and their locations in the appendix. Each method can be combined with local cancellation (see Sec.
4.3
) when addressing non-stochastic noise. Fig.
1
contextualises these methods within the overall taxonomy, illustrating that synchronised and concatenated local methods are subset of asynchronous methods, as the latter encompasses all possible approaches for generating circuit variants. Local mitigation methods can also (sometimes) be considered as synchronised mitigation applied to a single gate, this equivalence holds if we use a type preserving procedure (Sec.
4.2.1
). In the following section we will certify our mitigation methods with respect to the criteria in Sec.
2.5.1
.

4.3
) before we carry out the other method (LC is knowledge free if we have rotational errors and perfect hidden inverses). An unbiased method has no (intrinsic) bias (Sec.
2.3.1
) after mitigation so the only bias will be due to uncertainty in the characterisation or implementation of the mitigation.

:
Taxonomy of the mitigation methods in Table
1
(this is far from a complete taxonomy, e.g. see
[
4
,
3
]
): Rectangles are for mitigation methods, ellipses for tailoring methods designed to produce Pauli error channels from non-Pauli error channels, and rounded-vertex rectangles are for more general tailoring methods. Text in blue indicates methods developed in this work and text in violet indicates methods adapted or optimised within this work. Methods in bold font are knowledge free. A method that is below and linked to another method with a solid line is a special case of that method, if it is linked with a dotted line it is a special case only when the noise type is preserved by the variant generation procedure (see Sec.
4.2.1
).

5
Case Study: Method Certification

Here certify the mitigation methods presented in Table
1
for stochastic noise (Sec.
5.1
) and rotational errors (Sec.
5.2
). We first present the quantitative metrics (Tables
2
and
4
) from Sec.
2.3
in appropriate regimes (Sec.
2.4
) then use these metrics to certify the methods (Tables
3
and
5
) with respect to the criteria from Sec.
2.5.1
.

We conclude by providing a numerical analysis (Sec.
5.3
) of the methods for a simple simulated circuit (Sec.
5.3.1
) suffering from stochastic over-rotation errors to elucidate our analytical discussion.

5.1
Certification for Stochastic Noise

We consider uniform closed stochastic noise (see Secs
3.3
and
3.2.3
). We gauge the strength of the noise by the size of
e_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{SN}}
(Eq. (
55
)):

\displaystyle e_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{SN}}=

\displaystyle 2pN_{\mathrm{G}}.

The noise level (Sec.
2.3.2
) of the circuit being mitigated (in the small noise level regime, Sec.
2.4.2
). Here
p
is the (gate-wise) noise amplitude, i.e. the error probability for each gate, and
N_{\mathrm{G}}
is the number of noisy gates.

In Table
2
we provide the leading order expressions for the proxy bias (i.e. the (mitigated) noise level, see Sec.
2.3.3
), runtime scaling (Sec.
2.3.4
), and noise boundary (Sec.
2.3.5
) in the appropriate regimes (see Sec.
2.4
). We then use these quantitative metrics in order to certify our mitigation methods in Table
3
.

2
).

As we see in Table
5
there are a number of methods that meet all criteria, and therefore can be certified SUPER (see Sec.
2.5.1
). As for stochastic noise, CLM is featured but CHILM and LC-CLM are much more efficient and have noise boundaries with much better scaling (see runtime scaling and noise boundary column of Table
4
). In fact, for a fixed noise level the sampling costs of both of these methods tends to unity in the large circuit limit (
N_{\mathrm{G}}\rightarrow\infty
), so they would be effectively free in this limit. If we lack knowledge about the noise model then we could use IISM:KF but this will only be effective if the noise level is reasonably small (i.e.
e_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{RE}}<\frac{1}{2}
). A better choice would be LC-IISM:KF (if it is available) as this has a noise boundary that scales with the square root of the number of gates (see noise boundary column of Table
4
). So, though not efficient, it satisfies all the other criteria. It is worth noting that LC used on its own (see LC- row of Table
4
) gives unbiased results in the large circuit limit (
N_{\mathrm{G}}\rightarrow\infty
, for fixed
e_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{RE}}
), with no excess sampling cost.

5.3
Numerical Analysis

For our numerical analysis we simulate the mitigation of the circuit in Sec.
5.3.1
, suffering from (stochastic) over-rotation errors (see Sec.
3.5
), for a range of mitigation methods. Details about the simulation methods used can be found in App.
D
. Our numerical results support our analytical analysis.

5.3.1
Benchmarking Circuit

The circuit we use for benchmarking is:

(136)

where
\left|0\right\rangle
is the
+1
eigenstate of the Pauli Z operator.
N_{\mathrm{S}}
is the number of sets of gates we use in our circuit, X, Y, and Z are the standard Pauli operators and:

\displaystyle\mathrm{T}_{1}=

\displaystyle\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\frac{\uppi}{8}\mathrm{X}},

\displaystyle\mathrm{S}_{15}=

\displaystyle\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\frac{\uppi}{4}\mathrm{Z}\otimes\mathrm{Z}},

(137)

where the
\mathrm{S}_{15}
acts on two qubits, in this case always nearest neighbours. We define our benchmarking bias as:

\displaystyle\varepsilon=

\displaystyle\sqrt{\frac{\varepsilon_{\mathrm{X}}^{2}+\varepsilon_{\mathrm{Y}}^{2}+\varepsilon_{\mathrm{Z}}^{2}}{3}},

(138)

where (see Eqs (
1
) and (
4
)):

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{\hat{O}}=\mathrm{Tr}\left[\hat{O}\left(\mathcal{C}_{{\mathrm{}}{\mathrm{}}}\left(\rho_{0}\right)-\tilde{\mathcal{C}}_{{\mathrm{}};{\mathrm{M}}}\left(\rho_{0}\right)\right)\right],

(139)

where
\rho_{0}=\bigotimes_{n=1}^{4}\left|0\right\rangle
is the initial state,
\mathcal{C}_{{\mathrm{}}{\mathrm{}}}
is the channel corresponding to the noise-free circuit,
\tilde{\mathcal{C}}_{{\mathrm{}};{\mathrm{M}}}
is the (effective) channel corresponding to the mitigated circuit (see Sec.
2.1
), and
\hat{O}
corresponds to the Pauli X, Y, or Z operator measured on the first qubit. We use Eq. (
138
) rather than the fidelity (as suggested in Ref.
[
7
]
) since our noise-free state (
\mathcal{C}_{{\mathrm{}}{\mathrm{}}}\left(\rho_{0}\right)=\rho_{0}
) is a product state of
+1
Z eigenstates, so the fidelity would not adequately account for errors in other degrees of freedom. The runtime scaling is determined in the negligible initialisation and readout time limit (see Eq. (
33
)):

\displaystyle S_{\mathrm{Gl}}=C_{\mathrm{Gl}}^{2}F_{{\mathrm{Gl,L}}{\mathrm{}}}.

(140)

For our numerical analysis we consider two different length circuits, with
N_{\mathrm{S}}=1
(18 gates) and
N_{\mathrm{S}}=10
(180 gates), to demonstrate the scalability (or otherwise) of our methods. Scalable methods are those where the runtime scaling is independent of the number of gates (
N_{\mathrm{G}}
) for a fixed noise level (
e_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{SN}}
or
e_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{RE}}
).

We choose these circuit and observable definitions because it ensures that all the error channels contribute to the final bias and thus all must be mitigated to mitigate the circuit completely.

5.3.2
Noise Model

For our numerical analysis we use a uniform (stochastic) over-rotation error (see Sec.
3.5
) noise model. So every gate is followed by a stochastic noise channel (Sec.
3.3
), with noise amplitude
p
, and a rotational error channel (Sec.
3.4
), with noise amplitude
\phi
. These error channels are both generated by the same operator as the gate itself; i.e. an error channel generated by
\mathrm{X}
follows each
\mathrm{T}_{1}
(acting on the same qubit), an error channel generated by
\mathrm{Y}
follows each
\mathrm{Y}
(acting on the same qubit), and an error channel generated by
\mathrm{P}_{15}=\mathrm{Z}\otimes\mathrm{Z}
follows
\mathrm{S}_{15}
(acting on the same qubits).

To further simplify the analysis we consider the two extremes: pure stochastic noise, i.e.
\phi=0
(see Sec.
5.3.3
), and pure rotational errors, i.e.
p=0
(see Sec.
5.3.4
). All rotational errors act in the same direction
21
21

21

If the inverse gates had rotational error channels that were the inverse of those of the forward acting gates then these error channels would cancel and we would need only to mitigate the
\tilde{\mathrm{Y}}
gates (which are self-inverse) to mitigate the entire circuit. This is a known disadvantage of using a circuit that is equivalent to the identity in the absence of noise.
(i.e. have the same sign).

5.3.3
Marking the Bench: Stochastic Noise

In Figs
2
and
3
we plot the runtime scaling (Eq. (
140
)) against the bias (Eq. (
138
)) for various mitigation methods, demonstrating the superiority of CLM for almost all regimes.

The vertical black lines in Figs
2
and
3
show the bias before mitigation, horizontal lines are the runtime scalings of unbiased mitigation methods, and each point represents a different order of mitigation (
N_{\mathrm{M}}
) for the biased methods (some biased methods become unbiased for sufficiently large
N_{\mathrm{M}}
). Increasing
N_{\mathrm{M}}
results in lower bias but higher sampling cost. Some methods have both unbiased and biased variants. Methods not featured have runtime scalings outside the range of the plot. For example, IILM has a very large runtime scaling (see Table
2
). For scalable methods (see Sec.
2.5.1
) the runtime scaling for a mitigation with a given bias should not increase with the number of gates, for a constant noise level, so the top and bottom plots of Figs
2
and
3
(with the same noise level:
e_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{SN}}
) should look the same for scalable methods.

This numerical analysis also enables us to study mitigation methods that we have not yet derived analytic formula for. We consider a biased version of CSM, where we use
N_{\mathrm{M}}<N_{\mathrm{G}}
(see Secs
4.2.3
and
4.4.1
and Eqs (
111
) and (
112
)), to compliment the unbiased version that features in Tables
2
and
3
. At present there is not an analytic expression for the proxy bias of this method but the numerical results look promising. Though the unbiased method is not scalable for stochastic noise (see Tables
2
and
3
), the biased version may well be, since the runtime scaling required for a given residual bias (bias after mitigation) seems to depend only on the noise level (
e_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{G}}=e_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{SN}}
) and not the number of gates (
N_{\mathrm{G}}=18N_{\mathrm{S}}
), see Figs
2
and
3
.

(a)

e_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{SN}}=0.02
,
e_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{RE}}=0
,
N_{\mathrm{S}}=1
(for TIILM
m_{0}=0
,
m_{1}=246
).

(b)

e_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{SN}}=0.02
,
e_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{RE}}=0
,
N_{\mathrm{S}}=10
(for TIILM
m_{0}=0
,
m_{1}=2478
).

Figure 2
:
Runtime scaling (Eq. (
140
)) versus bias (Eq. (
138
)) for a circuit (Sec.
5.3.1
) with stochastic noise (see Secs
3.3
and
5.3.2
) with noise amplitude
p=\frac{e_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{SN}}}{2N_{\mathrm{G}}}
, where
N_{\mathrm{G}}=18N_{\mathrm{S}}
is the number of gates. The vertical black lines show the bias before mitigation, horizontal lines are the sampling costs of unbiased methods, and each point represents a different order of mitigation (
N_{\mathrm{M}}
). The methods from Table
2
that are not featured have runtime scalings higher than
10^{9}
, so they do not fit in the plot.
m_{0}
and
m_{1}
are the parameters used for TIILM (see Sec.
4.2.4
).

(a)

e_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{SN}}=2
,
e_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{RE}}=0
,
N_{\mathrm{S}}=1
(for TIILM
m_{0}=0
,
m_{1}=16
).

What is more, for CHILM, the larger the circuit we wish to mitigate the smaller the runtime scaling (for fixed noise level:
e_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{RE}}
). This means CHILM is not just scalable it improves as we scale up the circuit size (for constant noise level). So we can classify CHILM as super-scalable. The vertical black lines in Figs
4
and
5
show the bias before mitigation, horizontal lines are the runtime scalings of unbiased mitigation methods, and each point represents a different order of mitigation (
N_{\mathrm{M}}
) for the biased methods (some biased methods become unbiased for sufficiently large
N_{\mathrm{M}}
). Increasing
N_{\mathrm{M}}
results in lower bias but higher sampling cost.

Methods not featured have runtime scalings outside the range of the plot. For example, IILM has a very large runtime scaling (see Table
4
). It is worth noting that, for large enough noise levels, increasing the order of mitigation can result in a lower quality mitigation, as is the case for IISM (see Fig.
5
). For scalable methods (see Sec.
2.5.1
) the runtime scaling for a mitigation with a given bias should not increase with the number of gates, for a constant noise level, so the top and bottom plots of Figs
4
and
5
(with the same noise level:
e_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{RE}}
) should look the same; or, if we have a super-scalable method like CHILM, the sampling cost will be lower in the bottom plots.

In Figs
6
and
7
we provide a similar analysis but make use of local cancellation (LC) to tailor the noise model before applying the mitigation methods (for stochastic noise). We find LC-CLM performs identically to CHILM, which is not surprising as they make use of the same noisy gate variants, and all other methods perform much better with local cancellation. The black line with a star on either end is the bias after applying local cancellation, without additional mitigation.

In the next section we will use these results to develop a mitigation strategy for a piece of hardware suffering from (stochastic) over-rotation errors (Sec.
3.5
) and certify that it is USEful (see Sec.
6.3
).

6
Case Study: Strategy Certification

Here we present (Sec.
6.1
) and certify (Secs
6.2
and
6.3
) USEM:ORE
(Universal, Scalable, and Efficacious Mitigation for Over-Rotation Errors), a mitigation strategy for a piece of hardware suffering from (stochastic) over-rotation errors. We certify it as USEful (universal, scalable, and efficacious) using the certification criteria of Sec.
2.5.2
.

6.1
The USEM:ORE strategy

In order to construct a mitigation strategy (Sec.
2.5.2
) we need to carry out the following steps:

1.

Choose Application:
specify our target application and determine appropriate metrics (e.g. Sec.
2.3
) and certifications (e.g. Sec.
2.5
) for our strategy (Sec.
6.1.1
).

2.

Characterise Hardware:
determine the elementary physical operations our hardware can implement and their associated error channels (noise) (Sec.
6.1.2
).

3.

Compare Mitigation Methods:
select the mitigation methods for our mitigation strategy; using information from the hardware characterisation, metrics, and certification (Sec.
6.1.3
).

4.

Collate Gate Set:
designate a set of gates (or physical operations) that spans the space of gates needed for our calculations and mitigations (Sec.
6.1.4
).

5.

Compilation Scheme:
design a compilation scheme that permits the production of mitigated versions of the algorithms we wish to implement (Sec.
6.1.5
).

6.1.1
Application Specification

Our application is implementing mitigated versions of any quantum algorithm, taking maximal advantage of improvements in hardware. Therefore we want USEM:ORE to be universal, scalable, and efficacious (see Sec.
2.5.2
) for (stochastic) over-rotation errors. This means we will be interested in finding SUPER (see Sec.
2.5.1
) mitigation methods. This requires the analysis of the proxy bias and runtime scaling of mitigation methods (see Sec.
2.3
) in the large circuit and small noise regimes (see Sec.
2.4
).

6.1.2
Hardware Characterisation

For illustrative purposes we consider a piece of quantum hardware for which the native physical operations are rotations generated by any Pauli word operator (
\mathrm{P}_{i}
) of any angle (
\omega
), e.g. we can produce:

\displaystyle\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\frac{\omega}{2}\mathrm{P}_{i}},

for any

\displaystyle\mathrm{P}_{i}=

\displaystyle\bigotimes_{n=1}^{{N_{\mathrm{Q}}}}\vec{\sigma}_{\left[n\right],\left\lfloor\frac{i}{4^{n-1}}\right\rfloor\bmod{4}},

(141)

where
\omega
is the target rotation angle and
{N_{\mathrm{Q}}}
is the number of qubits the physical operation acts on.
\vec{\sigma}_{\left[n\right],j}
is the
j
th single qubit Pauli operator for qubit
n
(
\vec{\sigma}_{\left[n\right],0}=\mathrm{I}
,
\vec{\sigma}_{\left[n\right],1}=\mathrm{X}
,
\vec{\sigma}_{\left[n\right],2}=\mathrm{Y}
, and
\vec{\sigma}_{\left[n\right],3}=\mathrm{Z}
),
\left\lfloor\frac{i}{4^{n-1}}\right\rfloor
is the integer part of the division of
i
by
4^{n-1}
, and
k\bmod 4
is the remainder after dividing
k
by
4
. This means we have
22
22

22

Operators (
\vec{\sigma}_{\left[n\right]}
) acting on higher index (larger
n
) qubits are denoted to the left of those acting on lower index qubits, in our notation for Pauli words, so that the
i
index is independent of the number of qubits (
{N_{\mathrm{Q}}}
) for sufficiently large
{N_{\mathrm{Q}}}
(i.e.
{N_{\mathrm{Q}}}>\log_{4}i
, otherwise
\mathrm{P}_{i}
is not well defined). We neglect to denote leading order identities for notational convenience.

\mathrm{P}_{0}=\mathbb{I}
,
\mathrm{P}_{1}=\mathrm{X}
,
\mathrm{P}_{2}=\mathrm{Y}
,
\mathrm{P}_{3}=\mathrm{Z}
,
\mathrm{P}_{14}=\mathrm{Z}\otimes\mathrm{Y}
, and
\mathrm{P}_{15}=\mathrm{Z}\otimes\mathrm{Z}
(for
{N_{\mathrm{Q}}}\geq 2
, where we suppress leading identities in the tensor product, see Eq. (
141
)). To simplify our notation we define:

\displaystyle\mathrm{P}_{i}=

\displaystyle\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\frac{\uppi}{2}\mathrm{P}_{i}},

\displaystyle\mathrm{S}_{i}=

\displaystyle\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\frac{\uppi}{4}\mathrm{P}_{i}}=\sqrt{\mathrm{P}_{i}},

\displaystyle\mathrm{T}_{i}=

\displaystyle\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\frac{\uppi}{8}\mathrm{P}_{i}}=\left(\mathrm{P}_{i}\right)^{\frac{1}{4}}.

(142)

For this piece of hardware we assume each physical operation is noisy and suffers from (stochastic) over-rotation errors. This means each time we try to implement one of the native physical operations we rotate slightly too far or not far enough and the amount of over-rotation for each implementation is random (see Sec.
3.5
). We assume that the over-rotations follow a Gaussian distribution (with mean
\mu_{\mathrm{ORE}}
and standard deviation
\sigma_{\mathrm{ORE}}
) and therefore a noisy gate can be modelled by:

\begin{matrix}\scalebox{0.1}{\begin{picture}\put(0.0,0.0){\includegraphics[width,page]{gateM.pdf}}\put(0.40119,0.42637){\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0)[lt]{\begin{minipage}[335.53992pt]\raggedright\@add@raggedright\end{minipage}}}\put(0.27218,0.60885){\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0)[lt]{\begin{minipage}[391.46324pt]\raggedright\vskip 65.0pt-\heightof{\scalebox{8}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}$\displaystyle{\mathrm{P}_{i}}_{{\mathrm{}}}^{\phantom{\dagger}}$}}}/2\hskip 72.0pt\scalebox{8}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}$\displaystyle{\mathrm{P}_{i}}_{{\mathrm{}}}^{\phantom{\dagger}}$}}\@add@raggedright\end{minipage}}}\put(0.27218,0.63959){\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0)[lt]{\smash{\begin{tabular}[t]{l}\end{tabular}}}}\put(0.77218,0.10885){\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0)[lt]{\begin{minipage}[391.46324pt]\raggedright\vskip-17.07182pt\par\noindent\hskip 8.5359pt\scalebox{5}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}$\displaystyle{\mathrm{}}$}}\@add@raggedright\end{minipage}}}\put(0.0,0.0){\includegraphics[width,page]{gateM.pdf}}\end{picture}}\end{matrix}&\text{ with probability }p,\end{cases}

(143)

where
\phi
is the noise amplitude of the rotational (component of the) error channel and
p
is the noise amplitude of the stochastic (component of the) error channel:

\displaystyle\phi=

\displaystyle\mu_{\mathrm{ORE}},

\displaystyle p=

\displaystyle\frac{1-\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{\sigma_{\mathrm{ORE}}^{2}}{2}}}{2}=\frac{\sigma_{\mathrm{ORE}}^{2}}{4}+\mathcal{O}_{0}\left(\sigma_{\mathrm{ORE}}^{4}\right).

(144)

The noise channels have eigenvalues of the form (Eq. (
77
) of Sec.
3.5
):

\displaystyle\lambda_{\mathcal{E}_{{\mathrm{}}{\mathrm{}}},0}\left(p,\phi\right)=

\displaystyle 1,

\displaystyle\lambda_{\mathcal{E}_{{\mathrm{}}{\mathrm{}}},1}\left(p,\phi\right)=

\displaystyle\left(1-2p\right)\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\phi},

\displaystyle\lambda_{\mathcal{E}_{{\mathrm{}}{\mathrm{}}},2}\left(p,\phi\right)=

\displaystyle\left(1-2p\right)\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\phi}.

We will assume there can be different noise amplitudes (
\phi
and
p
) for each physical operation that we mitigate so we benefit from using a local mitigation method.

6.1.3
Mitigation Methods

There are a variety of local mitigation methods that we could choose from (see Table
1
of Sec.
4.6
). Since our noise has both stochastic and rotational components (see Eq. (
143
)) it seems reasonable to choose a method that performs well for both types of noise (see Tables
3
and
5
of Secs
5.1
and
5.2
, respectively). The obvious choice is CLM but CHILM is much more efficient for rotational errors. So we propose C(HI)LM, that is, if we have access to hidden inverse (for a particular physical operation) we will use CHILM but if not we will settle for CLM. We will use the version designed to mitigate rotation errors (but modify the mitigation coefficients to account for the stochastic contribution). Since the noise channels for stochastic over-rotation errors and rotational errors are the same (c.f. Secs
3.4
and
3.5
).

This means we find a linear combination of noisy gate variants that is equivalent to the noise-free target gate (Eq. (
124
) of Sec.
4.5
):

\displaystyle\begin{matrix}\scalebox{0.1}{\begin{picture}\put(0.0,0.0){\includegraphics[width,page]{gateM.pdf}}\put(0.40119,0.42637){\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0)[lt]{\begin{minipage}[335.53992pt]\raggedright\@add@raggedright\end{minipage}}}\put(0.27218,0.60885){\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0)[lt]{\begin{minipage}[391.46324pt]\raggedright\vskip 65.0pt-\heightof{\scalebox{8}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}$\displaystyle{U}_{{\mathrm{}}}^{\phantom{\dagger}}$}}}/2\hskip 72.0pt\scalebox{8}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}$\displaystyle{U}_{{\mathrm{}}}^{\phantom{\dagger}}$}}\@add@raggedright\end{minipage}}}\put(0.27218,0.63959){\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0)[lt]{\smash{\begin{tabular}[t]{l}\noindent\hskip 42.67912pt\scalebox{8}{$\displaystyle\sim$}\end{tabular}}}}\put(0.77218,0.10885){\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0)[lt]{\begin{minipage}[391.46324pt]\raggedright\vskip-17.07182pt\par\noindent\hskip 8.5359pt\scalebox{5}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}$\displaystyle{\mathrm{M}}$}}\@add@raggedright\end{minipage}}}\put(0.0,0.0){\includegraphics[width,page]{gateM.pdf}}\end{picture}}\end{matrix}\penalty\
