(151)

We assume that the stochastic noise level remain unchanged, i.e.
p_{j}=p
for both methods.

Now the eigenvalues associated with the transfer matrices (
\hat{T}_{\mathcal{E}_{{\mathrm{}};{\mathrm{}j}}}
) that characterise the effect of the (amplified) error channels (
\mathcal{E}_{{\mathrm{}};{\mathrm{}j}}
) are given by (see Eq. (
77
)):

\displaystyle\lambda_{j,0}=

\displaystyle\lambda_{0}\left(p_{j},\phi_{j}\right)=1,

\displaystyle\lambda_{j,1}=

\displaystyle\lambda_{1}\left(p_{j},\phi_{j}\right)=\left(1-2p_{j}\right)\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\phi_{j}},

(152)

\displaystyle\lambda_{j,2}=

\displaystyle\lambda_{2}\left(p_{j},\phi_{j}\right)=\left(1-2p_{j}\right)\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\phi_{j}}.

(153)

In order to perform an unbiased mitigation we must determine mitigation coefficients that satisfy (see Eq. (
126
) of Sec.
4.5.1
):

\displaystyle\sum_{j=0}^{N_{\mathrm{L},\mathrm{C}}}c_{\mathrm{L};j}\lambda_{j,k}=1,

for (integer)
0\leq k\leq 2
, i.e. we require:

\displaystyle\begin{cases}c_{\mathrm{L};0}=\frac{\cos\phi}{1-2p},\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ c_{\mathrm{L};1}=\frac{1-\cos\phi-2p-\sin\phi}{2\left(1-2p\right)},\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ c_{\mathrm{L};2}=\frac{1-\cos\phi-2p+\sin\phi}{2\left(1-2p\right)},&\text{ for CLM}\\
c_{\mathrm{L};0}=\frac{1}{2},\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ c_{\mathrm{L};1}=\frac{1-\left(\cos\phi\right)^{-1}-2p}{2\left(1-2p\right)},\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ c_{\mathrm{L};2}=\frac{1}{2\left(1-2p\right)\cos\phi},&\text{ for CHILM},\end{cases}

(154)

as the coefficients of our mitigation methods.

6.1.4
Gate Set

We desire a universal (see Sec.
2.5.2
) mitigation strategy so we require a gate set that includes: a collection of gates (or physical operations) that is sufficient to implement any algorithm we wish to apply and, also, all the gates (or physical operations) needed to mitigate that collection of gates. There are multiple different gate sets we could choose that would satisfy our criteria. We choose the following (Eq. (
141
)):

\displaystyle\mathrm{T}=\mathrm{T}_{3}=

\displaystyle\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\frac{\uppi}{8}\mathrm{Z}},

\displaystyle\mathrm{S}=\mathrm{S}_{3}=

\displaystyle\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\frac{\uppi}{4}\mathrm{Z}},

\displaystyle\mathrm{Z}=\mathrm{P}_{3}=

\displaystyle\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\frac{\uppi}{2}\mathrm{Z}},

(155)

\displaystyle\mathrm{S}_{1}=

\displaystyle\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\frac{\uppi}{4}\mathrm{X}},

\displaystyle\mathrm{X}=\mathrm{P}_{1}=

\displaystyle\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\frac{\uppi}{2}\mathrm{X}},

(156)

\displaystyle\mathrm{S}_{15}=

\displaystyle\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\frac{\uppi}{4}\mathrm{Z}\otimes\mathrm{Z}},

(157)

i.e. we choose the standard T, S, and Pauli Z gate (Eq. (
155
)); the Pauli X gate and its square root (
\mathrm{S}_{1}=\sqrt{\mathrm{X}}
, Eq. (
156
)); and one two-qubit gate:
\mathrm{S}_{15}
. We require a copy of each of the single qubit gates for each of the qubits that we wish to act on and a copy of the two qubit gate for each pair of qubits we wish to act on. We could do without
\mathrm{X}
,
\mathrm{Z}
, and/or
\mathrm{S}
if we wished to, since
\mathrm{T}^{2}=\mathrm{S}
,
\mathrm{S}^{2}=\mathrm{Z}
, and
\mathrm{S}_{1}^{2}=\mathrm{X}
.

This gate set is also sufficient for our mitigation since the amplification channels (see Eqs (
148
) and (
149
)) we need for C(HI)LM can be decomposed as:

\displaystyle\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\frac{\uppi}{4}\mathrm{P}_{3}}=

\displaystyle\mathrm{S}_{3}=\mathrm{S},

\displaystyle\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\frac{\uppi}{2}\mathrm{P}_{3}}=

\displaystyle\mathrm{P}_{3}=\mathrm{Z},

\displaystyle\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\frac{3\uppi}{4}\mathrm{P}_{3}}=

\displaystyle\mathrm{S}\mathrm{Z},

(158)

\displaystyle\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\frac{\uppi}{4}\mathrm{P}_{1}}=

\displaystyle\mathrm{S}_{1},

\displaystyle\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\frac{\uppi}{2}\mathrm{P}_{1}}=

\displaystyle\mathrm{P}_{1}=\mathrm{X},

\displaystyle\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\frac{3\uppi}{4}\mathrm{P}_{1}}=

\displaystyle\mathrm{S}_{1}\mathrm{X}

(159)

\displaystyle\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\frac{\uppi}{4}\mathrm{P}_{15}}=

\displaystyle\mathrm{S}_{15},

\displaystyle\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\frac{\uppi}{2}\mathrm{P}_{15}}=

\displaystyle\mathrm{P}_{15}=\mathrm{Z}\otimes\mathrm{Z},

\displaystyle\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\frac{3\uppi}{4}\mathrm{P}_{15}}=

\displaystyle\mathrm{S}_{15}\left(\mathrm{Z}\otimes\mathrm{Z}\right),

(160)

where each row of operators can be used to mitigate (all the gates in) the corresponding row of our gate set (Eqs (
155
), (
156
), and (
157
), see Sec.
6.1.3
).

If we wish to mitigate any gate using CHILM we do not require the
\mathrm{S}_{i}
gates for the mitigation but we do require the hidden inverse implementation of the gate we wish to mitigate, which is the same as the original gate except that the rotational error of the noise channel has its sign reversed (see Secs
4.2.2
and
6.1.2
), e.g. if we have the noisy gate (Eq. (

So we need to add the hidden inverse implementation, of any gate we wish to mitigate using CHILM, to our gate set (Eqs (
155
), (
156
), and (
157
)), which could double the number of gates in our gate set.

6.1.5
Compilation Scheme

The compilation scheme is simple. We produce a quantum circuit that is equivalent to the algorithm we wish to implement (how we go about doing this is beyond the scope of this work). Then we decompose every gate in this circuit in terms of the gates within our gate set (Eqs (
155
), (
156
), and (
157
)). If we wish to be more efficient we can combine the previous two steps and optimise the compilation scheme in order to minimise the circuit length and/or runtime scaling. Once we have a circuit that implements our algorithm, written in terms of gates from our gate set, we simply replace every gate with its mitigated version (Eq. (
145
)). This procedure means we end up with a mitigated circuit that only requires us to make use of gates that we can implement on our hardware (see Secs
6.1.3
and
6.1.4
), and this mitigated circuit is the circuit we will run on our quantum hardware to implement our algorithm.

6.1.6
Additional Requirements

Since we desire a scalable mitigation strategy we require the noise level to be independent of circuit length (see Sec.
2.5.1
). This means some level of hardware tuning, i.e. improving gate implementation to reduce errors; or error correction, i.e. actively correcting the errors that occur during the application of logical gates, might be required in order to permit the mitigation of larger circuits.

6.2
Method Certification

We can certify our mitigation methods (CLM and CHILM, see Sec.
6.1.3
) with regards to the criteria introduced in Sec.
2.5.1
, but first we need to calculate the quantitative metrics (Sec.
2.3
).

6.2.1
Quantitative Mitigation Metrics

The proxy bias (Sec.
2.3.1
) is zero for both our methods (
\varepsilon_{\scriptscriptstyle\heartsuit;\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{M}}=0
) since they are (intrinsically) unbiased and if our characterisation is perfect the mitigated gates will be error free.

To calculate the runtime scaling (Sec.
2.3.4
) we must first calculate the (local) sampling cost
25
25

25

For simplicity we assume every gate suffers from the same noise amplitude (i.e. we have a uniform noise model). This simplification can be easily relaxed by replacing the local sampling cost with the geometric mean of the sampling costs for each gate, but, as long as the noise amplitudes are all of similar orders of magnitude, our derivation is relevant.
(see Eqs (
32

131

\displaystyle C_{\mathrm{L}}=

\displaystyle\sum_{j=0}^{2}\left|c_{\mathrm{L};j}\right|=\begin{cases}\begin{cases}\frac{\cos\phi+\left|\sin\phi\right|}{1-2p}&\text{ for }\left|\phi\right|>2p,\\
\frac{2\cos\phi-1+2p}{1-2p}&\text{ for }\left|\phi\right|<2p,\\
\end{cases}&\text{ for CLM}\\
\frac{1}{\left(1-2p\right)\cos\phi},&\text{ for CHILM},\end{cases}

\displaystyle F_{{\mathrm{L,L}}{\mathrm{}}}=

\displaystyle\frac{\sum_{j=0}^{2}\left|c_{\mathrm{L};j}\right|\tau_{\mathrm{E},\mathrm{L},j}}{C_{\mathrm{L}}\tau_{\mathrm{E},\mathrm{L}}}=\begin{cases}\begin{cases}\frac{3\cos\phi-1+2p+5\left|\sin\phi\right|}{2\left(\cos\phi+\left|\sin\phi\right|\right)}&\text{ for }\phi<-2p,\\
\frac{7\cos\phi-5+10p-\sin\phi}{2\left(2\cos\phi-2+4p\right)}&\text{ for }\left|\phi\right|<2p,\\

\frac{3-\left(1-2p\right)\cos\phi}{2},&\text{ for CHILM},\end{cases}

S_{\mathrm{Gl}}=C_{\mathrm{L}}^{2N_{\mathrm{G}}}F_{{\mathrm{L,L}}{\mathrm{}}}
, is not particularly informative so we will instead expand it in the small noise regime (
e_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{SN}},e_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{RE}}\ll 1

\displaystyle S_{\mathrm{Gl}}=

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\begin{cases}1+2\left(1+\frac{1}{4N_{\mathrm{G}}}\right)e_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{SN}}+2\left(1+\frac{3}{4N_{\mathrm{G}}}\right)e_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{RE}}&\text{ for }\phi<-2p,\\

\end{cases}&\text{ for CLM}\\
1+2\left(1+\frac{1}{4N_{\mathrm{G}}}\right)e_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{SN}}+\left(1+\frac{1}{4N_{\mathrm{G}}}\right)\frac{e_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{RE}}^{2}}{N_{\mathrm{G}}},&\text{ for CHILM},\end{cases}

(166)

(where we have neglected terms of order
\mathcal{O}_{\mathrm{S}}\left(e_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{SN}}^{2},e_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{SN}}e_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{RE}},e_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{RE}}^{2}\right)
for CLM and those of order
\mathcal{O}_{\mathrm{S}}\left(e_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{SN}}^{2},e_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{SN}}e_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{RE}}^{2},e_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{RE}}^{4}\right)
for CHILM). In the large circuit regime (
N_{\mathrm{G}}\gg 1
) the runtime scaling is given by:

\displaystyle S_{\mathrm{Gl}}=

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\begin{cases}\mathrm{e}^{2\left(e_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{SN}}+e_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{RE}}\right)}\left(1+\mathcal{O}_{\mathrm{L}}\left(\frac{e_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{SN}}}{N_{\mathrm{G}}},\frac{e_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{SN}}^{2}}{N_{\mathrm{G}}},\frac{e_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{RE}}}{N_{\mathrm{G}}},\frac{e_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{RE}}^{2}}{N_{\mathrm{G}}}\right)\right)&\text{ for }e_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{RE}}>e_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{SN}},\\
\mathrm{e}^{4e_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{SN}}}\left(1+\mathcal{O}_{\mathrm{L}}\left(\frac{e_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{SN}}}{N_{\mathrm{G}}},\frac{e_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{RE}}}{N_{\mathrm{G}}},\frac{e_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{RE}}^{2}}{N_{\mathrm{G}}}\right)\right)&\text{ for }e_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{RE}}<e_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{SN}},\end{cases}&\text{ for CLM}\\
\mathrm{e}^{2e_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{SN}}}\left(1+\mathcal{O}_{\mathrm{L}}\left(\frac{e_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{SN}}}{N_{\mathrm{G}}},\frac{e_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{SN}}^{2}}{N_{\mathrm{G}}},\frac{e_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{RE}}^{2}}{N_{\mathrm{G}}}\right)\right),&\text{ for CHILM}.\end{cases}

(167)

Here
N_{\mathrm{G}}
is the number of mitigated gates and we have used (Eqs (
55
) and (
68
)):

\displaystyle e_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{SN}}=

\displaystyle 2pN_{\mathrm{G}},

\displaystyle e_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{RE}}=

\displaystyle N_{\mathrm{G}}\left|\phi\right|.

(168)

Interestingly, Eq. (
167
) means, if we have access to hidden inverses, the rotational noise level is largely irrelevant in the large circuit regime (provided the rotational noise level is independent of
N_{\mathrm{G}}
).

Determining the noise boundary (see Sec.
2.3.5
) is now just a case of inverting the large-circuit-regime runtime equation (Eq. (
167
)) in the large circuit limit (
N_{\mathrm{G}}\rightarrow\infty
). We find:

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\begin{cases}e_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{B}}=\frac{\ln S_{\pluscirc}}{2}&\text{ for }e_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{RE}}>e_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{SN}},\\
e_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{SN},\mathrm{B}}=\frac{\ln S_{\pluscirc}}{4}&\text{ for }e_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{RE}}<e_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{SN}},\end{cases}&\text{ for CLM}\\
e_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{SN},\mathrm{B}}=\frac{\ln S_{\pluscirc}}{2},&\text{ for CHILM},\end{cases}

(169)

where
e_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{B}}
is the total noise boundary and
e_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{SN},\mathrm{B}}
is the noise boundary for stochastic noise. So, if we have a tolerated runtime scaling of
S_{\pluscirc}
we can mitigate circuits with any (finite) noise levels (
e_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{SN}}
and
e_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{RE}}
) such that:

\displaystyle\begin{cases}e_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{RE}}<e_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{SN}}<e_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{SN},\mathrm{B}}\penalty\ \text{ or }\penalty\ \left(e_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{RE}}+e_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{SN}}<e_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{B}}\penalty\ \text{ and }e_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{RE}}>e_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{SN}}\right),&\text{ with CLM}\\
e_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{SN}}<e_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{SN},\mathrm{B}}\text{ and }e_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{RE}}\text{ is finite},&\text{ with CHILM},\end{cases}

(170)

in the large circuit regime. So CHILM offers a distinct advantage, particularly if
e_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{RE}}
is the most significant contribution to the noise.

6.2.2
Hardware Robustness

The test for hardware robustness is relatively simple (see Sec.
2.5.1
). We assume that every gate has independent (i.e. non uniform) noise, that we assume has been well-characterised. Then we calculate the proxy bias (which is still zero) and runtime scaling (Sec.
2.3.4
) of mitigation using these noisy gates for variant generation rather than the noise-free amplification gates (Eqs (
150
) and (
151
)) and hidden inverse procedures (Eq. (
161
)) that we considered in Secs
6.2
,
6.1.4
, and
6.1.3
, which did not add any uncontrolled errors into our circuit beyond those present in the original noisy gate. If the runtime scaling does not change (to leading order) then the method is robust for our hardware.

The two mitigation protocols we implement are given by (see Eqs (
142
), (
145
), (
148
), and (
150
)):

(171)

where the first decomposition is CLM and the second CHILM. Now, we assume our noisy gate
\tilde{U}_{{\mathrm{}}{\mathrm{}}}
has rotational and stochastic noise amplitudes
\phi
and
p
, respectively (see Eq. (
143
)), as before. However, we also assume that our mitigation gates and the hidden inverse procedure are noisy, so we have (see Eq. (
73
)):

\displaystyle\begin{matrix}\scalebox{0.1}{\begin{picture}\put(0.0,0.0){\includegraphics[width,page]{gateM.pdf}}\put(0.40119,0.42637){\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0)[lt]{\begin{minipage}[335.53992pt]\raggedright\@add@raggedright\end{minipage}}}\put(0.27218,0.60885){\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0)[lt]{\begin{minipage}[391.46324pt]\raggedright\vskip 65.0pt-\heightof{\scalebox{8}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}$\displaystyle{U}_{{\mathrm{}}}^{\phantom{\dagger}}$}}}/2\hskip 72.0pt\scalebox{8}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}$\displaystyle{U}_{{\mathrm{}}}^{\phantom{\dagger}}$}}\@add@raggedright\end{minipage}}}\put(0.27218,0.63959){\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0)[lt]{\smash{\begin{tabular}[t]{l}\noindent\hskip 42.67912pt\scalebox{8}{$\displaystyle\sim$}\end{tabular}}}}\put(0.77218,0.10885){\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0)[lt]{\begin{minipage}[391.46324pt]\raggedright\vskip-17.07182pt\par\noindent\hskip 8.5359pt\scalebox{5}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}$\displaystyle{\mathrm{}}$}}\@add@raggedright\end{minipage}}}\put(0.0,0.0){\includegraphics[width,page]{gateM.pdf}}\end{picture}}\end{matrix}\penalty\ =\penalty\

(173)

\tilde{\mathrm{\mathrm{P}_{i}}}_{{\mathrm{}}{\mathrm{}}}
;
\tilde{\mathrm{\mathrm{S}_{i}}}_{{\mathrm{}}{\mathrm{}}}
; and
\tilde{U}_{{\mathrm{}};{\mathrm{HI}}}
are noisy, with noise amplitudes
z_{\mathrm{P}}\phi
and
y_{\mathrm{P}}p
;
z_{\mathrm{S}}\phi
and
y_{\mathrm{S}}p
; and
-z_{\mathrm{HI}}\phi
(where
z_{\mathrm{HI}}>0
otherwise the hidden inverse failed) and
y_{\mathrm{HI}}p
, respectively. This means the rotational noise amplitudes of our noisy variants are given by:

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\phi_{0}=\phi,\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \phi_{1}=\left(1+z_{\mathrm{S}}\right)\phi+\frac{\uppi}{2},\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \phi_{2}=\left(1+z_{\mathrm{S}}+z_{\mathrm{P}}\right)\phi+\frac{3\uppi}{2},&\text{ for CLM}\\
\phi_{0}=\phi,\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \phi_{1}=\left(1+z_{\mathrm{P}}\right)\phi+\uppi,\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \phi_{2}=-z_{\mathrm{HI}}\phi,&\text{ for CHILM},\end{cases}

(174)

(where the gates are numbered from top to bottom in Eq. (
171
)) and their stochastic noise amplitudes are:

\displaystyle\begin{cases}p_{0}=p,\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ p_{1}=\left(1+y_{\mathrm{S}}\right)p,\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ p_{2}=\left(1+y_{\mathrm{S}}+y_{\mathrm{P}}\right)p,&\text{ for CLM}\\
p_{0}=p,\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ p_{1}=\left(1+y_{\mathrm{P}}\right)p,\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ p_{2}=y_{\mathrm{HI}}p,&\text{ for CHILM}.\end{cases}

(175)

72.0pt\scalebox{8}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}$\displaystyle{}_{{\mathrm{}}}^{\phantom{\dagger}}$}}\@add@raggedright\end{minipage}}}\put(0.27218,0.63959){\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0)[lt]{\smash{\begin{tabular}[t]{l}\end{tabular}}}}\put(0.77219,0.10885){\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0)[lt]{\begin{minipage}[391.46324pt]\raggedright\vskip-17.07182pt\par\noindent\hskip 8.5359pt\scalebox{5}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}$\displaystyle{\mathrm{}}$}}\@add@raggedright\end{minipage}}}\end{picture}}\end{matrix}&\text{ with probability }1-p_{j}\\
\begin{matrix}\scalebox{0.1}{\begin{picture}\put(0.0,0.0){\includegraphics[width,page]{gateM.pdf}}\put(0.40119,0.42637){\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0)[lt]{\begin{minipage}[335.53992pt]\raggedright\@add@raggedright\end{minipage}}}\put(0.27218,0.60885){\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0)[lt]{\begin{minipage}[391.46324pt]\raggedright\vskip 65.0pt-\heightof{\scalebox{8}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}$\displaystyle{\mathrm{P}_{i}}_{{\mathrm{}}}^{\phantom{\dagger}}$}}}/2\hskip 72.0pt\scalebox{8}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}$\displaystyle{\mathrm{P}_{i}}_{{\mathrm{}}}^{\phantom{\dagger}}$}}\@add@raggedright\end{minipage}}}\put(0.27218,0.63959){\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0)[lt]{\smash{\begin{tabular}[t]{l}\end{tabular}}}}\put(0.77218,0.10885){\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0)[lt]{\begin{minipage}[391.46324pt]\raggedright\vskip-17.07182pt\par\noindent\hskip 8.5359pt\scalebox{5}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}$\displaystyle{\mathrm{}}$}}\@add@raggedright\end{minipage}}}\put(0.0,0.0){\includegraphics[width,page]{gateM.pdf}}\end{picture}}\end{matrix}&\text{ with probability }p_{j},\end{cases}

(176)

is the
j
th gate variant used to mitigate the noisy gate
\tilde{U}_{{\mathrm{}}{\mathrm{}}}
.

Now the eigenvalues associated with the transfer matrices (
\hat{T}_{\mathcal{E}_{{\mathrm{}};{\mathrm{}j}}}
) that characterise the effect of the (amplified) error channels (
\mathcal{E}_{{\mathrm{}};{\mathrm{}j}}
) are given by Eq. (
152
) as before and we must solve Eq. (
126
) to find our coefficients (where
c_{\mathrm{L};j}=\tilde{c}_{\mathrm{C};j}
for CLM and
c_{\mathrm{L};j}=\tilde{c}_{\mathrm{CHI};j}
for CHILM). The resulting coefficients are too cumbersome to write in full here but we can examine their first order expansions (in the noise amplitudes), i.e. we neglect terms of order
\mathcal{O}_{0}\left(p^{2},p\phi,\phi^{2}\right)
. We also make the assumption that all the noise amplitudes have the same order of magnitude, i.e.
\left(1+z_{\mathrm{S}}+z_{\mathrm{P}}+z_{\mathrm{HI}}\right)\phi,\left(1+y_{\mathrm{S}}+y_{\mathrm{P}}+y_{\mathrm{HI}}\right)p\ll 1
.

The coefficients for CLM (to leading order) are:

\displaystyle\tilde{c}_{\mathrm{C};0}=

\displaystyle 1+2p,

\displaystyle\tilde{c}_{\mathrm{C};1}=

\displaystyle-p-\frac{\phi}{2},

\displaystyle\tilde{c}_{\mathrm{C};2}=

\displaystyle-p+\frac{\phi}{2},

(177)

these are independent of
y_{\mathrm{S}},z_{\mathrm{S}},y_{\mathrm{P}},z_{\mathrm{P}}
so CLM is robust to using noisy amplification gates since the runtime scaling is a function of the coefficients in Eq. (
177
) (to leading order).

The coefficients for CHILM (to leading order) are:

\displaystyle\tilde{c}_{\mathrm{CHI};0}=

\displaystyle\frac{z_{\mathrm{HI}}}{1+z_{\mathrm{HI}}}+\frac{\left(1+z_{\mathrm{HI}}\right)\left(z_{\mathrm{HI}}-y_{\mathrm{HI}}\right)-\left(z_{\mathrm{HI}}+y_{\mathrm{HI}}\right)z_{\mathrm{P}}}{\left(1+z_{\mathrm{HI}}\right)^{2}}p+\frac{z_{\mathrm{HI}}\left(z_{\mathrm{HI}}-3z_{\mathrm{P}}-1\right)}{12\left(1+z_{\mathrm{HI}}\right)}\phi^{2},

\displaystyle\tilde{c}_{\mathrm{CHI};1}=

\displaystyle-\frac{z_{\mathrm{HI}}+y_{\mathrm{HI}}}{1+z_{\mathrm{HI}}}p-\frac{z_{\mathrm{HI}}}{4}\phi^{2},

\displaystyle\tilde{c}_{\mathrm{CHI};2}=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{1+z_{\mathrm{HI}}}+\frac{2y_{\mathrm{HI}}\left(1+z_{\mathrm{HI}}\right)+\left(z_{\mathrm{HI}}+y_{\mathrm{HI}}\right)z_{\mathrm{P}}}{\left(1+z_{\mathrm{HI}}\right)^{2}}p+\frac{z_{\mathrm{HI}}\left(2z_{\mathrm{HI}}+3z_{\mathrm{P}}+4\right)}{12\left(1+z_{\mathrm{HI}}\right)}\phi^{2},

(178)

so these do depend on
y_{\mathrm{HI}},z_{\mathrm{HI}}
and
z_{\mathrm{P}}
. Thus we must calculate the runtime scaling explicitly to determine if CHILM is robust. CHILM’s runtime scaling is given by:

\displaystyle S_{\mathrm{Gl}}=

\displaystyle 1+2\left(1+\frac{1}{4N_{\mathrm{G}}}\right)\frac{y_{\mathrm{HI}}+z_{\mathrm{HI}}}{1+z_{\mathrm{HI}}}e_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{SN}}+\left(1+\frac{1}{4N_{\mathrm{G}}}\right)z_{\mathrm{HI}}\frac{e_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{RE}}^{2}}{N_{\mathrm{G}}}+\mathcal{O}_{\mathrm{S}}\left(e_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{SN}}^{2},e_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{SN}}e_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{RE}}^{2},e_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{RE}}^{4}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathrm{e}^{2\frac{y_{\mathrm{HI}}+z_{\mathrm{HI}}}{1+z_{\mathrm{HI}}}e_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{SN}}}\left(1+\mathcal{O}_{\mathrm{L}}\left(\frac{e_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{SN}}}{N_{\mathrm{G}}},\frac{e_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{SN}}^{2}}{N_{\mathrm{G}}},\frac{e_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{RE}}^{2}}{N_{\mathrm{G}}}\right)\right),

(179)

and the noise boundary is given by:

\displaystyle e_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{SN},\mathrm{B}}=\frac{1+z_{\mathrm{HI}}}{y_{\mathrm{HI}}+z_{\mathrm{HI}}}\frac{\ln S_{\pluscirc}}{2}.

(180)

So, though CHILM is not robust, it is quasi-robust (see Sec.
2.5.1
) since the orders of the most significant order terms for its noise boundary and runtime scaling are independent of the
y_{\mathrm{HI}},z_{\mathrm{HI}}
and
z_{\mathrm{P}}
, even though the values of the coefficients are not. The runtime scaling and noise boundary are robust to noise in the custom channel (i.e. the values of
z_{\mathrm{P}}
and
y_{\mathrm{P}}
) and, if
y_{\mathrm{HI}}<1
, using imperfect hidden inverses could actually perform better than using noise-free hidden inverses.

6.2.3
Method Certification

We can use our metrics to certify our mitigation methods (see Sec.
2.5.1
).

1.

Scalable
: Both methods are scalable since their runtime scaling in the large circuit regime depends only on the noise level (
e_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{SN}}
and/or
e_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{RE}}
) and not the number of gates (
N_{\mathrm{G}}
, see Eq. (
167
)). However, CHILM scales better than CLM as CHILM’s runtime is independent of the rotational noise level (
e_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{RE}}
) in the large circuit limit.

2.

Unbounded
(in principle): The noise boundary (see Eq. (
169
)) grows as the logarithm of the tolerated runtime scaling (
S_{\pluscirc}
) for both methods. So we can mitigate arbitrary noise levels with either methods, if we are willing to wait long enough. Thus both methods are unbounded but CHILM is independent of the rotational noise level, so, again, it has a slight edge.

3.

Precise
: Since both methods are unbiased they are both precise and can be used to reduce the bias to arbitrarily small levels, if we are willing to wait long enough.

4.

Efficient
: Both methods are also efficient since the runtime scaling in the small noise limit (
e_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{SN}},e_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{RE}}\rightarrow 0
) is unity (see Eq. (
166
)). So they can make good use of any reduction in noise level. However, CHILM is more efficient than CLM since it is independent of
e_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{RE}}
to first order.

5.

Robust
(see Sec.
6.2.2
): CLM is robust for our hardware since noise in the mitigation gates does not change the runtime cost to leading order in the large circuit or small noise regime provided the noise is characterised and the same order of magnitude as that of the gate being mitigated. CHILM is quasi-robust (see Sec.
2.5.1
) since noise in the hidden inverse procedure can changes the effective size of the stochastic noise level but it is robust to noise in the amplification channel.

So CLM can be certified SUPER (scalable, unbounded, precise, efficient, and robust) and CHILM is a SUPEqR (scalable, unbounded, precise, efficient, and quasi-robust) mitigation method. Though CHILM is not robust it does scale better than CLM in general. In the next section we shall certify the mitigation strategy as a whole.

6.3
Strategy Certification

We can now certify our full mitigation strategy according to the criteria in Sec.
2.5.2
.

1.

Universal
: We have demonstrated (see Sec.
6.1.3
) that we can mitigate any gate within our gate set (Eqs (
155
), (
156
), and (
157
)) using only gates that are already within the gate set to perform the mitigation (see Eqs (
158
), (
159
), and (
160
) of Sec.
6.1.4
, the exception being for CHILM where we also require hidden inverse implementations of the gate to be mitigated), if all the gates suffer from stochastic over-rotation errors (see Sec.
6.2.2
). Now, all that remains is to show that our gate set is universal. To do this we will provide decompositions of the gates within a universally recognised universal gate set (T-Z45, H-Hadamard, CX-Controlled Not, e.g. see Refs
[
37
,
38
]
) in terms of our (universally mitigatable) gate set (Eqs (
155
), (
156
), and (
157
)):

\displaystyle\mathrm{T}=

\displaystyle\mathrm{T}_{3},

\displaystyle\mathrm{CZ}=

\displaystyle\mathrm{e}^{-3\mathrm{i}\frac{\uppi}{4}}\left(\mathrm{S}_{3}\mathrm{Z}\otimes\mathrm{S}_{3}\mathrm{Z}\right)\mathrm{S}_{15},

\displaystyle\mathrm{H}=

\displaystyle\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\frac{\uppi}{2}}\mathrm{S}_{1}\mathrm{S}_{3}\mathrm{S}_{1},

\displaystyle\mathrm{CX}=

\displaystyle\left(\mathrm{I}\otimes\mathrm{H}\right)\mathrm{CZ}\left(\mathrm{I}\otimes\mathrm{H}\right).

(181)

2.

Scalable
: Since C(HI)LM is scalable (see Sec.
6.2.3
) even when using imperfect custom channels and hidden inverses to effect the mitigation (see Sec.
6.2.2
) the full mitigation strategy is scalable.

3.

Efficacious
: C(HI)LM is SUPE(q)R (see Sec.
6.2.3
), so it is unbounded, precise, efficient and quasi-robust for hardware suffering from over-rotation errors (see Sec.
6.2.2
) therefore it is efficacious (the quasi-robustness ensures that the method certifications are still relevant for the noisy hardware we consider).

Thus our mitigation strategy (USEM:ORE) can be certified USEful for hardware suffering from stochastic over-rotation errors and we can successfully conclude our certification.

7
Concluding Remarks

In this work, we introduced a unified framework for the classification and certification of quantum error mitigation (QEM) methods. Our approach combines quantitative metrics – such as proxy bias, runtime scaling, and noise boundaries – with qualitative criteria including scalability, robustness, and efficiency. These tools enable a systematic evaluation of linear mitigation methods and their applicability across different noise models.

Through our taxonomy and case studies, we demonstrated how the proposed certification scheme can be applied to compare and design mitigation strategies tailored to specific quantum hardware and noise profiles.

7.1
Further Work

Future efforts should aim to:

(i)

Extend the certification framework to non-linear and hybrid error mitigation methods as well as additional noise models (e.g. amplitude damping
[
2
]
). In Ref.
[
1
]
we consider virtual distillation – a non-linear mitigation method
[
11
,
12
,
13
]
– applied to circuits suffering from global depolarising noise and calculate the relevant quantitative metrics (runtime scaling, proxy bias, and noise boundary).

(ii)

Incorporate realistic experimental imperfections into the certification process and make use of real noise characterisation data obtained by tomography.

(iii)

Explore integration with other approaches to noise suppression, such as error correction and/or hardware tuning (e.g. incorporate metrics such as energy consumption
[
10
]
).

A promising direction is the development of automated standardised certification pipelines combining theoretical metrics and experimental data to enable adaptive mitigation strategies in near-term quantum devices.

Appendix A
Glossary of Terms

Here we present a glossary of terms that we use in this work in alphabetical order, with references to where they are used in the main text.

Term

Description

Reference

Area Scale Factor (
F_{{\mathrm{Gl,A}}{\mathrm{}}}
)

The product of the length and width scale factors, representing the resource overhead of mitigation per run of the circuit.

Sec.
2.3.4

Asynchronous Mitigation (AM)

Mitigation where different gates can be amplified by different amounts in each circuit variant.

Sec.
4.1

Bias (
\varepsilon
)

Deviation of the measured observable expectation value from the true noiseless value.

Sec.
2.1

Certification

Process of assessing a mitigation method using predefined quantitative and qualitative metrics.

Sec.
2.5

Circuit Variant

One of a selection of noisy implementations of a noise-free target circuit.

Sec.
4.2

CPTP

Completely Positive and Trace Preserving

Ref.
[
39
]

Custom Channel (C)

A tailored noise channel inserted after a gate to amplify or modify its noise characteristics.

Sec.
4.2.3

Efficacious

Mitigation strategy certification criteria for when all mitigation methods within the strategy are SUPER or SUPEqR.

Sec.
2.5.2

Efficient

Property of a mitigation method with unit runtime scaling in the limit of vanishing noise.

Sec.
2.5.1

Error Channel (
\mathcal{E}
)

Mathematical model representing the effect of errors in a quantum gate.

Sec.
2.2.1

Error Mitigation

Technique to reduce the impact of noise on quantum computations without reducing the noise level of any individual run.

Sec.
1.1

Gate Variant

One of a selection of noisy implementations of a noise-free target gate.

Sec.
4.2

Hidden Inverse (HI)

A gate variant constructed to have the opposite coherent error to the original gate, used for local cancellation.

Sec.
4.2.2

Identity Insertion (II)

A technique that amplifies noise by inserting sequences of gates and their inverses.

Sec.
4.2.4

Knowledge Free (KF)

Mitigation methods that do not require quantitive knowledge of the noise model.

Sec.
4.1

Length Scale Factor (
F_{{\mathrm{Gl,L}}{\mathrm{}}}
)

The average increase in circuit runtime due to mitigation.

Sec.
2.3.4

Linear Mitigation

Mitigation methods that use linear combinations of noisy circuit outputs to estimate noise-free results.

Sec.
2.1

Local Cancellation (LC)

A tailoring method that converts coherent noise to incoherent/stochastic noise.

Sec.
4.3

Local Mitigation (LM)

Mitigation applied independently to each gate or small group of nearby gates.

Sec.
4.1

Mitigation Method

A procedure for mitigating the noise of a quantum circuit.

Sec.
4

Mitigation Strategy

A procedure for producing mitigated circuits for quantum algorithms implemented on a selected and characterised piece of quantum hardware. May consist of multiple mitigation methods.

Sec.
2.5.2

NISQ

Noisy Intermediate-Scale Quantum: The current era of quantum devices with limited qubit counts and significant noise.

Ref.
[
40
]

{x}
,
p
or

)

Parameter quantifying the strength of noise in a quantum channel.

Sec.
2.2.1

Noise Aware (NA)

Mitigation methods that use quantitative knowledge of the noise model to optimise performance.

Sec.
4.1

Noise Boundary (
e_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{B}}
)

The maximum noise level that can be mitigated for a given tolerated runtime scaling and target bias.

Sec.
2.3.5

Noise Channel (
\mathcal{N}
)

A CPTP map that is part of a linear decomposition of an error channel.

Sec.
2.2.1

Noise Level (
e_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{G}}
)

Parameter quantifying the strength of noise in a quantum circuit to leading order.

Sec.
2.3.2

Over-Rotation Errors: An error channel equivalent to that produced by a rotation in Hilbert space rotating too far.

Term

Description

Reference

[

]

Property of a mitigation method that can reduce the effective noise level to an arbitrarily small amount.

Property of a mitigation method with runtime scaling independent of small characterised imperfections in method implementation.

The ratio of the total runtime required for a mitigated circuit to that of the unmitigated circuit to achieve the same precision.

The increase in the number of samples (shots) required for a mitigated circuit to preserve the statistical precision scaling of the original circuit.

Scalable, Unbounded, Precise, Efficient, Robust: Certification for the highest standard of mitigation methods.

Synchronous Mitigation (SM)

A mitigation method where all gates, within a particular circuit variant, are amplified by the same amount.

Structured classification of error mitigation techniques based on their characteristics and requirements.

Sec.

An optimised version of identity insertion where the number of identity insertions is chosen to minimise the runtime scaling.

Noise model where all gates have the same type of noise channel and the same noise amplitude.

The average increase in qubits required per circuit due to mitigation.

Sec.

Appendix B
Worked Example: IISM:NA

Here we provide calculations of proxy bias, before and after mitigation, and the sampling cost; for hardware suffering from uniform dephasing errors, mitigated using IISM:NA (noise-aware identity-insertion-assisted synchronous mitigation).

B.1
Notation

We use Pauli words (App.
B.1.1
) and transfer matrices (App.
B.1.2
).

B.1.1
Pauli Words and Bloch Decomposition

\vec{\mathrm{P}}
is the Pauli word vector and:

\displaystyle\mathrm{P}_{i}=

\displaystyle\left(\vec{\mathrm{P}}\right)_{i},

\displaystyle\mathrm{P}_{i}\in

\displaystyle\left\{\mathrm{I},\mathrm{X},\mathrm{Y},\mathrm{Z}\right\}^{\otimes{N_{\mathrm{Q}}}},

(B.182)

where
\mathrm{P}_{i}
is the
i
th Pauli word. For concreteness we define:

\displaystyle\left(\vec{\mathrm{P}}\right)_{i}=\mathrm{P}_{i}=\bigotimes_{n=1}^{{N_{\mathrm{Q}}}}\left(\vec{\sigma}_{\left[n\right]}\right)_{\left\lfloor\frac{i}{4^{n-1}}\right\rfloor\bmod{4}},

(B.183)

where a subscript
\left[n\right]
denotes that an operator acts on the
n
th qubit and
\vec{\sigma}_{\left[n\right]}
is the Pauli vector for the
n
th qubit:
\left(\vec{\sigma}_{\left[n\right]}\right)_{0}=\mathrm{I}
, the two by two identity matrix;
\left(\vec{\sigma}_{\left[n\right]}\right)_{1}=\mathrm{X}
, the two by two Pauli X matrix;
\left(\vec{\sigma}_{\left[n\right]}\right)_{2}=\mathrm{Y}
, the two by two Pauli Y matrix; and
\left(\vec{\sigma}_{\left[n\right]}\right)_{3}=\mathrm{Z}
, the two by two Pauli Z matrix. If we wish to denote the
n
th qubit operator of the
i
th component of
\vec{\mathrm{P}}
we use the notation:

\displaystyle\mathrm{P}_{i,n}=\left(\vec{\sigma}_{\left[n\right]}\right)_{\left\lfloor\frac{i}{4^{n-1}}\right\rfloor\bmod{4}}.

(B.184)

Some examples of Pauli words are:

\displaystyle\mathrm{P}_{0}=

\displaystyle\mathbb{I}=\mathrm{I}\otimes\dots\mathrm{I},

\displaystyle\mathrm{P}_{1}=

\displaystyle\mathrm{I}\otimes\dots\mathrm{X},

\displaystyle\mathrm{P}_{2}=

\displaystyle\mathrm{I}\otimes\dots\mathrm{Y},

\displaystyle\mathrm{P}_{3}=

\displaystyle\mathrm{I}\otimes\dots\mathrm{Z},

(B.185)

\displaystyle\mathrm{P}_{15}=

\displaystyle\mathrm{I}\otimes\dots\mathrm{Z}\otimes\mathrm{Z},

\displaystyle\mathrm{P}_{27}=

\displaystyle\mathrm{I}\otimes\dots\mathrm{X}\otimes\mathrm{Y}\otimes\mathrm{Z},

(B.186)

though generally we neglect leading identities.

The (generalised) Bloch decomposition is a vector representation of a density matrix in the basis of Pauli words:

\displaystyle\rho=\frac{{{\vec{b}}}\cdot\vec{\mathrm{P}}}{2^{{N_{\mathrm{Q}}}}},

(B.187)

where
{N_{\mathrm{Q}}}
is the number of qubits and

{{\vec{b}}}
, the generalised Bloch vector, is a vector of expectations for each of the Pauli words, so:

\displaystyle\left({{\vec{b}}}\right)_{i}=\langle\mathrm{P}_{i}\rangle=\mathrm{Tr}\left[\rho\mathrm{P}_{i}\right],

(B.188)

and therefore
-1\leq\left({{\vec{b}}}\right)_{i}\leq 1
.

B.1.2
Transfer Matrices

The output of a CPTP (completely-positive and trace-preserving) error channel (with input state
\rho
) is a density matrix:

\displaystyle\tilde{\rho}=\mathcal{E}_{{\mathrm{}}{\mathrm{}}}\left(\rho\right).

(B.189)

If
{\vec{b}}
is the Bloch vector of
\rho
and
\vec{\tilde{b}}
is the Bloch vector of
\tilde{\rho}
we have:

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2^{{N_{\mathrm{Q}}}}}\sum_{j=0}^{4^{{N_{\mathrm{Q}}}}-1}\left({\vec{\tilde{b}}}\right)_{j}\mathrm{P}_{j}=\frac{{\vec{\tilde{b}}}\cdot\vec{\mathrm{P}}}{2^{{N_{\mathrm{Q}}}}}=\tilde{\rho}=\mathcal{E}_{{\mathrm{}}{\mathrm{}}}\left(\rho\right)=\frac{\mathcal{E}_{{\mathrm{}}{\mathrm{}}}\left({{\vec{b}}}\cdot\vec{\mathrm{P}}\right)}{2^{{N_{\mathrm{Q}}}}}=\frac{1}{2^{{N_{\mathrm{Q}}}}}\sum_{j=0}^{4^{{N_{\mathrm{Q}}}}-1}\left({{\vec{b}}}\right)_{j}\mathcal{E}_{{\mathrm{}}{\mathrm{}}}\left(\mathrm{P}_{j}\right),

(B.190)

where we have used the linearity of the channel and the Bloch decomposition of the density operators (Eq. (
B.187
)). Thus we can express the channel as a map between Bloch vectors:

\displaystyle\left({\vec{\tilde{b}}}\right)_{i}=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2^{{N_{\mathrm{Q}}}}}\mathrm{Tr}\left[\sum_{j=0}^{4^{{N_{\mathrm{Q}}}}-1}\left({\vec{\tilde{b}}}\right)_{j}\mathrm{P}_{j}\mathrm{P}_{i}\right]=\mathrm{Tr}\left[\mathrm{P}_{i}\tilde{\rho}\right]=\mathrm{Tr}\left[\mathrm{P}_{i}\mathcal{E}_{{\mathrm{}}{\mathrm{}}}\left(\rho\right)\right]

(B.191)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2^{{N_{\mathrm{Q}}}}}\sum_{j=0}^{4^{{N_{\mathrm{Q}}}}-1}\mathrm{Tr}\left[\mathrm{P}_{i}\mathcal{E}_{{\mathrm{}}{\mathrm{}}}\left(\mathrm{P}_{j}\right)\right]\left({{\vec{b}}}\right)_{j},

(B.192)

so we have:

\displaystyle{\vec{\tilde{b}}}=

\displaystyle\hat{T}_{\mathcal{E}_{{\mathrm{}}{\mathrm{}}}}{{\vec{b}}},

(B.193)

were we define the Pauli transfer matrix as:

\displaystyle\left(\hat{T}_{\mathcal{E}_{{\mathrm{}}{\mathrm{}}}}\right)_{i,j}=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2^{{N_{\mathrm{Q}}}}}\mathrm{Tr}\left[\mathrm{P}_{i}\mathcal{E}_{{\mathrm{}}{\mathrm{}}}\left(\mathrm{P}_{j}\right)\right].

(B.194)

Since
{\vec{\tilde{b}}}
must have all elements between
-1
and
1
(see Eq. (
B.188
))
\hat{T}_{\mathcal{E}_{{\mathrm{}}{\mathrm{}}}}
must also have all elements between
-1
and
1
, otherwise there would be some choices of
{{\vec{b}}}
that would lead to unphysical
{\vec{\tilde{b}}}
.

B.2
Circuit

We consider a situation where we wish to implement the circuit:

(B.197)

and
\mathcal{E}_{{\mathrm{}}{\mathrm{}}}
is its error channel.

B.3
Assumptions and Requirements

1.

We assume a uniform noise model, which means every gate has an identical noise channel.

2.

We assume that the noise-free components of all gates commute with their error channels, a requirement for most methods using identity insertion.

3.

We assume that the noisy inverse gate has the same error channel as the noisy gate it is the (near) inverse of, a requirement for most methods using identity insertion.

4.

We use a single qubit dephasing error as our noise channel for illustrative purposes, but clearly this would not be appropriate for two qubit gates.

5.

We assume there are no SPAM (state preparation and measurement) errors, as our mitigation methods are designed to mitigate gates, there are other procedures for mitigating measurements
[
42
]
.

B.4
Noise Model Characterisation

We assume a dephasing noise channel with noise amplitude (error probability)
0\leq x\leq 1
:

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{{\mathrm{}}{\mathrm{}}}\!\left(\rho\right)=\left(1-x\right)\rho+x\mathrm{Z}\rho\mathrm{Z},

(B.198)

where
\mathrm{Z}
is a Pauli Z operator. Using circuit notation we have:

\displaystyle\begin{matrix}\scalebox{0.1}{\begin{picture}\put(0.0,0.0){\includegraphics[width,page]{gateM.pdf}}\put(0.40119,0.42637){\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0)[lt]{\begin{minipage}[335.53992pt]\raggedright\@add@raggedright\end{minipage}}}\put(0.27218,0.60885){\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0)[lt]{\begin{minipage}[391.46324pt]\raggedright\vskip (B.199)

B.4.1
Transfer Matrix

The transfer matrix for dephasing noise is given by (Eq. (
B.194
)):

\displaystyle\hat{T}_{\mathcal{E}_{{\mathrm{}}{\mathrm{}}}}=\begin{pmatrix}1&0&0&0\\
0&1-2x&0&0\\
0&0&1-2x&0\\
0&0&0&1\end{pmatrix},

(B.200)

this is a diagonal matrix with the eigenvalues:

\displaystyle\lambda_{{\mathrm{}0}{\mathrm{}}}=

\displaystyle 1,

\displaystyle\lambda_{{\mathrm{}1}{\mathrm{}}}=

\displaystyle 1-2x,

(B.201)

both of which are repeated.

B.4.2
Canonical Decomposition

We can decompose the channel into its canonical form by defining:

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{{\mathrm{}}{\mathrm{}}}=

\displaystyle f_{{\mathrm{}0}{\mathrm{}}}\!\left(x\right)\mathcal{N}_{{\mathrm{}0}{\mathrm{}}}+f_{{\mathrm{}1}{\mathrm{}}}\!\left(x\right)\mathcal{N}_{{\mathrm{}1}{\mathrm{}}},

\displaystyle f_{{\mathrm{}0}{\mathrm{}}}\!\left(x\right)=

\displaystyle 1-x,

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}_{{\mathrm{}0}{\mathrm{}}}=

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}_{{\mathrm{}}{\mathrm{}}},

(B.202)

\displaystyle f_{{\mathrm{}1}{\mathrm{}}}\!\left(x\right)=

\displaystyle x,

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}_{{\mathrm{}1}{\mathrm{}}}\!\left(\rho\right)=

\displaystyle\mathrm{Z}\rho\mathrm{Z},

(B.203)

where
\mathcal{I}_{{\mathrm{}}{\mathrm{}}}
is the identity channel (returns the input unchanged). It is worth noting here that
\mathcal{N}_{{\mathrm{}n}{\mathrm{}}}
is independent of the noise amplitude:
x
; and that both
\mathcal{I}_{{\mathrm{}}{\mathrm{}}}
and
\mathcal{N}_{{\mathrm{}n}{\mathrm{}}}
are CPTP (completely-positive and trace-preserving) channels. This means that if they act on a physical state they will return a physical state.

Since the identity channel commutes with all other channels this decomposition is self-compatible (i.e. its constituent noise channels commute). It is also closed under composition since the composition of two consecutive dephasing noise channels is equivalent to a single dephasing noise channel with a modified noise amplitude. That is, if we define:

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{{\mathrm{}x}{\mathrm{}}}=

\displaystyle\left(1-x\right)\mathcal{I}_{{\mathrm{}}{\mathrm{}}}+x\mathcal{N}_{{\mathrm{}1}{\mathrm{}}},

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{{\mathrm{}y}{\mathrm{}}}=

\displaystyle\left(1-y\right)\mathcal{I}_{{\mathrm{}}{\mathrm{}}}+y\mathcal{N}_{{\mathrm{}1}{\mathrm{}}},

(B.204)

then:

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{{\mathrm{}x}{\mathrm{}}}\circ\mathcal{E}_{{\mathrm{}y}{\mathrm{}}}=\mathcal{E}_{{\mathrm{}y}{\mathrm{}}}\circ\mathcal{E}_{{\mathrm{}x}{\mathrm{}}}=

\displaystyle\left(\left(1-x\right)\left(1-y\right)+xy\right)\mathcal{I}_{{\mathrm{}}{\mathrm{}}}+\left(x\left(1-y\right)+y\left(1-x\right)\right)\mathcal{N}_{{\mathrm{}1}{\mathrm{}}},

(B.205)

(since
\mathcal{N}_{{\mathrm{}1}{\mathrm{}}}^{2}=\mathcal{I}_{{\mathrm{}}{\mathrm{}}}
) which is just the dephasing noise channel
\mathcal{E}_{{\mathrm{}z}{\mathrm{}}}
where:

\displaystyle z=x+y\left(1-2x\right).

(B.206)

B.4.3
Circuit Noise Model

The channel that corresponds to the full circuit (see Eq. (
B.196
)) is:

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathcal{C}}_{{\mathrm{}}{\mathrm{}}}\penalty\ =

\displaystyle\penalty\ \tilde{\mathcal{U}}_{{\mathrm{}N_{\mathrm{G}}}{\mathrm{}}}\circ\tilde{\mathcal{U}}_{{\mathrm{}N_{\mathrm{G}}-1}{\mathrm{}}}\circ\dots\tilde{\mathcal{U}}_{{\mathrm{}1}{\mathrm{}}}\penalty\ =\mathcal{U}_{{\mathrm{}N_{\mathrm{G}}}{\mathrm{}}}\circ\mathcal{E}_{{\mathrm{}}{\mathrm{}}}\circ\mathcal{U}_{{\mathrm{}N_{\mathrm{G}}-1}{\mathrm{}}}\circ\mathcal{E}_{{\mathrm{}}{\mathrm{}}}\circ\dots\mathcal{U}_{{\mathrm{}1}{\mathrm{}}}\circ\mathcal{E}_{{\mathrm{}}{\mathrm{}}}

\displaystyle\penalty\ =

\displaystyle\penalty\ \left(1-x\right)^{N_{\mathrm{G}}}\mathcal{U}_{{\mathrm{}N_{\mathrm{G}}}{\mathrm{}}}\circ\mathcal{U}_{{\mathrm{}N_{\mathrm{G}}-1}{\mathrm{}}}\circ\dots\mathcal{U}_{{\mathrm{}1}{\mathrm{}}}

\displaystyle+x\left(1-x\right)^{N_{\mathrm{G}}-1}\left(\mathcal{U}_{{\mathrm{}N_{\mathrm{G}}}{\mathrm{}}}\circ\mathcal{N}_{{\mathrm{}1}{\mathrm{}}}\circ\mathcal{U}_{{\mathrm{}N_{\mathrm{G}}-1}{\mathrm{}}}\circ\dots\mathcal{U}_{{\mathrm{}1}{\mathrm{}}}+\dots+\mathcal{U}_{{\mathrm{}N_{\mathrm{G}}}{\mathrm{}}}\circ\mathcal{U}_{{\mathrm{}N_{\mathrm{G}}-1}{\mathrm{}}}\circ\dots\mathcal{U}_{{\mathrm{}1}{\mathrm{}}}\circ\mathcal{N}_{{\mathrm{}1}{\mathrm{}}}\right)

\displaystyle+\dots+x^{N_{\mathrm{G}}}\mathcal{U}_{{\mathrm{}N_{\mathrm{G}}}{\mathrm{}}}\circ\mathcal{N}_{{\mathrm{}1}{\mathrm{}}}\circ\mathcal{U}_{{\mathrm{}N_{\mathrm{G}}-1}{\mathrm{}}}\circ\mathcal{N}_{{\mathrm{}1}{\mathrm{}}}\circ\dots\mathcal{U}_{{\mathrm{}1}{\mathrm{}}}\circ\mathcal{N}_{{\mathrm{}1}{\mathrm{}}},

(B.207)

where we have used the canonical error channel decomposition of Eq. (
B.202
);
\tilde{\mathcal{C}}_{{\mathrm{}}{\mathrm{}}}
is the channel corresponding to the whole circuit; and
\tilde{\mathcal{U}}_{{\mathrm{}n}{\mathrm{}}}
,
\mathcal{U}_{{\mathrm{}n}{\mathrm{}}}
, and
\tilde{\mathcal{E}}_{{\mathrm{}}{\mathrm{}}}
are the channels corresponding to the
n
th noisy-gate, noise-free component of the noisy gate, and error channel of the noisy gate, respectively.

We can write the expansion in Eq. (
B.4.3
) more succinctly in the form:

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathcal{C}}_{{\mathrm{}}{\mathrm{}}}\penalty\ =

\displaystyle\penalty\ \sum_{k=0}^{N_{\mathrm{G}}}\left(1-x\right)^{N_{\mathrm{G}}-k}x^{k}\breve{\chi}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{SN},k}},

(B.208)

where:

\displaystyle\breve{\chi}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{SN},0}}=

\displaystyle\mathcal{U}_{{\mathrm{}N_{\mathrm{G}}}{\mathrm{}}}\circ\mathcal{U}_{{\mathrm{}N_{\mathrm{G}}-1}{\mathrm{}}}\circ\dots\mathcal{U}_{{\mathrm{}1}{\mathrm{}}},

(B.209)

\displaystyle\breve{\chi}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{SN},1}}=

\displaystyle\mathcal{U}_{{\mathrm{}N_{\mathrm{G}}}{\mathrm{}}}\circ\mathcal{N}_{{\mathrm{}1}{\mathrm{}}}\circ\mathcal{U}_{{\mathrm{}N_{\mathrm{G}}-1}{\mathrm{}}}\circ\dots\mathcal{U}_{{\mathrm{}1}{\mathrm{}}}+\dots+\mathcal{U}_{{\mathrm{}N_{\mathrm{G}}}{\mathrm{}}}\circ\mathcal{U}_{{\mathrm{}N_{\mathrm{G}}-1}{\mathrm{}}}\circ\dots\mathcal{U}_{{\mathrm{}1}{\mathrm{}}}\circ\mathcal{N}_{{\mathrm{}1}{\mathrm{}}},

(B.210)

\displaystyle\dots

\displaystyle\breve{\chi}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{SN},N_{\mathrm{G}}}}=

\displaystyle\mathcal{U}_{{\mathrm{}N_{\mathrm{G}}}{\mathrm{}}}\circ\mathcal{N}_{{\mathrm{}1}{\mathrm{}}}\circ\mathcal{U}_{{\mathrm{}N_{\mathrm{G}}-1}{\mathrm{}}}\circ\mathcal{N}_{{\mathrm{}1}{\mathrm{}}}\circ\dots\mathcal{U}_{{\mathrm{}1}{\mathrm{}}}\circ\mathcal{N}_{{\mathrm{}1}{\mathrm{}}},

(B.211)

i.e.
\breve{\chi}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{SN},k}}
is the term containing all the constituent maps of
\tilde{\mathcal{C}}_{{\mathrm{}}{\mathrm{}}}
that include exactly
k
occurrences of the channel
\mathcal{N}_{{\mathrm{}1}{\mathrm{}}}
. Clearly,
\breve{\chi}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{SN},0}}
is equivalent to the noise-free target circuit (
\mathcal{C}_{{\mathrm{}}{\mathrm{}}}
, see Eq. (
B.195
)):

\displaystyle\breve{\chi}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{SN},0}}=\mathcal{U}_{{\mathrm{}N_{\mathrm{G}}}{\mathrm{}}}\circ\mathcal{U}_{{\mathrm{}N_{\mathrm{G}}-1}{\mathrm{}}}\circ\dots\mathcal{U}_{{\mathrm{}1}{\mathrm{}}}=\mathcal{C}_{{\mathrm{}}{\mathrm{}}}.

(B.212)

We christen the number of constituent channels of
\breve{\chi}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{SN},k}}
its multiplicity:

\displaystyle\#\left[\breve{\chi}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{SN},k}}\right]=\frac{N_{\mathrm{G}}!}{k!\left(N_{\mathrm{G}}-k\right)!}.

(B.213)

B.4.4
Proxy Bias and Noise Level

The bias (
\varepsilon_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{O}}
) due to noise in a circuit for an observable (
\hat{O}
, here assumed to be a Pauli word operator
27
27

27

We choose a Pauli word operator for our proxy bias observable to simplify the calculation but this is not really necessary. In fact, all we need is an operator with an expectation (on a physical state) bounded between
-1
and
1
. Since rescaling and shifting an observable does not change its fractional error, we can obtain a bound for any observable simply by rescaling the proxy bias given here.
:
\hat{O}=\mathrm{P}_{i}
) is given by:

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{O}}=\left|\left\langle\tilde{O}\right\rangle-\left\langle\hat{O}\right\rangle\right|,

(B.214)

where:

\displaystyle\left\langle\hat{O}\right\rangle=

\displaystyle\mathrm{Tr}\left[\hat{O}\mathcal{C}_{{\mathrm{}}{\mathrm{}}}\left(\rho_{0}\right)\right],

\displaystyle\left\langle\tilde{O}\right\rangle=

\displaystyle\mathrm{Tr}\left[\hat{O}\tilde{\mathcal{C}}_{{\mathrm{}}{\mathrm{}}}\left(\rho_{0}\right)\right],

(B.215)

where
\rho_{0}
is the initial state of the qubits,
\left\langle\hat{O}\right\rangle
is the noise-free observable expectation and
\left\langle\tilde{O}\right\rangle
is the noisy observable expectation. Using our notation in Eq. (
B.208
) we can re-express the bias in terms of our chi-terms (see Eq. (
B.213
)):

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{O}}=

\displaystyle\left|\mathrm{Tr}\left[\hat{O}\left(\tilde{\mathcal{C}}_{{\mathrm{}}{\mathrm{}}}\left(\rho_{0}\right)-\mathcal{C}_{{\mathrm{}}{\mathrm{}}}\left(\rho_{0}\right)\right)\right]\right|=\left|\mathrm{Tr}\left[\hat{O}\left(\sum_{k=0}^{N_{\mathrm{G}}}\left(1-x\right)^{N_{\mathrm{G}}-k}x^{k}\breve{\chi}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{SN},k}}\left(\rho_{0}\right)-\breve{\chi}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{SN},0}}\left(\rho_{0}\right)\right)\right]\right|

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left|\left(\left(1-x\right)^{N_{\mathrm{G}}}-1\right)\mathrm{Tr}\left[\hat{O}\breve{\chi}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{SN},0}}\left(\rho_{0}\right)\right]+\sum_{k=1}^{N_{\mathrm{G}}}\left(1-x\right)^{N_{\mathrm{G}}-k}x^{k}\mathrm{Tr}\left[\hat{O}\breve{\chi}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{SN},k}}\left(\rho_{0}\right)\right]\right|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\left(1-\left(1-x\right)^{N_{\mathrm{G}}}\right)+\sum_{k=1}^{N_{\mathrm{G}}}\left(1-x\right)^{N_{\mathrm{G}}-k}x^{k}\frac{N_{\mathrm{G}}!}{k!\left(N_{\mathrm{G}}-k\right)!}=2\left(1-\left(1-x\right)^{N_{\mathrm{G}}}\right),

(B.216)

where we have inverted the binomial expansion in the final step and in the penultimate step used the triangle inequality and the fact that (see Eq. (
B.213
)):

\displaystyle-\#\left[\breve{\chi}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{SN},k}}\right]\leq\mathrm{Tr}\left[\hat{O}\breve{\chi}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{SN},k}}\left(\rho_{0}\right)\right]\leq\#\left[\breve{\chi}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{SN},k}}\right].

(B.217)

This holds because each of the sub-maps in the sum that makes up
\breve{\chi}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{SN},k}}\left(\rho_{0}\right)
is a CPTP map (see Eq. (
B.209
)), which means when it acts on
\rho_{0}
(a physical state) it returns a physical state. Since the modulus of the expectation of a Pauli operator with respect to a physical state (
\rho
) is bounded by unity:

\displaystyle-1\leq\mathrm{Tr}\left[\rho\mathrm{P}_{i}\right]\leq 1,

(B.218)

the maximum magnitude of the expectation with respect to
\breve{\chi}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{SN},k}}\left(\rho_{0}\right)
occurs when every sub-map expectation has the same sign. In this case we simply count the sub-maps (i.e. use the multiplicity) to find the bounds for the expectation with respect to
\breve{\chi}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{SN},k}}\left(\rho_{0}\right)
. We define the upper bound to the bias in Eq. (
B.4.4
) as the proxy bias:

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{\scriptscriptstyle\heartsuit}=

\displaystyle 2\left(1-\left(1-x\right)^{N_{\mathrm{G}}}\right),

(B.219)

which is independent of the structure of the gates (
U_{{\mathrm{}n}{\mathrm{}}}
) within the circuit, depending only on how many error channels there are and what their noise amplitude is.

We define the noise level (
e_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{G}}
) of the circuit as the first order expansion of the proxy bias in the small noise (
xN_{\mathrm{G}}\ll 1
) regime:

\displaystyle e_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{G}}=2xN_{\mathrm{G}}.

(B.220)

B.5
Mitigation: IISM:NA

We will now work through the mitigation method evaluation procedure for IISM:NA (noise-aware identity-insertion-assisted synchronous mitigation). This method uses knowledge about the noise model to effect the mitigation. It makes use of identity insertions to amplify the errors for each gate in the circuit. It is a synchronous mitigation strategy so it amplifies the errors of each gate by the same amount for each circuit variant.

B.5.1
Identity Insertion

Identity insertion involves resolving the (noisy) identity before every noisy gate, in terms of the noisy gate and its inverse. So:

\displaystyle\penalty\ =\penalty\

, after

\mathcal{E}_{{\mathrm{}};{\mathrm{}i}}
, where (see Eqs (
B.199

(B.223)

where (see Eqs (
B.200
) and (
B.206
)):

\displaystyle x_{i}=\frac{1}{2}\left(1-\left(1-2x\right)^{2i+1}\right),

(B.224)

is the amplified noise amplitude, where
x_{0}=x
is the original noise amplitude. So, to leading order, the noise amplitude of the
i
th circuit is
2i+1
times the original noise amplitude:

\displaystyle x_{i}=\left(2i+1\right)x+\mathcal{O}_{0}\left(x^{2}\right).

(B.225)

B.5.2
Zero-Noise Extrapolation

Zero-noise extrapolation or Richardson extrapolation
[
16
]
is a mitigation method that involves finding a weighted sum of noisy observables that is equivalent to the noise-free observable up to some power in the noise amplitude:

\displaystyle\left\langle\tilde{O}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{M}}=\sum_{i=0}^{N_{\mathrm{M}}}c_{\mathrm{Gl};i}\left\langle\tilde{O}\right\rangle_{i}=\left\langle\hat{O}\right\rangle+\mathcal{O}_{0}\left(x^{N_{\mathrm{M}}+1}\right),

(B.226)

where (see Eq. (
B.215
)):

\displaystyle\left\langle\tilde{O}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{M}}=

\displaystyle\mathrm{Tr}\left[\hat{O}\sum_{i=0}^{N_{\mathrm{M}}}c_{\mathrm{Gl};i}\tilde{\mathcal{C}}_{{\mathrm{}};{\mathrm{}i}}\left(\rho_{0}\right)\right],

\displaystyle\left\langle\tilde{O}\right\rangle_{i}=

\displaystyle\mathrm{Tr}\left[\hat{O}\tilde{\mathcal{C}}_{{\mathrm{}};{\mathrm{}i}}\left(\rho_{0}\right)\right],

\displaystyle\left\langle\hat{O}\right\rangle=

\displaystyle\mathrm{Tr}\left[\hat{O}\mathcal{C}_{{\mathrm{}}{\mathrm{}}}\left(\rho_{0}\right)\right],

(B.227)

are the mitigated observable,
i
th noisy observable, and noise-free observable, respectively; and
N_{\mathrm{M}}
is the order of the mitigation and the number of extra noisy circuit variants required for the mitigation. Each of the noisy circuit variants should be equivalent to the original circuit except for having an amplified noise amplitude.

We Taylor expand our noisy observables in terms of the noise amplitude:

\displaystyle\left\langle\tilde{O}\right\rangle_{i}=\left\langle\tilde{O}\right\rangle\left(x_{i}\right)=\sum_{m=0}^{\infty}d_{m}x_{i}^{m}=\left\langle\hat{O}\right\rangle+\mathcal{O}_{0}\left(x_{i}\right),

(B.228)

where
d_{m}
are coefficients independent of
x_{i}
. For dephasing noise we have (Eq. (
B.208
)):

\displaystyle\left\langle\tilde{O}\right\rangle_{i}=\mathrm{Tr}\left[\hat{O}\tilde{\mathcal{C}}_{{\mathrm{}};{\mathrm{}i}}\left(\rho_{0}\right)\right]=\sum_{k=0}^{N_{\mathrm{G}}}\left(1-x_{i}\right)^{N_{\mathrm{G}}-k}x^{k}\mathrm{Tr}\left[\hat{O}\breve{\chi}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{SN},k}}\left(\rho_{0}\right)\right],

(B.229)

and therefore:

\displaystyle d_{m}=\begin{cases}\sum_{k=0}^{m}\frac{\left(N_{\mathrm{G}}-k\right)!}{\left(N_{\mathrm{G}}-m\right)!\left(m-k\right)!}\left(-1\right)^{m-k}\mathrm{Tr}\left[\hat{O}\breve{\chi}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{SN},k}}\left(\rho_{0}\right)\right]&\text{ for }m\leq N_{\mathrm{G}},\\
0&\text{ for }m>N_{\mathrm{G}}.\end{cases}

(B.230)

This means Eq. (
B.226
) is equivalent to:

\displaystyle\left\langle\tilde{O}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{M}}=\sum_{i=0}^{N_{\mathrm{M}}}c_{\mathrm{Gl};i}\left\langle\tilde{O}\right\rangle_{i}=\sum_{m=0}^{\infty}d_{m}\sum_{i=0}^{N_{\mathrm{M}}}c_{\mathrm{Gl};i}x_{i}^{m}=\left\langle\hat{O}\right\rangle+\mathcal{O}_{0}\left(x^{N_{\mathrm{M}}+1}\right),

(B.231)

or equivalently:

\displaystyle\sum_{i=0}^{N_{\mathrm{M}}}c_{\mathrm{Gl};i}=

\displaystyle 1,

\displaystyle\sum_{i=0}^{N_{\mathrm{M}}}c_{\mathrm{Gl};i}x_{i}^{k}=x^{k}\sum_{i=0}^{N_{\mathrm{M}}}c_{\mathrm{Gl};i}\left(\frac{x_{i}}{x}\right)^{k}=

\displaystyle 0\text{ for }1\leq k\leq N_{\mathrm{M}}.

(B.232)

So the coefficients depend only on the relative quantity
x_{i}/x
and not on the absolute value of the noise amplitude. If we solve Eq. (
B.232
) we find (see Ref.
[
34
]
):

\displaystyle c_{\mathrm{Gl};i}=

\displaystyle\prod_{m=0:m\neq i}^{N_{\mathrm{M}}}\frac{x_{m}}{x_{m}-x_{i}},

\displaystyle\sum_{i=0}^{N_{\mathrm{M}}}c_{\mathrm{Gl};i}x_{i}^{N_{\mathrm{M}}+1}=

\displaystyle\left(-1\right)^{N_{\mathrm{M}}}\prod_{n=0}^{N_{\mathrm{M}}}x_{n}.

(B.233)

Thus (Eq. (
B.231
)):

\displaystyle\left\langle\tilde{O}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{M}}=\left\langle\hat{O}\right\rangle+\left(-1\right)^{N_{\mathrm{M}}}\left(\prod_{n=0}^{N_{\mathrm{M}}}x_{n}\right)d_{N_{\mathrm{M}}+1}+\mathcal{O}_{0}\left(x^{N_{\mathrm{M}}+2}\right).

(B.234)

B.5.3
Bias, Proxy Bias, and Noise Level after Mitigation

The bias is given by (see Eqs (
B.213
), (
B.214
), (
B.217
), and (
B.230
)):

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{O}}=

\displaystyle\left|\left\langle\tilde{O}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{M}}-\left\langle\hat{O}\right\rangle\right|=\left(\prod_{n=0}^{N_{\mathrm{M}}}x_{n}\right)\left|d_{N_{\mathrm{M}}+1}\right|+\mathcal{O}_{0}\left(x^{N_{\mathrm{M}}+2}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(\prod_{n=0}^{N_{\mathrm{M}}}x_{n}\right)\sum_{k=0}^{N_{\mathrm{M}}+1}\frac{\left(N_{\mathrm{G}}-k\right)!}{\left(N_{\mathrm{G}}-N_{\mathrm{M}}-1\right)!\left(N_{\mathrm{M}}+1-k\right)!}\left|\mathrm{Tr}\left[\hat{O}\breve{\chi}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{SN},k}}\left(\rho_{0}\right)\right]\right|+\mathcal{O}_{0}\left(x^{N_{\mathrm{M}}+2}\right)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\left(\prod_{n=0}^{N_{\mathrm{M}}}x_{n}\right)\sum_{k=0}^{N_{\mathrm{M}}+1}\frac{N_{\mathrm{G}}!}{\left(N_{\mathrm{G}}-N_{\mathrm{M}}-1\right)!\left(N_{\mathrm{M}}+1-k\right)!}\frac{}{k!}+\mathcal{O}_{0}\left(x^{N_{\mathrm{M}}+2}\right)

\displaystyle=2^{N_{\mathrm{M}}+1}\left(\prod_{n=0}^{N_{\mathrm{M}}}x_{n}\right)\frac{N_{\mathrm{G}}!}{\left(N_{\mathrm{G}}-N_{\mathrm{M}}-1\right)!\left(N_{\mathrm{M}}+1\right)!}+\mathcal{O}_{0}\left(x^{N_{\mathrm{M}}+2}\right),

(B.235)

where we have assumed that the observable is a Pauli word operator (
\hat{O}=\mathrm{P}_{i}
), as in App.
B.4.4
, and therefore the modulus of its expectation for any physical operator is bounded by unity. We have also assumed that
N_{\mathrm{M}}<N_{\mathrm{G}}
, if
N_{\mathrm{M}}\geq N_{\mathrm{G}}
then the mitigation is unbiased. So the mitigated proxy bias (an upper bound for the bias) can be given by:

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{\scriptscriptstyle\heartsuit;\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{M}}=2^{N_{\mathrm{M}}+1}\left(\prod_{n=0}^{N_{\mathrm{M}}}\left(2i+1\right)\right)\frac{N_{\mathrm{G}}!}{\left(N_{\mathrm{G}}-N_{\mathrm{M}}-1\right)!\left(N_{\mathrm{M}}+1\right)!}x^{N_{\mathrm{M}}+1}+\mathcal{O}_{0}\left(x^{N_{\mathrm{M}}+2}\right),

(B.236)

where we have used the expression for the noise amplitude of the
i
th circuit variant given in Eq. (
B.225
). Since we have:

\displaystyle\frac{N_{\mathrm{G}}!}{\left(N_{\mathrm{M}}+1\right)!\left(N_{\mathrm{G}}-N_{\mathrm{M}}-1\right)!}=\frac{N_{\mathrm{G}}^{N_{\mathrm{M}}+1}}{\left(N_{\mathrm{M}}+1\right)!}+\mathcal{O}_{\mathrm{L}}\left(N_{\mathrm{G}}^{N_{\mathrm{M}}}\right),

(B.237)

in the large circuit (
N_{\mathrm{G}}\ll 1
) and small noise (
N_{\mathrm{G}}x\ll 1
) regime we have:

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{\scriptscriptstyle\heartsuit;\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{M}}=2^{N_{\mathrm{M}}+1}\left(\prod_{n=0}^{N_{\mathrm{M}}}\left(2i+1\right)\right)\frac{\left(N_{\mathrm{G}}x\right)^{N_{\mathrm{M}}+1}}{\left(N_{\mathrm{M}}+1\right)!}+\mathcal{O}_{\mathrm{LS}}\left(N_{\mathrm{G}}^{N_{\mathrm{M}}}x^{N_{\mathrm{M}}+1},N_{\mathrm{G}}^{N_{\mathrm{M}}+2}x^{N_{\mathrm{M}}+2}\right),

(B.238)

where we have used Eqs (
B.230
) and (
B.231
) in order to determine the correct scaling with
N_{\mathrm{G}}
and
x
. This means the noise level (after mitigation) is given by (Eq. (
B.236
)):

\displaystyle e_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{G};\mathrm{M}}=\left(\prod_{n=0}^{N_{\mathrm{M}}}\left(2i+1\right)\right)\frac{N_{\mathrm{G}}!}{N_{\mathrm{G}}^{N_{\mathrm{M}}+1}\left(N_{\mathrm{G}}-N_{\mathrm{M}}-1\right)!\left(N_{\mathrm{M}}+1\right)!}e_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{G}}^{N_{\mathrm{M}}+1},

(B.239)

in the small noise regime (
N_{\mathrm{G}}x\ll 1
) and
28
28

28

The ratio of the value used to bound the mitigated noise level in Eq. (
B.240
) to the true mitigated noise level is roughly
\sqrt{\uppi\left(N_{\mathrm{M}}+1\right)}/2
(this value has been obtained numerically) for large
N_{\mathrm{M}}
.
:

\displaystyle e_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{G};\mathrm{M}}=\frac{\left(2N_{\mathrm{M}}+1\right)!!}{\left(N_{\mathrm{M}}+1\right)!}e_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{G}}^{N_{\mathrm{M}}+1}<\frac{\left(2N_{\mathrm{M}}+2\right)!!}{2\left(N_{\mathrm{M}}+1\right)!}e_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{G}}^{N_{\mathrm{M}}+1}=\frac{\left(2e_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{G}}\right)^{N_{\mathrm{M}}+1}}{2},

(B.240)

in the small noise and large circuit regime (
xN_{\mathrm{G}},N_{\mathrm{G}}^{-1}\ll 1
). Where
!!
is the double factorial, defined by:

\displaystyle n!!=

\displaystyle n\left(n-2\right)!!,

\displaystyle 1!!=0!!=1,

(B.241)

and
e_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{G}}=2xN_{\mathrm{G}}
is defined in Eq. (
B.220
).

B.5.4
Sampling Cost, Length Scale Factor, and Runtime Scaling

The most efficient way to implement our mitigation procedure (Eq. (
B.231
)) is to use Monte-Carlo sampling
[
14
]
. To implement this we define a probability (
q_{\mathrm{Gl};i}
) and sign (
s_{\mathrm{Gl};i}
) associated with each of circuit variants, so the
i
th circuit has:

\displaystyle q_{\mathrm{Gl};i}=

\displaystyle\frac{\left|c_{{\mathrm{Gl}};{\mathrm{}i}}\right|}{C_{{\mathrm{Gl}}{\mathrm{}}}},

\displaystyle s_{\mathrm{Gl};i}=

\displaystyle\frac{c_{{\mathrm{Gl}};{\mathrm{}i}}}{\left|c_{{\mathrm{Gl}};{\mathrm{}i}}\right|},

\displaystyle C_{{\mathrm{Gl}}{\mathrm{}}}=

\displaystyle\sum_{i=0}^{N_{\mathrm{M}}}\left|c_{{\mathrm{Gl}};{\mathrm{}i}}\right|,

(B.242)

where
C_{{\mathrm{Gl}}{\mathrm{}}}
is known as the sampling cost
[
36
]
, for reasons that will become apparent. We simply make
N_{\mathrm{R}}
runs of our circuit and each time we randomly choose one of our
N_{\mathrm{M}}+1
circuits to implement and multiply the resulting output by the sign
s_{\mathrm{Gl};i}
corresponding to the circuit
i
that we choose for that run. We sample circuit
i
with probability
q_{\mathrm{Gl};i}
. The expectation of this procedure is
\left\langle\tilde{O}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{M}}/C_{{\mathrm{Gl}}{\mathrm{}}}
so we simply multiply our expectation by the sampling cost in order to obtain our mitigated value. The variance of the Monte Carlo sampling procedure is given by the shot noise:

\displaystyle\sigma_{\mathrm{MC}}^{2}\propto\frac{1}{N_{\mathrm{R}}}.

(B.243)

However, because we multiply our final result by
C_{{\mathrm{Gl}}{\mathrm{}}}
we will increase the variance by a factor of
C_{{\mathrm{Gl}}{\mathrm{}}}^{2}
. This means the ratio of the number of runs needed for the mitigated circuit to the number of runs needed for the unmitigated circuit, to yield the same variance is approximately
[
14
]
:

\displaystyle\frac{N_{\mathrm{R},\mathrm{M}}}{N_{\mathrm{R}}}\approx C_{{\mathrm{Gl}}{\mathrm{}}}^{2}.

(B.244)

So
C_{{\mathrm{Gl}}{\mathrm{}}}
is the (square root of the) cost for sampling quasi-stochastic ensembles, if
c_{{\mathrm{Gl}};{\mathrm{}i}}>0
for all
i
then
C_{{\mathrm{Gl}}{\mathrm{}}}=1
(see Eq. (
B.232
)). For the coefficients given in Eq. (
B.233
) (see Eq. (
B.225
)):

\displaystyle c_{\mathrm{Gl};i}=

\displaystyle\prod_{m=0:m\neq i}^{N_{\mathrm{M}}}\frac{2m+1+\mathcal{O}_{0}\left(x\right)}{2\left(m-i\right)+\mathcal{O}_{0}\left(x\right)}=\frac{\left(2N_{\mathrm{M}}+1\right)!!}{2^{N_{\mathrm{M}}}\left(2i+1\right)\left(i!\right)\left(\left(N_{\mathrm{M}}-i\right)!\right)}+\mathcal{O}_{0}\left(x\right).

(B.245)

So the sampling cost for using IISM:NA to mitigate dephasing noise is given by
29
29

29

The sampling cost sum (see Eqs (
B.245
) and (
B.246
)) was calculated using Mathematica
[
43
]
.
:

\displaystyle C_{{\mathrm{Gl}}{\mathrm{}}}=

\displaystyle\sum_{i=0}^{N_{\mathrm{M}}}\left|c_{{\mathrm{Gl}};{\mathrm{}i}}\right|=\frac{\left(2N_{\mathrm{M}}+1\right)!!{}_{2}F_{1}\left(\frac{1}{2},-N_{\mathrm{M}};\frac{3}{2};-1\right)}{2^{N_{\mathrm{M}}}\left(N_{\mathrm{M}}!\right)}+\mathcal{O}_{0}\left(x\right),

(B.246)

where
!!
is the double factorial,

\displaystyle{}_{2}F_{1}\left(a,b;c;z\right)=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\frac{\Gamma\left(a+k\right)}{\Gamma\left(a\right)}\frac{\Gamma\left(b+k\right)}{\Gamma\left(b\right)}\frac{\Gamma\left(c\right)}{\Gamma\left(c+k\right)}\frac{z^{k}}{k!},

(B.247)

is a Hypergeometric function and:

\displaystyle\Gamma\left(z\right)=\int_{0}^{\infty}t^{z-1}\mathrm{e}^{-t}\mathrm{d}t,

(B.248)

is the Euler gamma function (see
[
43
]
).

As well as increasing the number of samples required, each run will take longer, on average, to implement because we are increasing the number of gates we use. The
i
th circuit has
2i+1
times the gates of the original noisy circuit and so will take
2i+1
times longer (neglecting initialisation and measurement time). Thus the average runtime of the mitigated circuit (
\tau_{\mathrm{E},\mathrm{M}}
) relative to the original noisy circuit (
\tau_{\mathrm{E}}
) is:

\displaystyle F_{{\mathrm{Gl,L}}{\mathrm{}}}=\frac{\tau_{\mathrm{E},\mathrm{M}}}{\tau_{\mathrm{E}}}=\sum_{i=0}^{N_{\mathrm{M}}}q_{\mathrm{Gl};i}\left(2i+1\right)=\frac{\sum_{i=0}^{N_{\mathrm{M}}}\left|c_{{\mathrm{Gl}};{\mathrm{}i}}\right|\left(2i+1\right)}{C_{{\mathrm{Gl}}{\mathrm{}}}},

(B.249)

which we denote the length scale factor (again neglecting initialisation and measurement time). So the length scale factor for using IISM:NA to mitigate dephasing noise is given by:

\displaystyle F_{{\mathrm{Gl,L}}{\mathrm{}}}=

\displaystyle\frac{\left(2N_{\mathrm{M}}+1\right)!!}{2^{N_{\mathrm{M}}}C_{{\mathrm{Gl}}{\mathrm{}}}}\sum_{i=0}^{N_{\mathrm{M}}}\frac{1}{\left(i!\right)\left(\left(N_{\mathrm{M}}-i\right)!\right)}+\mathcal{O}_{0}\left(x\right)=\frac{\left(2N_{\mathrm{M}}+1\right)!!}{N_{\mathrm{M}}!C_{{\mathrm{Gl}}{\mathrm{}}}}+\mathcal{O}_{0}\left(x\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{2^{N_{\mathrm{M}}}}{{}_{2}F_{1}\left(\frac{1}{2},-N_{\mathrm{M}};\frac{3}{2};-1\right)}+\mathcal{O}_{0}\left(x\right).

(B.250)

Thus the ratio of the total mitigation procedure runtime (
t_{\mathrm{M}}
) compared to that for the original noisy circuit (
t_{0}
) to achieve the same variance is given by:

\displaystyle S_{{\mathrm{Gl}}{\mathrm{}}}=\frac{t_{\mathrm{M}}}{t_{0}}\approx C_{{\mathrm{Gl}}{\mathrm{}}}^{2}F_{{\mathrm{Gl,L}}{\mathrm{}}}=\frac{\left(\left(2N_{\mathrm{M}}+1\right)!!\right)^{2}{}_{2}F_{1}\left(\frac{1}{2},-N_{\mathrm{M}};\frac{3}{2};-1\right)}{2^{N_{\mathrm{M}}}\left(N_{\mathrm{M}}!\right)^{2}}+\mathcal{O}_{0}\left(x\right),

(B.251)

which we denote the runtime scaling (again we assume negligible initialisation and measurement times). The width scale factor doesn’t feature here because we use the same number of qubits as the original noisy circuit.

B.5.5
Noise Boundary

The noise boundary is the maximum noise that can be mitigated for a fixed maximum runtime scaling (
S_{{\mathrm{Gl}}{\mathrm{}}}=S_{\pluscirc}
) and target bias (
\varepsilon_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{O}}=\varepsilon_{\scriptscriptstyle\pluscirc}
). To ensure we do not exceed the bias we choose an order of mitigation (
N_{\mathrm{M}}
) that will reduce the proxy bias to below the target bias (
\varepsilon_{\scriptscriptstyle\pluscirc}
). To make our analytical analysis more straightforward we will use the mitigated noise level bound derived in Eq. (
B.240
) rather than the full proxy bias. So we set the target bias (
\varepsilon_{\scriptscriptstyle\pluscirc}
) equal to the mitigated noise level bound and then invert the expression to find the required mitigation order (
N_{\mathrm{M}}
):

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{\scriptscriptstyle\pluscirc}=

\displaystyle\frac{\left(2e_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{G}}\right)^{N_{\mathrm{M}}+1}}{2}

\displaystyle\implies

\displaystyle N_{\mathrm{M}}=

\displaystyle\frac{\ln\left(2\varepsilon_{\scriptscriptstyle\pluscirc}\right)}{\ln\left(2e_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{G}}\right)}-1.

(B.252)

Now, we want to find the largest possible noise level (
e_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{G}}
) that we can mitigate so we will assume that
N_{\mathrm{M}}\gg 1
, i.e. our target bias is much smaller than our original noise level. In this case we can use the fact that:

\displaystyle{}_{2}F_{1}\left(\frac{1}{2},-N_{\mathrm{M}};\frac{3}{2};-1\right)=

\displaystyle\frac{2^{N_{\mathrm{M}}}}{N_{\mathrm{M}}}\left(1+\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{N_{\mathrm{M}}^{2}}\right)\right),

(B.253)

\displaystyle\frac{\left(2N_{\mathrm{M}}+1\right)!!}{2^{N_{\mathrm{M}}}\left(N_{\mathrm{M}}!\right)}=

\displaystyle 2\sqrt{\frac{N_{\mathrm{M}}}{\uppi}}\left(1+\frac{3}{8N_{\mathrm{M}}}+\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{N_{\mathrm{M}}^{2}}\right)\right).

(B.254)

(found by numerical and analytical analysis) to simplify the form of our sampling cost and length scale factor (Eqs (
B.246
) and (
B.5.4
)):

\displaystyle C_{{\mathrm{Gl}}{\mathrm{}}}=

\displaystyle\frac{\left(2N_{\mathrm{M}}+1\right)!!{}_{2}F_{1}\left(\frac{1}{2},-N_{\mathrm{M}};\frac{3}{2};-1\right)}{2^{N_{\mathrm{M}}}\left(N_{\mathrm{M}}!\right)}+\mathcal{O}_{0}\left(x\right)=\frac{2^{N_{\mathrm{M}}+1}}{\sqrt{N_{\mathrm{M}}\uppi}}\left(1+\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{N_{\mathrm{M}}}\right)\right)+\mathcal{O}_{0}\left(x\right),

(B.255)

\displaystyle F_{{\mathrm{Gl,L}}{\mathrm{}}}=

\displaystyle\frac{2^{N_{\mathrm{M}}}}{{}_{2}F_{1}\left(\frac{1}{2},-N_{\mathrm{M}};\frac{3}{2};-1\right)}+\mathcal{O}_{0}\left(x\right)=N_{\mathrm{M}}\left(1-\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{N_{\mathrm{M}}^{2}}\right)\right)+\mathcal{O}_{0}\left(x\right),

(B.256)

which means the runtime scaling is given by (Eq. (
B.251
)):

\displaystyle S_{{\mathrm{Gl}}{\mathrm{}}}\approx C_{{\mathrm{Gl}}{\mathrm{}}}^{2}F_{{\mathrm{Gl,L}}{\mathrm{}}}=\frac{4^{N_{\mathrm{M}}+1}}{\uppi}\left(1+\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{N_{\mathrm{M}}}\right)\right)+\mathcal{O}_{0}\left(x\right).

(B.257)

to leading order. Now, we set our runtime scaling equal to our maximum runtime scaling (
S_{\pluscirc}
) and substitute our mitigation order from Eq. (
B.252
), then rearrange to determine the maximum noise level we can mitigate:

\displaystyle S_{\pluscirc}=

\displaystyle\frac{4^{\frac{\ln\left(2\varepsilon_{\scriptscriptstyle\pluscirc}\right)}{\ln\left(2e_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{G}}\right)}}}{\uppi}

\displaystyle\implies

\displaystyle e_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{G}}=2^{2\frac{\ln\left(2\varepsilon_{\scriptscriptstyle\pluscirc}\right)}{\ln\left(\uppi S_{\pluscirc}\right)}-1}.

(B.258)

So the noise boundary is given by:

\displaystyle e_{{\mathrm{B}}{\mathrm{}}}=2^{2\frac{\ln\left(2\varepsilon_{\scriptscriptstyle\pluscirc}\right)}{\ln\left(\uppi S_{\pluscirc}\right)}-1}=\frac{\left(2\varepsilon_{\scriptscriptstyle\pluscirc}\right)^{\frac{\ln\left(4\right)}{\ln\left(\uppi S_{\pluscirc}\right)}}}{2}.

(B.259)

So we require a runtime scaling greater than
4/\uppi
to achieve any mitigation at all and the larger our tolerated runtime scaling the larger noise boundary we can tolerate. However large our tolerated runtime scaling is, the noise boundary will always be smaller than 0.5.

B.5.6
Robustness

A method is robust if the runtime scaling and the proxy bias are unchanged (to leading order) if there are some imperfections in the noise amplification procedure. For identity insertions (App.
B.5.1
), the source of imperfections would be differences between the noise model of the inverse gate and the original noisy gate, i.e. we have (c.f. Eq. (
B.222
)):

\displaystyle\begin{matrix}\scalebox{0.1}{\begin{picture}\put(0.0,0.0){\includegraphics[width,page]{gateM.pdf}}\put(0.40119,0.42637){\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0)[lt]{\begin{minipage}[335.53992pt]\raggedright\@add@raggedright\end{minipage}}}\put(0.27218,0.60885){\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0)[lt]{\begin{minipage}[391.46324pt]\raggedright\vskip 65.0pt-\heightof{\scalebox{8}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}$\displaystyle{U}_{{\mathrm{}n}}^{\phantom{\dagger}}$}}}/2\hskip 72.0pt\scalebox{8}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}$\displaystyle{U}_{{\mathrm{}n}}^{\phantom{\dagger}}$}}\@add@raggedright\end{minipage}}}\put(0.27218,0.63959){\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0)[lt]{\smash{\begin{tabular}[t]{l}\noindent\hskip 42.67912pt\scalebox{8}{$\displaystyle\sim$}\end{tabular}}}}\put(0.77218,0.10885){\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0)[lt]{\begin{minipage}[391.46324pt]\raggedright\vskip-17.07182pt\par\noindent\hskip 8.5359pt\scalebox{5}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}$\displaystyle{\mathrm{}}$}}\@add@raggedright\end{minipage}}}\put(0.0,0.0){\includegraphics[width,page]{gateM.pdf}}\end{picture}}\end{matrix}\penalty\ =

(B.260)

where:
